### [1] [Total-Lagrangian vectorial lattice Boltzmann method for finite-strain hyperelasticity with curved boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20646)
*Jingsen Feng,Xu Chu*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A total-Lagrangian vectorial lattice Boltzmann method is developed for finite-strain hyperelasticity on curved embedded domains, using D2Q4×6 and D3Q6×12 schemes with cut-link reconstruction and compatibility projection to handle geometric mismatches between lattice directions, surface normals, and deformation directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finite-strain hyperelasticity on curved embedded domains poses a geometric challenge for lattice Boltzmann methods because the lattice direction, surface normal, and tangential deformation directions are generally distinct, requiring a method that resolves this geometric mismatch.

Method: The continuum equations are formulated as a conservative first-order system for material velocity and deformation gradient. Vector-valued populations recover state and Piola fluxes (D2Q4×6, D3Q6×12). Curved boundaries are embedded via level set with cut-link interpolation, opposite-population moment identities, and local geometric information. A compatibility projection keeps recovered displacement aligned with the evolved deformation gradient on embedded active-node graphs. The method retains explicit collide-stream updates on Cartesian grids.

Result: Benchmarks in 2D and 3D show agreement with exact finite-strain fields, nonlinear radial boundary-value problems, and finite-element references.

Conclusion: The method extends the previous grid-aligned 2D formulation to curved domains and 3D lattices, successfully resolving geometric mismatches for hyperelastic dynamics on curved embedded surfaces.

Abstract: Finite-strain hyperelasticity on curved embedded domains poses a geometric challenge for lattice Boltzmann methods. After streaming across an embedded material surface, the missing population is recovered at the physical cut-link point, where the lattice direction, surface normal, and tangential deformation directions are generally distinct. We develop a total-Lagrangian vectorial lattice Boltzmann method that resolves this geometric mismatch for two- and three-dimensional hyperelastic dynamics. The continuum equations are written as a conservative first-order system for material velocity and deformation gradient. Vector-valued populations are chosen so that their moments recover the state and the material-coordinate Piola fluxes, giving D2Q4\(\times\)6 and D3Q6\(\times\)12 schemes from one \(D\)-dimensional construction. Curved boundaries are embedded by a level set and closed link by link through opposite-population moment identities, cut-link interpolation, and local geometric information at the boundary point. The reconstruction is coupled to a compatibility projection that keeps the recovered displacement aligned with the evolved deformation gradient on embedded active-node graphs. The resulting method extends the previous grid-aligned two-dimensional formulation to curved domains and three-dimensional lattices while retaining explicit collide-stream updates on Cartesian grids. Benchmarks in two and three dimensions show agreement with exact finite-strain fields, nonlinear radial boundary-value problems, and finite-element references.

</details>


### [2] [Input-schema identifiability limits in physics-informed surrogates for mechanics-governed flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20655)
*Daniel Cieslak,Andrzej Czyzewski*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces an input-schema identifiability certificate for computational surrogates that pre-audits whether target physical quantities are identifiable from available inputs, using incompressible tubular flow as an instantiation. Experiments on aortic CFD, Womersley flows, and advection-diffusion confirm that supplying signed direction collapses error but magnitude alone leaves a sign ambiguity (16-33% sign flips), validating that certain errors cannot be fixed by architecture or data changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current physics-informed and data-driven surrogates lack a principled way to determine whether target quantities (e.g., velocity fields) are actually identifiable from the input variables available at prediction time, leading to potential failure modes that aggregated error metrics can mask.

Method: The authors develop an identifiability certificate that decomposes a target field into: (1) components measurable from geometry, (2) components requiring boundary-condition information, and (3) components identifiable only up to a symmetry quotient. They instantiate this for incompressible tubular flow via Cosserat-rod reduction, where lumen velocity separates into a mesh-measurable tangent direction, a boundary-condition-dependent magnitude, and a signed-orientation ambiguity.

Result: Controlled experiments on patient-specific aortic CFD geometries, analytic Womersley flows, and an advection-diffusion transfer problem confirm that supplying signed direction collapses angular error to the oracle regime, while supplying magnitude without orientation leaves the predicted sign ambiguity, yielding 16-33% per-node sign flips.

Conclusion: The framework provides a mechanics-based diagnostic for deciding whether a surrogate modeling task is physically identifiable before training, exposing failure modes that aggregate error metrics can hide, and predicting which oracle-channel interventions should reduce error, which should fail, and which ambiguity cannot be removed by changing architecture, loss, optimizer, or sample size.

Abstract: Physics-informed and data-driven surrogates are increasingly used to approximate mechanics-governed flow fields, but the target quantities assigned to such models are not always identifiable from the input variables available at prediction time. We introduce an input-schema identifiability certificate for computational surrogates. Starting from a reduced physical model, the certificate decomposes a target field into components that are measurable from geometry, components that require boundary-condition information, and components identifiable only up to a symmetry quotient. This yields a pre-training audit: it predicts which oracle-channel interventions should reduce error, which should fail, and which ambiguity cannot be removed by changing the architecture, loss, optimizer, or sample size. We instantiate the framework for incompressible tubular flow using a Cosserat-rod reduction, where lumen velocity separates into a mesh-measurable tangent direction, a boundary-condition-dependent magnitude, and a signed-orientation ambiguity. Controlled experiments on patient-specific aortic CFD geometries, analytic Womersley flows, and an advection-diffusion transfer problem confirm the predicted pattern: supplying signed direction collapses angular error to the oracle regime, whereas supplying magnitude without orientation leaves the predicted sign ambiguity and yields 16-33 percent per-node sign flips. The results provide a mechanics-based diagnostic for deciding whether a surrogate modelling task is physically identifiable before training, and expose failure modes that aggregate error metrics can hide.

</details>


### [3] [Physics-Informed Neural Networks for coupled stiff transport systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21236)
*Laetitia Laguzet,Gabriel Turinici*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) fail on stiff, regime-changing transport equations like the Marshak wave equations due to extreme scale disparities. The authors propose three modifications—ScaledSigmoid activation, logarithmic MSE loss, and explicit conservation law enforcement—that together successfully recover Marshak wave dynamics in under 30 minutes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard PINNs fail on stiff, regime-changing transport equations (e.g., Marshak wave equations) due to instability, loss imbalance, and violations of physical consistency, where initial and boundary conditions differ by up to 12 orders of magnitude.

Method: Three modifications to standard PINNs: (1) ScaledSigmoid final activation for bounded, positivity-preserving outputs; (2) logarithmic MSE loss for initial/boundary conditions to handle extreme scale disparities; (3) explicit global conservation law enforcement as an additional physics loss term. Monte Carlo sampling with exponential time weighting is employed.

Result: The method recovers Marshak wave dynamics (hot, cold, and wave-front regions) matching an Implicit Monte Carlo reference solution, with run times under 30 minutes. Ablation studies show each modification is individually essential for success.

Conclusion: The combination of bounded activations, scale-aware loss functions, and conservation law enforcement constitutes a validated framework for solving stiff hyperbolic systems with nonlinear coupling, applicable to radiative transport and related engineering PDE systems.

Abstract: Purpose: Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) struggle with stiff, regime-changing transport equations due to instability, loss imbalance, and violations of physical consistency. This paper investigates these failures through the Marshak wave equations - a canonical benchmark from radiative transport - where initial and boundary conditions differ by up to 12 orders of magnitude, and proposes targeted modifications to the standard PINN framework to overcome them.
  Design/methodology/approach: Three modifications are introduced: (1) a ScaledSigmoid final activation enforcing physical bounds and positivity of the unknowns; (2) a logarithmic MSE loss replacing the standard quadratic loss for initial and boundary conditions, enabling training across extreme scale disparities; and (3) explicit enforcement of global conservation laws derived from the governing equations as an additional physics loss term. Monte Carlo sampling with exponential time weighting is used throughout.
  Findings: The proposed framework successfully recovers the Marshak wave dynamics - including the hot, cold, and wave-front regions - in agreement with a reference Implicit Monte Carlo solution, with run times under 30 minutes. Ablation studies confirm that each ingredient is essential: linear activation, absence of the logarithmic loss, or removal of the PDE term each independently cause the method to fail qualitatively.
  Originality/value: This work identifies and resolves three concrete failure modes of standard PINNs on stiff hyperbolic systems with nonlinear coupling. The combination of bounded activations, scale-aware loss functions, and conservation law enforcement constitutes a novel and practically validated framework, with applicability to radiative transport and other coupled stiff PDE systems in engineering.

</details>


### [4] [AI-accelerated metallized $σ$-bonding screening for superconductor discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21251)
*Zechen Tang,Wen-Han Dong,Baochun Wu,Jian-Feng Zhang,Yuxiang Wang,Yang Li,Honggeng Tao,Qiyu Zeng,Chong Wang,Chen Si,Zhong-Yi Lu,Wenhui Duan,Tao Xiang,Yong Xu*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces σ-bonding density of states (σDOS) as a cheap DFT-level descriptor to identify high-Tc superconductors without expensive DFPT calculations, accelerates it with deep learning, screens 2 million materials, and discovers B13Se as a candidate with Tc > 40 K alongside a family of B13X superconductors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DFPT is computationally prohibitive for large-scale screening of phonon-mediated superconductors, motivating the need for a cheaper yet effective physical descriptor.

Method: Guided by the metallized σ-bonding picture, the authors define σDOS as a DFT-level descriptor. They further accelerate σDOS evaluation using a deep-learning DFT Hamiltonian method, enabling screening of 2 million materials.

Result: Identified B13Se as an ambient-pressure superconductor candidate with predicted Tc > 40 K, and a family of high-Tc B13X candidates, validating the strategy's effectiveness.

Conclusion: Bridging physics priors with AI acceleration provides an efficient and generalizable route for computational materials discovery in the AI era.

Abstract: The computational discovery of phonon-mediated superconductors is hindered by the prohibitive cost of density functional perturbation theory (DFPT). Here, guided by the metallized $σ$-bonding picture, we introduce the $σ$-bonding density of states ($σ$DOS) as an efficient physical descriptor to identify high-transition-temperature ($T_{\mathrm{c}}$) superconductors from density functional theory (DFT)-level electronic structure without explicit DFPT calculations. The evaluation of $σ$DOS can be further accelerated by a deep-learning DFT Hamiltonian method, enabling efficient large-scale screening for superconductors. Screening 2 million materials, we identify B$_{13}$Se as an ambient-pressure superconductor candidate with predicted $T_{\mathrm{c}} > 40$~K, together with a family of high-$T_{\mathrm{c}}$ B$_{13}X$ candidates, supporting the effectiveness of this discovery strategy. By bridging physics priors with AI acceleration, this study delivers an efficient and generalizable route for computational materials discovery in the AI era.

</details>


### [5] [MADField: Multi-fidelity Amortized Density Field for Adsorption in Nanoporous Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21284)
*Yoonho Kim,Seongsu Kim,Sungsoo Ahn,Honghui Kim*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: MADField is a multi-fidelity deep learning framework that reframes adsorption prediction in nanoporous materials as equilibrium density-field estimation, combining cDFT and GCMC supervision to achieve significantly faster and more accurate gas uptake predictions and screening performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-throughput computational screening of nanoporous materials for gas storage and separation requires fast and accurate characterization of adsorption equilibrium. Existing methods (GCMC and cDFT) suffer from insufficient speed-accuracy tradeoff for large-scale screening.

Method: MADField reframes adsorption prediction as equilibrium density-field estimation using a multi-fidelity learning framework. It learns from two complementary fidelities: broad and scalable cDFT density supervision combined with higher-fidelity GCMC density labels. Gas uptake is recovered by integrating the predicted density field.

Result: MADField improves uptake accuracy over baselines by 6.0x for cDFT and 15.4x for GCMC. Its predicted fields accelerate cDFT solvers with 2.0x fewer iteration steps while recovering 42% of cases failing under standard settings. On the 270k-structure ARC-MOF database for CH4 working capacity screening, it achieves 56x higher average precision than the strongest baseline, accelerates inference by five orders of magnitude vs. GCMC, and recovers 95% of targets with top 1.7% candidates and 100% recall with top 6%.

Conclusion: MADField effectively bridges the speed-accuracy gap in nanoporous materials screening by providing rapid, accurate adsorption predictions, enabling practical large-scale screening of rare high-capacity targets with dramatically improved efficiency.

Abstract: High-throughput computational screening of nanoporous materials for gas storage and separation requires fast and accurate characterization of adsorption equilibrium. Particle-based grand canonical Monte Carlo (GCMC) and density-based classical density functional theory (cDFT) provide simulation-based estimates of gas uptake and adsorbate density fields, but their speed-accuracy tradeoff remains insufficient for large-scale screening. In this work, we address this gap with Multi-fidelity Amortized Density Field for Adsorption in Nanoporous Materials (MADField), which reframes adsorption prediction as equilibrium density-field estimation. MADField learns from two complementary fidelities, combining broad and scalable cDFT density supervision with higher-fidelity GCMC density labels, and recovers gas uptake by integrating the predicted density field. MADField improves uptake accuracy over the strongest baselines by 6.0x for cDFT and 15.4x for GCMC, and its predicted fields accelerate cDFT solvers with 2.0x fewer iteration steps while recovering 42 percent of cases that fail under standard settings. Finally, we evaluate MADField for conventional CH4 working capacity screening on the 270k-structure ARC-MOF database. Within this space of extremely rare high-capacity targets, 167 in total, the model achieves 56x higher average precision than the strongest baseline and accelerates inference by five orders of magnitude compared to GCMC. By prioritizing the MADField rankings, selecting the top 1.7 percent of candidates recovers 95 percent of all targets, while the top 6 percent ensures 100 percent recall.

</details>


### [6] [Observations Regarding the Construction of Multipoint Kinetics Models from Observational Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21741)
*K. G. Howey,Giorgio Valocchi,Dean Price*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates the recovery of the coupling coefficient matrix K in multipoint kinetics (MPK) equations from transient flux measurements. Using analytical spectral solutions of the Kobayashi MPK formulation, the authors show that recovering K is challenging due to dominant eigenmode behavior, and that delayed neutron precursors delay but don't fully resolve the problem. Successful recovery requires capturing nondominant eigenmodes without oversampling the dominant mode.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Determining the coupling coefficient matrix K from transient data is an open problem without established experimental procedures, and this work provides analytical groundwork to understand recoverability conditions.

Method: Analytical spectral solutions to the Kobayashi MPK formulation (no precursors and single precursor family cases); modal sensitivity analysis; Hessian condition number analysis of the spectral recovery problem.

Result: Inclusion of delayed neutron precursors delays eigenmode divergence but may be insufficient for practical recovery of K in many scenarios; nondominant eigenmodes must be captured without oversampling the dominant eigenmode for successful recoverability.

Conclusion: Capturing nondominant eigenmode behavior without oversampling the dominant eigenmode is critical to the recoverability of K through transient data.

Abstract: The multipoint kinetics (MPK) equations extend point kinetics models by tracking neutron populations in lumped reactor regions, offering improved spatial resolution at modest computational cost. However, determining the coupling coefficient matrix $K$ from transient data remains an open problem without established experimental procedures. This work provides some commentary on the task of recovering $K$ from observable forward flux measurements during reactor transients.
  Analytical solutions to the Kobayashi MPK formulation in the case of no precursors and with a single precursor family are derived in spectral form, enabling a mathematically rigorous investigation of eigenmode behavior and parameter observability. Coupling coefficient recoverability is investigated using modal sensitivity analysis and the Hessian condition number of the spectral recovery problem. The inclusion of delayed neutron precursors delays this eigenmode divergence but may be insufficient for enabling practical recovery of $K$ in many scenarios. In conclusion, capturing the behavior of nondominant eigenmodes without oversampling the dominant eigenmode is critical to the recoverability of $K$ through transient data.

</details>


### [7] [False Summit and Silent Drift: A Failure Taxonomy and Efficiency Analysis of LLM-Assisted Multiphysics Simulation in an Open-Source Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21841)
*Chi-Chun Chiang,Shih-Ming He*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether LLMs (GPT and Claude) can lower the entry barrier for multiphysics simulation development, using an LPCVD model for graphene growth in MOOSE as a case study. Staged development with LLMs produced converged simulations but revealed two failure classes: false summit (converged but physically incorrect simulations) and silent drift (undetected propagation of unverified assumptions). Literature guidance reduced prompt counts by 91% (GPT) and 64% (Claude). The authors propose a failure taxonomy, oversight strategies, and a stage-gated workflow to improve detection of hidden modeling errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The high entry barrier for newcomers to multiphysics semiconductor process simulation due to the need for simultaneous expertise in governing equations, numerical methods, software implementation, and process physics. The authors aim to determine if LLM assistance and curated literature can reduce this barrier.

Method: Comparison of GPT and Claude across three conditions: direct prompting, staged development without literature, and staged development with literature guidance, applied to building an LPCVD graphene growth model in MOOSE. Failure modes were analyzed through a case study approach.

Result: Staged development successfully produced converged simulations. Two failure classes identified: false summit (converged but physically incorrect physics) and silent drift (undetected propagation of unverified assumptions). A non-conservative species-transport formulation produced visually indistinguishable spurious methane depletion. Literature guidance reduced prompt counts by 91% (GPT) and 64% (Claude) during the most challenging development stage.

Conclusion: LLMs can lower the barrier to multiphysics simulation development, but rigorous physical validation remains essential because apparently reasonable solutions may conceal critical defects. An eight-category failure taxonomy, model-specific human oversight strategies, and a stage-gated workflow are proposed to improve detectability of hidden modeling errors.

Abstract: Multiphysics simulation of semiconductor processes requires simultaneous command of governing equations, numerical methods, software implementation, and process physics, creating a substantial entry barrier for newcomers. We investigate whether large language model (LLM) assistance and curated literature guidance can reduce this barrier during the development of a low-pressure chemical vapor deposition (LPCVD) model for graphene growth using the open-source Multiphysics Object-Oriented Simulation Environment (MOOSE). We compare GPT and Claude across direct prompting, staged development without literature, and staged development with literature guidance. Staged development successfully produced converged simulations but revealed two recurring failure classes. We define a false summit as a simulation that converges and appears physically plausible while implementing incorrect physics, and silent drift as the undetected propagation of unverified assumptions through successive development stages. In our case study, a non-conservative species-transport formulation generated spurious methane depletion that was visually indistinguishable from genuine surface consumption, while an omitted self-limiting surface-reaction term produced physically incorrect growth behavior that the LLM repeatedly rationalized as valid. Literature guidance substantially reduced interaction burden during the most challenging development stage, decreasing prompt counts by 91% for GPT and 64% for Claude. Based on these observations, we propose an eight-category failure taxonomy, model-specific human oversight strategies, and a stage-gated workflow to improve the detectability of hidden modeling errors. The results demonstrate that LLMs can lower the barrier to multiphysics simulation development, but rigorous physical validation remains essential because apparently reasonable solutions may conceal critical defects

</details>


### [8] [Inverse Identification of Surface Elastic Parameters in Soft Solids Using GA-ANN Surrogate Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22120)
*Md. Ayaz,Abhishek Ghosh,Andrew McBride,M. Rashid Zafar,Md. Irfan Ansari*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a GA-based optimization framework with an ANN surrogate (trained on nonlinear FE simulations) to identify surface elastic parameters (surface tension 0-5, shear modulus 0-50) from force-displacement data of soft cylindrical specimens, achieving <8% error and robust performance even with 5% measurement noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurately identifying surface elastic parameters is challenging due to complex interactions with bulk elasticity and nonlinearity, despite surface elasticity being important at micro/milli scales for soft solids.

Method: A genetic algorithm (GA) optimization framework combined with an artificial neural network (ANN) surrogate forward model is used. The ANN is trained on force-displacement data from nonlinear finite element (FE) simulations of soft cylindrical specimens with model elastic surfaces, covering non-dimensional surface tension (0-5) and surface shear modulus (0-50). The ANN replaces idealized analytical forward models with a data-driven surrogate trained under realistic boundary conditions.

Result: Maximum identification error <8% across all parameter sets; robust performance with 5% added noise; narrow confidence intervals from repeatability and uncertainty analyses over multiple noisy realizations.

Conclusion: The proposed GA-ANN framework offers a novel, efficient, and robust alternative to FE-based inverse identification of surface parameters, especially for experimentally relevant boundary conditions.

Abstract: Surface elasticity plays a crucial role in the mechanics of soft solids at the scale of micrometers and may become significant even at the scale of millimeters in exceptional cases. However, despite the efforts made for the identification of surface elastic parameters, accurately determining these values remains challenging due to the complex interplay with bulk elasticity and nonlinearity. To address this issue, a novel GA-based optimization framework is developed by employing an ANN-based surrogate forward model for efficient identification of surface parameters from the force-displacement response of a cylindrical specimen. The ANN is trained on force-displacement data of a soft cylindrical specimen, generated by nonlinear FE model predictions incorporating model elastic surfaces. The data used for training is generated for non-dimensional values of surface tension in the range of 0-5 and surface shear modulus in the range of 0-50. The accuracy and reliability of the trained ANN are established. The key novelty lies in replacing analytical forward models, which rely on idealized boundary conditions, with a data-driven surrogate trained on FE simulations under realistic constraints. The parameter identification is performed for a number of surface parameter sets using numerically generated force-displacement data as well as for noisy data obtained by adding 5% error in simulated data. The maximum error in identified values of parameters in all the cases is less than 8%. Repeatability and uncertainty analyses based on multiple noisy realizations further demonstrate the robustness of the approach, yielding narrow confidence intervals for the predicted parameters. The proposed framework provides a novel, efficient, and robust alternative to FE-based inverse identification of surface parameters, particularly for experimentally relevant boundary conditions.

</details>


### [9] [Properties of the matrix functions arising in exponential integrators with applications to stochastic simulations of PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23227)
*Elliot J. Carr*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper investigates nonnegativity and stochasticity properties of matrix phi-functions (generalizations of the matrix exponential), applies these to deterministic solutions of linear/semi-linear ODEs, and shows how these solutions enable stochastic simulations for quantifying prediction variability, with examples from PDE discretizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend known nonnegativity and stochasticity properties of the matrix exponential to matrix phi-functions, which are essential in exponential integrators for stiff ODEs and semi-linear problems, and to bridge deterministic ODE solutions with stochastic simulation methods.

Method: Theoretical analysis of matrix phi-function properties (nonnegativity, stochasticity) for essentially nonnegative and transition-rate matrices; application to linear and semi-linear ODE solution characterization; linkage to stochastic simulation frameworks; illustrative numerical examples from spatially discretized PDEs.

Result: Matrix phi-functions retain nonnegativity and stochasticity properties analogous to the matrix exponential under appropriate conditions; these properties provide insights into solution behavior and enable connection to stochastic simulation methods for variability quantification.

Conclusion: The established properties of matrix phi-functions extend the theoretical foundation for exponential integrators and open pathways for hybrid deterministic-stochastic modeling of ODE/PDE systems.

Abstract: The matrix exponential and the closely related matrix phi-functions play a fundamental role in the solution of first-order systems of ordinary differential equations (ODEs). In particular, they appear in exact solutions of certain linear ODE systems, and in exponential integrators, a class of explicit time discretisation methods for computing approximate solutions of stiff semi-linear ODE systems. Fundamental properties of the matrix exponential include that it is nonnegative if the matrix is essentially nonnegative and stochastic if the matrix is a transition-rate matrix. In this paper, we study related properties for the matrix phi-functions. Using these properties, we then provide insights into deterministic solutions of linear and semi-linear ODE systems and outline how such solutions can be used to generate stochastic simulations to quantify variability in model predictions. The paper concludes with some illustrative examples exploring the theory for some ODE systems arising from spatial discretisation of PDEs of diffusion, advection-diffusion, Fisher-KPP and Allen-Cahn type.

</details>


### [10] [Parallelized contraction of tensor trains or matrix product operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23274)
*Simone Foderà,Marc K. Ritter,Hiroshi Shinaoka,Jan von Delft*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This work presents two strategies to accelerate the fit algorithm for MPO-MPO contractions: MPI-based distributed-memory parallelization (using inverse canonical or site-canonical gauge) and randomized projections to reduce local update costs from 2-site to 1-site while maintaining accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The fit algorithm for MPO-MPO contractions is computationally expensive due to 2-site updates, and its parallelization has received little attention.

Method: Two strategies: (1) MPI-based distributed-memory parallelization with two gauge choices—inverse canonical gauge (near-ideal speedup) and site-canonical gauge (avoids SVD inversion, good speedup for large problems); (2) Randomized projections to reduce local updates from 2-site to 1-site cost while preserving 2-site accuracy, and to accelerate environment tensor contractions.

Result: Inverse canonical gauge yields near-ideal parallelization speedup across all problem sizes; site-canonical gauge yields excellent speedup only for large problems requiring multiple sweeps. Randomized projections reduce local update costs from 2-site to 1-site while retaining accuracy.

Conclusion: The two acceleration strategies (parallelization with appropriate gauge choices and randomized projections) can be used in combination to significantly accelerate MPO-MPO contractions via the fit algorithm.

Abstract: Tensor Trains (TT), also known as Matrix Product States (MPS) and Matrix Product Operators (MPO), provide a compact and structured representation for high-dimensional data and operators. One of the most expensive manipulations involving tensor trains is the contraction of two MPOs. A popular and accurate method for mitigating this cost is the fit algorithm. However, it is still comparatively costly since it involves 2-site updates. Moreover, the parallelization of the fit algorithm when used for MPO-MPO contractions has received comparatively little attention. In this work, we present two strategies for accelerating the fit algorithm, usable in combination: (1) We use MPI-based distributed-memory parallelization tailored for MPO-MPO contractions, employing one of two MPO gauge choices: (1a) the inverse canonical gauge, which yields near-ideal parallelization speedup across all problem sizes; and (1b) the site-canonical gauge, which avoids the inversion of singular values but requires extra computations to ensure global consistency, thus yielding excellent parallelization speedup only for large problems requiring several sweeps before convergence. (2) We use randomized projections to reduce the cost of local updates from 2-site to 1-site costs while retaining 2-site accuracy, and to speed up contractions of environment tensors with MPO tensors.

</details>

### [11] [Scanning-probe quantum sensing of microwave and static magnetic field response of an on-chip superconducting resonator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20904)
*Senlei Li,Jingcheng Zhou,Zelong Xiong,Hanyi Lu,Hailong Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper introduces scanning-probe quantum microscopy using a quantum spin sensor to achieve nanoscale sensing of microwave and static magnetic fields from an on-chip niobium superconducting resonator, enabling coherent control of the sensor and visualization of vortex behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To gain detailed microscopic knowledge of electromagnetic properties of superconducting resonators, which is critical for improving circuitry design and device performance in quantum circuits.

Method: Scanning-probe quantum microscopy with a quantum spin sensor, using Rabi oscillation measurements to probe microwave magnetic fields and visualize static field responses of a niobium superconducting resonator.

Result: Demonstrated coherent control of a quantum spin sensor via microwave magnetic fields from the resonator mode, and visualized static electromagnetic field response including formation, evolution, and depinning of superconducting vortices in the Nb resonator.

Conclusion: Provides insights for future design, testing, and evaluation of solid-state superconducting resonators, highlighting quantum sensors as local probes for investigating electromagnetic properties of superconducting quantum circuits.

Abstract: Superconducting resonators are finding increasing applications in designing advanced quantum circuits for ongoing sensing, metrology, and computing technological revolution. A detailed knowledge of microscopic electromagnetic properties of superconducting resonators is directly relevant for their further improvements on circuitry design and device performance. Here, we introduce scanning-probe quantum microscopy to report nanoscale sensing of microwave and static magnetic field environment of an on-chip niobium (Nb) superconducting resonator. Taking advantage of Rabi oscillation measurements, we show that microwave magnetic fields generated by the superconducting resonator mode can be utilized to achieve coherent control of a quantum spin sensor. We further visualize static electromagnetic field response of the Nb resonator, showing magnetic field-induced formation, evolution, and depinning of superconducting vortices. Our results provide insights into future design, testing and evaluation of solid-state superconducting resonators, highlighting the potential of quantum sensors as a local probe to investigate electromagnetic properties of superconducting quantum circuits.

</details>


### [12] [Observation of Berry curvature fluctuations from incipient polar order in an oxide interface](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20949)
*S. Avraham,D. Gitman,P. Matus,S. Sandik,E. Raz,S. Jana,M. Dahan,T. Holder,Y. Dagan*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that second-harmonic resistivities in KTaO₃ exhibit large, reproducible mesoscopic fluctuations that persist in macroscopically large devices, arising from local structural symmetry breaking due to incipient ferroelectric polarization pinned to interfacial impurities. These findings establish nonlinear mesoscopic transport as a tool to reveal hidden local orders invisible to conventional probes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diagnosing hidden local orders at buried interfaces remains a central challenge in designing and characterizing quantum materials, as these inversion-symmetry-breaking terms are invisible to linear transport.

Method: The authors utilize KTaO₃ (a complex oxide with strong Ta 5d spin-orbit coupling and interfacial inversion symmetry breaking) to measure second-harmonic resistivities, probing nonlinear Hall effects and mesoscopic fluctuations across different surface orientations.

Result: Large, reproducible mesoscopic fluctuations of second-harmonic resistivities are observed in macroscopically large (200 μm) devices, ubiquitous across all surface orientations. The fluctuations are suppressed above 10 K due to phase decoherence and show complete loss of mesoscopic memory upon thermal cycling above 40 K.

Conclusion: The robust magnetic-field-driven interference patterns arise from local structural symmetry breaking driven by incipient ferroelectric polarization pinned to the interfacial impurity landscape. Nonlinear mesoscopic transport is established as a powerful characterization tool for revealing local polar tendencies and hidden structural orders in complex materials.

Abstract: Diagnosing hidden local orders at buried interfaces remains a central challenge in the design and characterization of quantum materials. Second-order electrical responses, such as the nonlinear Hall effect, probe inversion-symmetry-breaking terms invisible to linear transport, offering a direct window into these nanoscale environments via the quantum geometry of Bloch electrons. Here, we utilize $\text{KTaO}_3$, a complex oxide driven by strong tantalum $5d$ spin-orbit coupling and interfacial inversion symmetry breaking, to demonstrate that second-harmonic resistivities exhibit large, reproducible mesoscopic fluctuations. Remarkably, these fluctuations persist in macroscopically large ($200\,μ\text{m}$) devices and are ubiquitous across all studied surface orientations, even where macroscopic conductivity strictly adheres to underlying crystal symmetries. We propose that these robust, magnetic-field-driven interference patterns arise from local structural symmetry breaking, driven by incipient ferroelectric polarization pinned to the interfacial impurity landscape. This defect-pinned polar mechanism is firmly supported by the signal's suppression above $10\text{ K}$ due to phase decoherence, and a complete loss of mesoscopic memory upon thermal cycling above $40\text{ K}$. By linking quantum geometry to dynamic lattice ordering, our findings establish nonlinear mesoscopic transport as a powerful new characterization tool, capable of revealing local polar tendencies and hidden structural orders in complex materials that remain fundamentally invisible to conventional probes.

</details>


### [13] [Observation and Control of Spontaneous Magnon Emission from Spin Ensembles in 2D Hexagonal Boron Nitride](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20996)
*Ling-Jie Zhou,Jayakrishnan M. P. Nair,Senlei Li,Thomas Poirier,Yiran Zhao,Zelong Xiong,Sumedh Rathi,Jingcheng Zhou,Zhigang Jiang,Hailong Wang,James H. Edgar,Benedetta Flebus,Chunhui Rita Du*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Boron-vacancy centers in 2D hexagonal boron nitride exhibit spontaneous magnon emission—a novel qubit-magnon dipole coupling channel dominant near zero temperature—which transitions to thermal magnon effects at higher temperatures and can establish quantum correlations in dense spin ensembles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore novel coupling mechanisms between optically active spin defects (boron-vacancy centers in hBN) and quantum degrees of freedom (magnons) in hybrid solid-state quantum platforms.

Method: Observation and control of spontaneous magnon emission from boron-vacancy centers in 2D hBN via experimental spectroscopy and theoretical modeling, with variation of temperature and spin defect density.

Result: Spontaneous magnon emission dominates near zero temperature but is overshadowed by thermal magnon effects as temperature rises; increasing spin defect density enables spontaneous magnon emission into a common spin bath, helping establish quantum correlations.

Conclusion: The findings are quantitatively captured by theory, providing insights into qubit-magnon coupling, correlated spin dynamics, and many-body physics of color centers in the quantum regime.

Abstract: Hybrid systems consisting of color centers and magnetic materials provide an appealing solid-state platform for advancing the burgeoning quantum technological revolution. Exploring novel coupling mechanisms between optically active spin defects and quantum degrees of freedom is directly relevant in this context. Here, we report observation and control of spontaneous magnon emission from boron-vacancy centers in 2D hexagonal boron nitride (hBN), an unconventional qubit-magnon dipole coupling channel that dominates in the near-zero temperature limit. The spontaneous magnon emission process starts to be overshadowed by thermal magnon effect as temperature increases, reflecting the crossover from an emission-dominated, effectively cold magnon reservoir to a thermally occupied spin bath where absorption and stimulated processes restore balance. By increasing the spin defect density, we further present that spontaneous magnon emission into a common spin bath could help establish quantum correlations in dense hBN spin ensembles. Our results are quantitatively captured by detailed theoretical modeling, bringing insights into understanding qubit-magnon coupling, correlated spin dynamics, and many-body physics of color centers in the quantum regime.

</details>


### [14] [Electrically Programmable Correlated Topology and Magnetism in a Moiré Trilayer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21004)
*Christiano Wang Beach,Courtney Baier,Kaijie Yang,Huiyuan Zheng,Yueyao Fan,Weijie Li,Shuai Yuan,Yifan Zhao,Yue Sun,Chaowei Hu,Takashi Taniguchi,Kenji Watanabe,Jiun-haw Chu,Liang Fu,Ting Cao,Satoshi Okamoto,Di Xiao,Xiaodong Xu*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Researchers demonstrate an electrically programmable quantum many-body system in alternating twisted trilayer MoTe₂, where a displacement field tunes the system between a triangular lattice hosting correlated insulators and magnetism, and a honeycomb lattice with flat Chern bands supporting integer and fractional Chern insulators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve in situ electrical control over lattice geometry, bandwidth, and band topology within a single platform for studying strong electron-electron interactions.

Method: Using an alternating twisted trilayer MoTe₂ system with an out-of-plane displacement field to continuously modify layer polarization, effective lattice, and topology of moiré bands.

Result: At zero displacement field: triangular lattice with correlated insulator at ν=-1, asymmetric magnetic responses (double-exchange-like ferromagnetism for |ν|>1, spin polarons/antiferromagnetism for |ν|<1). At large displacement field: honeycomb lattice with flat Chern band supporting integer and fractional Chern insulators. Magneto-optical measurements show gap closure and Landau-level formation near the crossover.

Conclusion: This establishes a unified, electrically tunable platform where correlated magnetism and topological states emerge from a single controllable band structure.

Abstract: Strong electron-electron interactions underlie a wide range of quantum many-body phenomena, including magnetism, superconductivity, and charge fractionalization. A central goal is to achieve in situ control over lattice geometry, bandwidth, and band topology within a single platform. Here we realize such an electrically programmable quantum many-body system in an alternating twisted trilayer MoTe$_2$, where an out-of-plane displacement field continuously modifies the layer polarization, effective lattice, and topology of the moiré bands. At zero displacement field, the system realizes a triangular lattice hosting a correlated insulator at one hole per moiré unit cell ($ν= -1$). Doping this state produces strongly asymmetric magnetic responses: double-exchange-like ferromagnetism for $|ν| > 1$, and signatures of spin polarons and antiferromagnetism for $|ν| < 1$. At large displacement field, interlayer hybridization reconstructs the electronic structure into a honeycomb lattice with a flat Chern band, supporting integer and fractional Chern insulators. Magneto-optical measurements further reveal the signatures of gap closure and Landau-level formation from a spin-polarized Fermi surface near the crossover between the two regimes. These results establish a unified, electrically tunable platform in which correlated magnetism and topological states emerge from a single controllable band structure.

</details>


### [15] [Mechanically Exfoliated Metallic Delafossite PdCoO2 Nanomembranes: Quantum Transport and Electrical Evaluation Toward Interconnect Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21057)
*Chengyu Zhu,Pahuni Jain,Yi Zhang,Junghyun Koo,Weideng Sun,Yaotian Li,Alexander McLeod,Chris Leighton,Gang Qiu*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Single crystalline PdCoO2 nanomembranes were fabricated, enabling mesoscopic transport studies. Quantum oscillations revealed electron effective mass and phase coherence length. The material showed nearly thickness-independent resistivity down to 40 nm, ultrahigh breakdown current density (113 MA cm⁻²), and excellent thermal stability, making it promising for interconnects and quantum transport research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Metallic delafossite oxides exhibit ultralow resistivity and coherent electronic transport, but mesoscopic transport studies have been hindered by the difficulty of fabricating high-quality nanoscale devices.

Method: Single crystalline PdCoO2 nanomembranes were fabricated via mechanical exfoliation. Shubnikov-de Haas and Aharonov-Bohm oscillations under varying magnetic field orientations were measured at low temperatures to extract electron effective mass and phase coherence length. Electrical performance was evaluated for interconnect applications.

Result: Nearly thickness-independent room-temperature resistivity was observed for flakes down to 40 nm. Breakdown current density reached up to 113 MA cm⁻² with excellent thermal stability and electromigration resistance.

Conclusion: Mechanically exfoliated PdCoO2 flakes maintain the high crystalline and electronic quality of bulk crystals in the quasi-2D limit, providing a platform for mesoscopic transport studies and interconnect applications.

Abstract: Metallic delafossite oxides have drawn attention for their ultralow resistivity and coherent electronic transport. However, mesoscopic transport studies are hindered by the limited access to high quality nanoscale devices. Here, we report single crystalline PdCoO2 nanomembranes, enabling exploration of quasi two dimensional (2D) transport. Shubnikov de Haas and Aharonov Bohm oscillations under different magnetic field orientations are observed at low temperatures, from which the electron effective mass and electron phase coherence length are extracted. Beyond quantum transport, the electrical performance of PdCoO2 toward interconnect applications is evaluated. A nearly thickness independent room temperature resistivity is observed for flakes down to 40 nm thickness. The nanomembranes exhibit a breakdown current density up to 113 MA cm-2, with excellent thermal stability and electromigration resistance. These results demonstrate that mechanically exfoliated PdCoO2 flakes preserve the high crystalline and electronic quality of bulk crystals in the quasi-2D limit, providing a useful platform for mesoscopic transport studies and interconnect applications.

</details>


### [16] [Propagating edge and interfacial states in corrugated graphene: Robustness and configurability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21065)
*Adel Belayadi,Dawei Zhai,Nancy Sandler*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Periodically strained graphene on patterned substrates creates moiré-like electronic structures. Strain-induced pseudomagnetic fields combined with displacement-field-controlled scalar potentials produce isolated narrow bands, multiple energy gaps, and nontrivial topology with valley-opposite Chern numbers. Despite zero total Chern number, robust in-gap edge states emerge in nanoribbons preserving valley symmetry. These states can be electrically switched on/off and spatially reconfigured, establishing strain superlattices as a platform for topological electronics in graphene.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore periodically strained graphene on patterned substrates as a versatile route for realizing moiré-like electronic structures via strain engineering, and to understand the interplay between strain-induced pseudomagnetic fields and displacement-field-controlled scalar potentials for generating topological electronic states.

Method: The authors theoretically study graphene strain superlattices by modeling the combined effects of strain-induced pseudomagnetic fields and externally controlled displacement fields (scalar potentials). They analyze band structures, Chern numbers, and edge state formation in nanoribbon geometries using theoretical and computational methods.

Result: Isolated narrow bands and multiple energy gaps form near charge neutrality and higher energies. Low-energy bands carry valley-opposite Chern numbers (nontrivial topology). Despite vanishing total Chern number, propagating in-gap edge states appear in nanoribbons preserving valley symmetry. Edge states are robust against variations in superlattice termination and moderate disorder. An externally applied staggered potential can electrically switch the zero-energy gap and edge channels on/off. Split-gate geometries yield topologically protected, spatially reconfigurable interfacial states.

Conclusion: Strain superlattices in graphene serve as a powerful platform for engineering topological electronic states and electronic transport, offering electrically switchable and reconfigurable edge/interfacial channels without relying on conventional topological protection.

Abstract: Periodically strained graphene on patterned substrates provides a versatile route to realizing moiré-like electronic structures through strain engineering. Here, we show that the interplay between a strain-induced pseudomagnetic field and a displacement-field-controlled scalar potential enables the formation of isolated narrow bands and multiple energy gaps near charge neutrality and at higher energies. Some of the low-energy bands exhibit nontrivial topology, carrying valley-opposite Chern numbers. Remarkably, despite a vanishing total Chern number, propagating in-gap edge states emerge in a wide range of nanoribbon geometries that preserve valley symmetry. We elucidate the distinct mechanisms responsible for edge states in the zero-energy and higher-energy gaps and demonstrate that they remain robust against variations in superlattice termination and moderate disorder, despite lacking conventional topological protection. Leveraging these properties, we propose device architectures in which an externally applied staggered potential electrically switches the zero-energy gap and its associated edge channels on and off. Furthermore, split-gate geometries generate topologically protected interfacial states that coexist with the edge modes and can be spatially reconfigured by gate voltages. These results establish strain superlattices as a powerful platform for engineering topological electronic states and electronic transport in graphene.

</details>


### [17] [Moiré-Enhanced Plasmonics in Non-Hermitian Twisted Bilayer Graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21186)
*Andrianos Sygrimis,Giorgos P. Tsironis*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper studies non-Hermitian plasmonics in twisted bilayer graphene, showing that gain-loss engineering within a parity-time-symmetric framework modifies plasmonic modes, yielding subwavelength confinement and enhanced propagation, though practical realization faces experimental challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how non-Hermitian effects (gain and loss) modify plasmonic excitations in twisted bilayer graphene, a moiré quantum material, and to explore the potential for tunable non-Hermitian plasmonics.

Method: Non-Hermitian extension of the Bistritzer-MacDonald continuum model combined with a biorthogonal Kubo formalism for optical conductivity.

Result: Non-Hermiticity yields optical and acoustic plasmon branches, with the acoustic branch showing strong subwavelength confinement; PT-symmetric gain-loss engineering reduces spatial damping and enhances propagation length; strongly localized transverse-magnetic near fields are produced.

Conclusion: The plasmon enhancement arises from moiré-band reconstruction, biorthogonal optical matrix elements, and non-Hermitian pole modification—not merely from adding gain. Twisted bilayer graphene is a promising but experimentally demanding platform for non-Hermitian moiré plasmonics, with limitations from disorder, substrate loss, gain saturation, and PT-symmetry stability.

Abstract: We study plasmonic excitations in twisted bilayer graphene within a non-Hermitian framework that incorporates effective gain and loss. Using a non-Hermitian extension of the Bistritzer--MacDonald continuum model together with a biorthogonal Kubo formalism for the optical conductivity, we determine how the moiré electronic structure enters the plasmonic response of the active bilayer. We find that non-Hermiticity modifies the collective spectrum, yielding optical and acoustic plasmon branches, with the acoustic branch exhibiting strong subwavelength confinement. In the parity-time-symmetric configuration, gain--loss engineering can reduce the effective spatial damping and enhance the propagation length within the ideal linear model. The same regime produces strongly localized transverse-magnetic near fields. We argue that the enhancement is not a generic consequence of adding gain to a bilayer, but results from the combined influence of moiré-band reconstruction, biorthogonal optical matrix elements, and non-Hermitian modification of the plasmon pole. We also discuss the limitations imposed by disorder, substrate loss, gain saturation, and stability of the parity-time-symmetric regime. These results identify twisted bilayer graphene as a promising, but experimentally demanding, platform for tunable non-Hermitian plasmonics in moiré quantum materials.

</details>


### [18] [Bose-Einstein Condensation of Three-Dimensional Exciton-Polaritons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21219)
*Junhui Cao,Alexey Kavokin*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper develops a band-structure-based theory of exciton-polaritons in 3D inverse-opal photonic crystals doped with quantum dots, showing that polariton condensation is controlled not only by the band minimum but also by valley geometry, van-Hove singularities, and relaxation pathways. Tuning exciton resonance and light-matter coupling can reconstruct the relative W-X band ordering, strongly affecting critical temperature and condensation regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a theoretical framework for exciton-polaritons in 3D photonic crystals and understand how band geometry beyond the global minimum (W-point valleys and X-point van-Hove saddle) controls Bose-Einstein condensation and nonequilibrium polariton kinetics.

Method: Symmetry-selected bright photonic branch near the photonic gap edge; exciton-photon Hamiltonian construction; lower-polariton band structure analysis; momentum-resolved Boltzmann model for driven-dissipative kinetics under non-resonant pumping.

Result: W valleys determine equilibrium BEC threshold; X-point saddle provides finite excited-state capacity that renormalizes critical temperature when thermally accessible; tuning exciton resonance and coupling reconstructs W-X ordering, strongly varying Tc; kinetic modeling yields three regimes: W-dominated condensation, mixed W-X, or X-dominated nonequilibrium coherent state.

Conclusion: 3D photonic-crystal polaritons offer a platform where condensation is controlled by valley geometry, van-Hove-enhanced phase space, and relaxation pathways, not just the band minimum.

Abstract: We develop a band-structure-based theory of exciton-polaritons in a three-dimensional inverse-opal photonic crystal doped with semiconductor quantum dots. Starting from a symmetry-selected bright photonic branch near the photonic gap edge, we construct an exciton-photon Hamiltonian and obtain a lower-polariton band with a W-point global minimum and a nearby X-point van-Hove-enhanced density of states. We show that the W valleys determine the equilibrium Bose-Einstein condensation threshold, while the X-point saddle provides a finite excited-state capacity that renormalizes the critical temperature when the W-X offset is thermally accessible. By tuning the exciton resonance and the light-matter coupling, the relative W-X ordering can be reconstructed, leading to a strong variation of the critical temperature. We further formulate a momentum-resolved Boltzmann model for driven-dissipative kinetics. Under non-resonant pumping, reservoir feeding, radiative decay, and inter-sector relaxation can produce either W-dominated condensation, a mixed W-X regime, or an X-dominated nonequilibrium coherent state. Our results establish three-dimensional photonic-crystal polaritons as a platform where condensation is controlled not only by the band minimum but also by valley geometry, van-Hove-enhanced phase space, and relaxation pathways.

</details>


### [19] [In-plane and out-of-plane magnetic field driven Josephson diode effect in magic-angle twisted four-layer graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21229)
*Marta Perego,Sayan Banerjee,Mohamad Abu El Hija,Clara Galante Agero,Alexandra Mestre-Torà,Giovanni Zhang,Takashi Taniguchi,Kenji Watanabe,Mathias S. Scheurer,Thomas Ihn,Klaus Ensslin*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Researchers demonstrate both out-of-plane and in-plane driven Josephson diode effects in magic-angle twisted four-layer graphene junctions, observing a diode effect at zero out-of-plane field tuned by in-plane magnetic fields due to strong in-plane orbital coupling sensitive to layer parity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore the superconducting diode effect in even-layer twisted graphene systems (specifically four-layer) and understand the symmetry-breaking mechanisms, as prior studies focused on bilayer or trilayer systems under out-of-plane magnetic fields.

Method: Experimental measurement of Josephson diode effect in a magic-angle twisted four-layer graphene junction under both out-of-plane and in-plane magnetic field configurations.

Result: Observation of a diode effect at zero out-of-plane magnetic field, tunable by increasing in-plane magnetic field, indicating strong in-plane orbital coupling with sensitivity to layer parity.

Conclusion: In-plane magnetic fields are a vital tool for probing microscopic properties and symmetry-breaking mechanisms in even-layer twisted graphene, with layer parity playing a key role.

Abstract: The superconducting diode effect offers a powerful probe into the fundamental symmetries of quantum materials. Recent studies on twisted graphene diodes have predominantly focused on bilayer or trilayer systems under out-of-plane magnetic fields. Here, we demonstrate both out-of-plane and in-plane driven Josephson diode effects in a magic-angle twisted four-layer graphene junction, i.e., an even number of layers. We observe the emergence of a diode effect at zero out-of-plane field, tuned by an increasing in-plane magnetic field. This result points to the presence of strong in-plane orbital coupling, which is highly sensitive to the specific layer parity of the structure. Our findings provide experimental insights into the symmetry-breaking mechanisms of even-layer twisted graphene, establishing in-plane magnetic fields as a vital tool for unravelling their microscopic properties.

</details>


### [20] [Topological Hall Effect in Antiferromagnetic Co doped Fe$_3$GaTe$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21254)
*Shyam Raj Karullithodi,Yeonkyu Lee,Vadym Kulichenko,W. Kice Brown,Sang-Eon Lee,Chanyoung Lee,Jinyoung Yun,Gregory T. McCandless,Julia Y. Chan,Jeehoon Kim,Luis Balicas*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper investigates Fe₃₋ₓCoₓGaTe₂, a van der Waals material system exhibiting a temperature- and Co-doping-dependent phase diagram spanning ferromagnetic, ferrimagnetic, and antiferromagnetic phases. For x ≈ 0.6, magnetization, Hall effect, and magnetic force microscopy reveal a metamagnetic transition and a pronounced topological Hall effect, indicating field-induced chiral spin textures (skyrmions). The study suggests the coexistence and transformation of antiferromagnetic skyrmions into ferromagnetic skyrmions under applied magnetic field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To experimentally confirm the phase diagram of Fe₃₋ₓCoₓGaTe₂ as a function of Co substitution and temperature, and to explore the emergence of topological spin textures (skyrmions) in the antiferromagnetic and metamagnetic regimes.

Method: Magnetization measurements, electrical transport (Hall effect), and magnetic force microscopy (MFM) on Fe₃₋ₓCoₓGaTe₂ single crystals with varying Co fraction x.

Result: For x ≈ 0.6, the Hall effect mimics magnetization via a metamagnetic transition; a pronounced topological Hall signal peaks at μ₀H = 4 T; MFM reveals nearly circular magnetic domains (100–200 nm) in the AFM phase; analysis links topological Hall effect to field-stabilized chiral domain structures.

Conclusion: Co-doped Fe₃GaTe₂ hosts field-induced transformation from AFM skyrmions to FM skyrmions, offering a platform to study coupled topological spin textures and their Hall effects.

Abstract: Fe$_3$GaTe$_2$ is van der Waals (vdW) ferromagnet with a Curie temperature $T_C$ ranging from 350 K to 380 K, followed upon cooling by a ferrimagnetic transition near room temperature. Substituting Fe with Co was previously reported to induce antiferromagnetism (AFM) at a Co fraction dependent Neel temperature $T_N$. In this work, we confirm the overall phase diagram of the Fe$_{3-x}$Co$_x$GaTe$_2$ series as a function of $x$ and temperature via magnetization and electrical transport measurements. For $x \simeq 0.6$ the Hall effect is observed to mimic the magnetization as the AF ground state is suppressed by the external magnetic field via a metamagnetic transition, thus displaying an anomalous Hall response. At low temperatures, we also observe a pronounced topological Hall signal peaking at $μ_0H$ = 4 T, or within the metamagnetic transition region of fields. This observation points to the presence of magnetic field-induced chiral spin textures, such as skyrmions upon approaching magnetization saturation. Magnetic force microscopy (MFM) reveals the emergence of nearly circular magnetic domains, with diameters on the order of 100 to 200 nm, within the antiferromagnetic phase. A detailed analysis of the MFM images indicates that the topological Hall effect is closely linked to the field-induced stabilization of magnetic domain structures, likely exhibiting chiral textures. This observation suggests the possible formation of skyrmions already in the AFM phase, i.e., AFM skyrmions, that evolve into ferromagnetic (FM) ones upon increasing the magnetic field. Consequently, Co-doped Fe$_3$GaTe$_2$ might provide a platform to investigate the transformation of skyrmions, initially coupled antiferromagnetically into ferromagnetic skyrmions, and to explore its impact on the topological and skyrmion Hall effects.

</details>


### [21] [Spin qubit operations by conveyor-mode shuttling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21452)
*M. De Smet,Y. Matsumoto,D. Fernández-Fernández,L. Tryputen,S. L. de Snoo,D. J. Michalak,H. G. J. Eenink,G. Platero,S. Bosco,G. Scappucci,L. M. K. Vandersypen*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates coherent single- and two-qubit control via conveyor-mode electron shuttling in semiconductor quantum processors, using conveyor EDSR and conveyor diabatic gates to achieve motion-controlled qubit operations, motivating a reconfigurable, transport-driven spin qubit architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic qubit routing via conveyor-mode shuttling is emerging as a promising path for semiconductor quantum processors, and the authors seek to explore whether this motion itself can directly perform qubit operations beyond just spin transport.

Method: Two approaches are used: (1) conveyor electric-dipole spin resonance (EDSR), which resonantly shuttles spins through transverse magnetic-field gradients at the Larmor frequency to achieve high-fidelity rotations; (2) conveyor diabatic gates, which exploit quantization-axis tilts for tunable bang-bang control. Two-qubit interactions are achieved by combining diabatic transport with exchange activation controlled by shuttling speed and distance.

Result: Coherent single- and two-qubit control is experimentally demonstrated using conveyor-mode shuttling, with selectable effective two-qubit interactions via shuttling parameters.

Conclusion: These results motivate a new architectural paradigm of reconfigurable, transport-driven spin qubits where motion directly enables qubit operations.

Abstract: Dynamic qubit routing is emerging as a promising architectural path for semiconductor quantum processors. Charge carriers can be rapidly moved around on a chip using traveling-wave potentials known as conveyors, preserving the spin state with high fidelity. Originally developed for spin transport, conveyor-mode shuttling may also offer opportunities for performing qubit operations directly controlled by the motion itself. Here, we demonstrate coherent single- and two-qubit control by conveyor-mode electron shuttling, using two conceptually different approaches. First, conveyor electric-dipole spin resonance (conveyor EDSR) achieves high-fidelity rotations by resonantly shuttling spins through transverse magnetic-field gradients at their mean Larmor frequency. Second, conveyor diabatic gates exploit quantization-axis tilts for tunable bang-bang control. Combining diabatic conveyor transport with exchange activation controlled by the motion directly yields a variety of effective two-qubit interactions selectable via the shuttling speed and distance. These experimental results motivate an architectural paradigm of reconfigurable and transport-driven spin qubits.

</details>


### [22] [Hyperfine versus exchange interaction in the spin dynamics of spatially indirect excitons in CsPbI$_{3}$ perovskite nanocrystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21469)
*Timur S. Shamirzaev,Nataliia E. Kopteva,Dmitry S. Smirnov,Dmitri R. Yakovlev,Elena V. Kolobkova,Maria S. Kuznetsova,Eugeniyus L. Ivchenko,Yan E. Maidebura,Evgeny A. Zhukov,Manfred Bayer*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper investigates recombination dynamics, optical orientation, and optical alignment of excitons in CsPbI₃ nanocrystals embedded in glass. Large NCs (>16 nm) show low-energy emission from indirect excitons at NC/glass interfaces, with recombination spanning tens of nanoseconds to microseconds following a power law. The spin dynamics are influenced by varying exchange interaction. A theory for polarized photoluminescence of triplet excitons is developed, incorporating exchange, Zeeman, and hyperfine interactions, revealing that for the smallest exchange splitting, the exciton fine structure is dominated by hyperfine interaction with random nuclear spin fluctuations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the recombination dynamics and spin properties of excitons in CsPbI₃ nanocrystals, particularly the role of interface-localized indirect excitons and their fine structure governed by exchange, Zeeman, and hyperfine interactions.

Method: Experimental photoluminescence spectroscopy (recombination dynamics, optical orientation, optical alignment) combined with theoretical modeling of polarized photoluminescence for triplet excitons, considering electron-hole exchange interaction, Zeeman effect, and hyperfine interaction with nuclear spins.

Result: Large CsPbI₃ nanocrystals (>16 nm) exhibit indirect excitons at NC/glass interfaces with power-law recombination dynamics spanning tens of nanoseconds to microseconds. The optical alignment and orientation reveal spin dynamics governed by exchange interactions varying by orders of magnitude. For the smallest exchange splitting, the hyperfine interaction with random nuclear spin fluctuations dominates the exciton fine structure.

Conclusion: The exciton fine structure in large CsPbI₃ nanocrystals can be dominated by hyperfine interactions with nuclear spins when exchange splitting is minimal, establishing a regime where random nuclear spin fluctuations control the spin properties of the excitons.

Abstract: We study the dynamics of recombination, optical orientation, and optical alignment of excitons in ensembles of CsPbI$_{3}$ nanocrystals (NCs), synthesized in a glass matrix. In large NCs with size exceeding 16 nm, the low-energy photoluminescence is contributed by the emission of indirect in real space excitons formed by spatially separated electrons and holes, which are localized at the NC/glass interface. The recombination dynamics of an ensemble of such excitons extends from tens of nanoseconds to microseconds and exhibits a power-law dependence. Their optical alignment and optical orientation reveal a peculiar spin dynamics caused by excitons influenced by the exchange interaction, varying by orders of magnitude. We develop a theory of the polarized photoluminescence of triplet excitons, taking into account the interplay between the electron-hole exchange interaction, their Zeeman effect, and their hyperfine interaction with the nuclei. This model reveals that for the excitons with the smallest exchange splitting we reach the regime, where the exciton fine structure becomes dominated by the hyperfine interaction with the random nuclear spin fluctuations in the NCs.

</details>


### [23] [Solid-state transcapacitor, a new gain element for logic, memory and interconnects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21772)
*Amrita Mathuriya,Roza Kotlyar,Neal Reynolds,Rafael Rios,Alan Kalitsov,Peter B. Meisenheimer,James Clarkson,Noriyuki Sato,Tanay Gosavi,Ramamoorthy Ramesh,Dmitri E. Nikonov,Sasikanth Manipatruni*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper proposes a solid-state transcapacitor (TCAP), a 3-terminal logic element that uses displacement current modulation instead of dissipative transport current, overcoming the voltage, current density, and modulation limits of CMOS transistors. TCAP-based logic offers a pathway to 100-fold lower energy consumption with comparable delay to scaled CMOS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transistors face fundamental energy scaling challenges due to dissipative transport current, Boltzmann modulation limits, and inability to lower voltages. AI systems are limited by memory energy, with most energy consumed in intrachip interconnects whose voltage/charge scales are set by transistors. A new logic element that lowers voltage and charge needs is needed for both logic and memory access power.

Method: The TCAP is a solid-state displacement current modulator where a gate controls the charge-voltage relationship of the channel. A piezoelectric transcapacitor is demonstrated, using a gate-controlled stressor to modulate the capacitance of a polar channel via electromechanical coupling, achieving inversion and gain.

Result: TCAPs eliminate dissipative transport current, are not bound by Boltzmann current modulation limit, and operate with displacement currents limited only by polarization response and contact resistance. The device achieves inversion and gain for logic, is functionally equivalent to a 1T-1C memory cell, and offers a pathway to 100-fold lower energy consumption with delay comparable to ultimately scaled CMOS.

Conclusion: The TCAP approach provides a new pathway for low-energy computing beyond transistor limits, using electro-mechanics and multiferroics to overcome fundamental energy scaling challenges in logic and memory.

Abstract: Today's transistors dictate the voltage and charge scales for both logic and memory. While AI systems are recognized to be limited by memory energy, the dominant share of the energy is expended in the intrachip interconnects whose voltage and charge scales are set by transistors. The energy scaling challenges of transistors can be attributed to simultaneously meeting high current density, high current/impedance modulation, and the inability to lower voltages. Hence, a new logic element that lowers the voltage and charge needs is a priority, not only for lowering logic power but also memory access power. Here, we propose a novel 3-terminal logic element for low energy computing, a solid-state transcapacitor (TCAP). A TCAP is a solid state displacement current modulator realized by a gate which controls the charge-voltage relationship of the channel. Unlike transistors, TCAPs eliminate the dissipative transport current, are not bound by the Boltzmann current modulation limit, and operate with displacement currents limited only by the polarization response and contact resistance. Hence, TCAP circuits may simultaneously overcome the voltage, current density, and current modulation limits of CMOS. We describe a solid state TCAP using a piezoelectric transcapacitor in which a gate-controlled stressor modulates the capacitance of a polar channel via electromechanical coupling. This device achieves inversion and gain, essential for logic, and is functionally equivalent to a 1T-1C memory cell, enabling dense memory. Using voltage scaling, capacitive energy recovery, and high polarization densities of polar materials, the logic based on TCAP offers a pathway to 100 fold lower energy consumption with a delay comparable to ultimately scaled CMOS devices. This approach provides a new potential pathway for low-energy computing beyond the limits of transistors using electro-mechanics and multiferroics.

</details>


### [24] [Quantum Metric Bound State of Light Confinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22479)
*Jinchao Zhao,Rongning Liu,Xue-Yang Song,K. T. Law*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper establishes quantum metric bound states as a third confinement regime in flat bands, proving the exponential decay length is lower-bounded by the quantum metric, with experimental verification across platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the open question of the fundamental length scale of bound states induced by generic local impurities in flat bands, where conventional effective mass and compact localized state (CLS) mechanisms do not apply.

Method: Rigorous mathematical proof establishing a lower bound on exponential decay length via the quantum metric; construction of tunable flat-band generators to demonstrate tightness; verification across realistic architectures.

Result: The quantum metric of the unperturbed flat band provides a universal lower bound on the exponential decay length of impurity-induced bound states, with the bound proven to be tight.

Conclusion: The quantum metric serves as a predictive design principle for engineering confined modes in synthetic wave platforms, classifying a third confinement regime distinct from effective-mass and CLS mechanisms.

Abstract: The spatial confinement of defect-induced bound states is conventionally governed by the effective mass in dispersive bands. More recently, Compact Localized States (CLSs) arising from exact destructive interference have been utilized to achieve confinement in flat bands. However, CLSs rely on pristine lattice symmetries and fine-tuned defect profiles. The introduction of a generic local impurity inevitably breaks these strict phase-matching conditions, resulting in extensive bound states whose fundamental length scale has remained an open question. Here, we establish a third regime of confinement: the quantum metric bound state. We provide a rigorous mathematical proof demonstrating that in the absence of kinetic energy and CLS protection, the exponential decay length of these states is lower-bounded by the quantum metric of the unperturbed flat band. We demonstrate the tightness of this geometric limit by constructing a family of highly tunable flat-band generators, and we verify its universality across diverse realistic architectures. Ultimately, this classification establishes the independently measurable quantum metric as a predictive design principle for engineering confined modes in synthetic wave platforms.

</details>


### [25] [A thermodynamic uncertainty relation for (hybrid) N--S coherent conductors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21555)
*Sergi Vidal,Rosa Lopez*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper derives a hybrid quantum thermodynamic uncertainty relation (TUR) for normal-superconducting hybrid conductors, valid for arbitrary superconducting gap, by showing that the nonequilibrium excess noise admits a positive representation despite interference between Andreev and quasiparticle contributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend thermodynamic uncertainty relations (TURs), which bound current fluctuations relative to entropy production, from pure Andreev regimes to hybrid normal-superconducting systems where both quasiparticle transmission and Andreev reflection coexist, clarifying how superconducting coherence affects dissipation-precision trade-offs.

Method: Using the Anantram-Datta scattering formalism, the charge current and zero-frequency noise are decomposed into Andreev, quasiparticle, and interference contributions. The nonequilibrium excess noise is shown to admit a positive representation, enabling the proof of a hybrid quantum TUR valid for arbitrary real superconducting gap.
