Result: Although interference prevents a simple additive bound at the level of individual noise components, the excess noise can be positively represented, allowing a hybrid quantum TUR to be proven. The result extends the pure Andreev quantum TUR to regimes where both quasiparticle and Andreev processes are present.

Conclusion: Superconducting coherence reshapes current fluctuations while preserving a universal dissipation-precision constraint in hybrid quantum conductors.

Abstract: Thermodynamic uncertainty relations establish fundamental bounds between current fluctuations and entropy production in nonequilibrium systems. In hybrid normal superconducting conductors, transport is governed by the coexistence of quasiparticle transmission and Andreev reflection, where electron hole conversion transfers charge through the superconducting condensate. Using the Anantram Datta scattering formalism, we decompose the charge current and zero-frequency noise into Andreev, quasiparticle, and interference contributions. Although the interference term prevents a simple additive bound at the level of individual noise components, we show that the nonequilibrium excess noise admits a positive representation. This allows us to prove a hybrid quantum thermodynamic uncertainty relation valid for an arbitrary real superconducting gap. Our result extends the pure Andreev quantum TUR to regimes where quasiparticle and Andreev processes coexist, clarifying how superconducting coherence reshapes current fluctuations while preserving a universal dissipation precision constraint in hybrid quantum conductors.

</details>


### [26] [Assessing Majorana states and qubits through quantum capacitance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21580)
*Rodrigo A. Dourado,Ramón Aguado,Jeroen Danon,Martin Leijnse,Rubén Seoane Souto*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper shows that quantum capacitance (QC) measurements, using an auxiliary quantum dot as a sensor, can simultaneously resolve ground-state energy splitting and Majorana bound state (MBS) overlap in topological qubits, providing direct access to internal degrees of freedom. Analytic expressions derived from a low-energy effective model allow determination of these figures of merit from QC maxima positions and magnitudes in even and odd parity sectors. Results are validated with microscopic QD-based Kitaev chain models, establishing QC as a probe of MBS quality and a tool for topological-device optimization preserving fermion parity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend quantum capacitance (QC) beyond parity readout of Majorana-based topological qubits, enabling simultaneous measurement of ground-state energy splitting and MBS overlap—two key figures of merit that characterize the internal degrees of freedom of the device.

Method: Uses a low-energy effective model to derive analytic expressions linking QC maxima (position and magnitude in even/odd parity sectors) to ground-state splitting and MBS overlap, validated by a microscopic model of quantum-dot-based Kitaev chains and qubits.

Result: The ground-state energy splitting and MBS overlap can be extracted from the relative position and magnitude of QC maxima as functions of the auxiliary quantum dot energy.

Conclusion: Quantum capacitance serves not only as a parity readout tool but also as a direct probe of MBS quality and a device-optimization tool that preserves fermion parity, applicable across various MBS-based device architectures.

Abstract: Quantum capacitance (QC) has recently emerged as a promising tool for parity readout in topological qubits based on Majorana bound states (MBSs). Here, we show that this capability can be extended further: by employing an auxiliary quantum dot (QD) as a sensor, we demonstrate that QC measurements simultaneously resolve two fundamental figures of merit of the device, the ground-state energy splitting and the MBS overlap, thus providing direct access to the underlying internal degrees of freedom. Using a low-energy effective model, we provide analytic expressions for these two figures of merit that can be determined from the relative position and magnitude of the QC maxima in the even and odd parity sectors as functions of the auxiliary-QD energy. We further validate these results with a microscopic model of QD-based Kitaev chains and qubits, demonstrating their applicability in a wide range of MBS-based devices. Our results establish QC as a probe of MBS quality and a tool for topological-device optimization that preserves fermion parity.

</details>


### [27] [Subgap Linear Thermoelectricity in Superconducting Quantum Hall Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21599)
*Leonardo Pierattelli,Fabio Taddei,Alessandro Romito,Alessandro Braggio*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Integer quantum Hall setups proximized by superconductors can exhibit subgap thermoelectric effects in the linear-response regime when triplet superconducting correlations are present, with Seebeck coefficients reaching ~k_B/e in the middle of the quantum Hall plateau.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demonstrate that thermoelectric effects can emerge in quantum Hall systems proximized by superconductors despite linear band dispersion of edge states, specifically through triplet superconducting correlations.

Method: Minimal device setup with integer quantum Hall system proximized by superconductors; analytical calculations in the linear-response regime; analysis of Andreev process-mediated Seebeck effect; parameter dependence characterization.

Result: Seebeck coefficient can reach values on the order of k_B/e in the middle of the quantum Hall plateau; both triplet correlations and spin polarization are essential for the thermoelectric effect to emerge.

Conclusion: Triplet superconducting correlations enable subgap thermoelectric effects in quantum Hall-superconductor hybrid systems, with the effect arising despite linear edge state dispersion and requiring both triplet correlations and spin polarization.

Abstract: We show that an integer quantum Hall setup proximized by superconductors can exhibit subgap thermoelectric effects in the linear-response regime when triplet superconducting correlations are present. We devise a minimal setup that enables a nonzero Seebeck effect mediated by Andreev processes and predict that the corresponding Seebeck coefficient can reach values on the order of $k_B/e$ in the middle of the quantum Hall plateau. We analytically show that both triplet correlations and spin polarization are essential for the emergence of the thermoelectric effect, which arises despite the linear band dispersion of the edge states. We characterize the dependence of the thermoelectric response on the Hamiltonian parameters and the system's temperature regime.

</details>


### [28] [Superconducting cavity probes sliding ferroelectricity in small-angle twisted WSe$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22140)
*Krishnendu Maji,Supriya Mandal,Sriram H.,Rishiraj Rajkhowa,Malhar Date,Sayani Pal,Meghan P. Patankar,Kenji Watanabe,Takashi Taniguchi,Vibhor Singh,Mandar M. Deshmukh*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This work probes sliding ferroelectricity in small-angle parallel stacked WSe₂ bilayers using a high-frequency AC cavity technique, observing hysteretic capacitance and quality factor responses that confirm ferroelectric switching and reveal domain-wall relaxation dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sliding ferroelectricity in 2D materials (hBN, TMDCs) is a recent discovery with potential applications in memory and transistors. Probing the polarization and dynamics of such systems, especially at high frequencies, is crucial for understanding and utilizing this new ferroelectric type.

Method: The polarization of small-angle parallel stacked WSe₂ is probed by measuring its high-frequency AC response. The material is embedded into a half-wave superconducting coplanar waveguide cavity to observe capacitance and quality factor changes during ferroelectric switching.

Result: A hysteretic response is observed in both the capacitance of the stack and the quality factor of the cavity, confirming ferroelectric switching. Relaxation effects associated with ferroelectric domain-wall motion are also detected.

Conclusion: The cavity-based high-frequency technique successfully probes sliding ferroelectricity and domain-wall dynamics in 2D WSe₂, and has potential for broader application in studying domain-wall motion in ferroelectric systems at high frequencies.

Abstract: Ferroelectricity is a property of materials that exhibit spontaneous charge polarization. Ferroelectricity in 2D materials is interesting because of their applications in memory devices and field-effect transistors. Recently, a new type of ferroelectricity, known as sliding ferroelectricity, has been discovered, in which parallel-stacked bilayers of hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) or transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDCs) develop an out-of-plane electric polarization. In this work, we probe the polarization of small-angle parallel stacked WSe$_2$ by measuring its high-frequency AC response, achieved by embedding it into a half-wave superconducting coplanar waveguide cavity. We observe a hysteretic response in the capacitance of the stack and quality factor of the cavity, confirming ferroelectric switching in the system. Our results further reveal relaxation effects associated with ferroelectric domain-wall motion. This cavity-based technique has potential applications in probing domain-wall dynamics in a ferroelectric system at high frequencies.

</details>


### [29] [Antiferromagnetic pseudospintronics without spin splitting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21860)
*Shu-Hui Zhang,Shu-Qi Liu,Dan-Yang Han,Zhan Kong,Ding-Fu Shao,Wen Yang,Kai Chang*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper proposes "AFM pseudospintronics," a new paradigm exploiting sublattice pseudospin in zero-spin-splitting antiferromagnets, enabling Néel-vector-controlled transport and tunneling magnetoresistance without relying on spin splitting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional AFM spintronics requires spin splitting, which excludes many collinear antiferromagnets with compensated spin sublattices. The authors aim to extend spintronics to zero-spin-splitting AFMs by utilizing the sublattice degree of freedom.

Method: The authors uncover partial pseudospin-spin coupling in a honeycomb AFM: out-of-plane pseudospin polarization is spin-dependent, enabling Néel vector control of pseudospin. They analyze sublattice-resolved Friedel oscillations from impurities and design an all-in-one AFM junction for gate-tunable tunneling magnetoresistance.

Result: They demonstrate that pseudospin polarization can be switched by reversing the Néel vector, and that impurity-induced Friedel oscillations exhibit a sublattice-resolved amplitude ratio determined solely by pseudospin polarization, measurable by spin-polarized STM. They also show Néel-vector-controlled transmission and large nonvolatile tunneling magnetoresistance with resonant enhancement in 2D devices.

Conclusion: This work establishes AFM pseudospintronics as a new paradigm that extends spintronics beyond the conventional spin-splitting requirement, leveraging sublattice pseudospin in zero-spin-splitting antiferromagnets for functional spintronic devices.

Abstract: Antiferromagnets (AFMs) are promising for high-density spintronics due to their zero net magnetization, yet conventional AFM spintronics relies on spin splitting-a requirement that excludes many collinear AFMs with compensated spin sublattices. Here we exploit the sublattice degree of freedom in a honeycomb AFM with zero spin splitting. We uncover a coupling between spin and sublattice: the out-of-plane pseudospin polarization is spin-dependent, a mechanism we term partial pseudospin-spin coupling. This allows switching of the pseudospin polarization by reversing the Néel vector. Introducing an impurity into a specific sublattice induces Friedel oscillations with a sublattice-resolved amplitude ratio dictated solely by the pseudospin polarization, which is directly measurable by spin-polarized scanning tunneling microscopy. Furthermore, we demonstrate Néel-vector-controlled transmission and a large nonvolatile tunneling magnetoresistance in an all-in-one AFM junction, with pronounced resonant enhancement in gate-tunable two-dimensional devices. Our work establishes a new paradigm-AFM pseudospintronics-that utilizes the sublattice pseudospin in zero-spin-splitting AFMs, extending spintronics beyond the conventional spin-splitting paradigm.

</details>


### [30] [Rotating Zeeman field as a tool for Majorana zero mode detection in topological superconducting wire](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23183)
*Piotr Stefański*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Quantum dot spin polarization analysis can detect Majorana zero modes and topological phase transitions in nanowires by rotating the Zeeman field direction, revealing distinct polarization responses in topological vs trivial states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a detection method for Majorana zero modes and topological phase transitions in nanowires using spin polarization measurements of a quantum dot attached to the topological wire.

Method: Spin polarization of a quantum dot attached to a topological nanowire is analyzed by rotating the Zeeman field direction in the wire while keeping the dot's Zeeman field fixed. The polarization response at Fermi energy is measured as a function of field orientation and magnitude.

Result: In the presence of MZMs, QD spin polarization changes significantly when the wire's Zeeman field rotates from parallel to perpendicular to the wire axis. In trivial states, polarization remains constant during rotation. A characteristic nonlinear dependence of spin polarization on magnetic field magnitude at its critical value identifies topological phase transitions, independent of coupling strength or Zeeman field angle.

Conclusion: QD spin polarization analysis serves as a robust and experimentally accessible probe for detecting Majorana zero modes and topological phase transitions in nanowire systems.

Abstract: We demonstrate that analysis of the spin polarization of a quantum dot (QD) attached to the topological wire can provide valuable insights into Majorana zero mode (MZM) formation and topological phase transition. Detection is realized by rotation of the Zeeman field in the wire, while retaining the Zeeman field direction in the dot intact. In the presence of Majorana mode, the effective QD spin polarization at Fermi energy changes significantly when the direction of the Zeeman field in the wire changes from parallel to perpendicular to the wire axis. It can be opposed to the wire in its trivial state, when spin polarization remains practically constant while the magnetic field is rotated. Similar unaltered spin polarization is observed when QD spin sub-level at Fermi energy mimics MZM. Moreover, the characteristic non-linear dependence of the spin polarization on the magnetic field magnitude at its critical value identifies a topological phase transition in the wire. This feature is observed independently on the coupling strength of the wire to the dot and the angle of the Zeeman field.

</details>


### [31] [Effect of weak positional disorder on the miniband structure of spherical quantum dot chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22128)
*R. Ya. Leshko*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A theoretical framework using tight-binding and effective-medium approximations maps weak positional disorder in quantum dot chains onto renormalized Hamiltonian parameters, showing ~8-12% miniband broadening at σ=0.1a while states remain delocalized.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how unavoidable fabrication-induced positional disorder affects the electronic miniband structure in 1D chains of spherical quantum dots, which is crucial for designing quantum dot-based devices.

Method: Tight-binding approximation combined with effective-medium approach; ensemble averaging over narrow Gaussian distribution of positional deviations; analytical derivation of renormalized hopping integral B, overlap integral Q, and on-site energy shift M.

Result: Weak positional disorder broadens minibands by 8-12% for σ=0.1a; sensitivity decreases with increasing lattice period due to exponential wavefunction decay; Anderson localization length remains much larger than lattice constant.

Conclusion: In the weak-disorder regime, miniband states remain delocalized despite measurable band broadening, and disorder effects diminish for larger inter-dot separations.

Abstract: A theoretical framework is developed for the electron miniband structure in one-dimensional chains of spherical quantum dots subjected to weak positional disorder. Within the tight-binding approximation combined with the effective-medium approach, the stochastic fluctuations of the inter-dot spacing are mapped onto the renormalization of the key Hamiltonian parameters: the hopping integral $ B $, the overlap integral $ Q $, and the on-site energy shift $ M $. Analytical expressions for these disorder-renormalized parameters are derived by performing an ensemble average over a narrow Gaussian distribution of positional deviations ($ σ\ll a $). The resulting generalized dispersion relation shows that weak positional disorder causes a broadening of the minibands. Specifically, for typical fabrication fluctuations $σ= 0.1\,a $, the miniband width increases by 8-12\% (depending on the mean inter-dot distance $a$). At the same time, the sensitivity of the miniband width to disorder decreases rapidly with increasing lattice period due to the exponential decay of the electron wave functions. In the considered weak-disorder regime, the Anderson localization length significantly exceeds the lattice constant, so the miniband states remain delocalized.

</details>


### [32] [Helical Domain-Wall-Ring Networks Reshape Superconducting Correlations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23599)
*Yi-Chun Hung,Arun Bansil*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper studies superconducting correlations in helical domain-wall-ring networks in moiré materials (twisted bilayer honeycomb lattices). Using both infinite-size theory and finite-size variational approaches, they find that while inter-ring pair tunneling is renormalization-group relevant, finite-size effects strongly suppress phase locking, and the SC scaling dimension remains insensitive to pair-tunneling strength—revealing a qualitative mismatch from infinite-size predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore interaction-driven orders (specifically superconductivity) in confined domain-wall networks formed in moiré materials, which remain largely unexplored despite the distinct platform provided by extended domain-wall networks.

Method: Two-pronged theoretical approach: (1) infinite-size renormalization group analysis to study inter-ring SC-pair tunneling relevance; (2) self-consistent variational method to capture finite-size effects from the ring-network geometry.

Result: In finite-size ring networks, the induced phase-locking scale is strongly suppressed even when infinite-size theory predicts strongly-coupled pair tunneling. The SC scaling dimension decreases with decreasing twist angle but remains insensitive to pair-tunneling strength, qualitatively differing from infinite-size expectations.

Conclusion: Confinement effects and ring-network geometry reshape SC correlations such that finite ring networks exhibit qualitatively distinct collective behavior, not simply approaching their infinite-size counterparts.

Abstract: Extended domain-wall networks emerging in moiré materials provide a distinct platform for quasi-one-dimensional electronic states. However, the interaction-driven orders in confined networks remain largely unexplored. Here, we discuss superconducting (SC) correlations in interacting helical domain-wall-ring networks that emerge in the closed topological domains formed within the moiré patterns of an underlying twisted bilayer honeycomb lattice. We first analyze the system within the framework of an infinite-size theory and show that inter-ring SC-pair tunneling is renormalization-group relevant and thus enhances SC correlations through inter-ring phase locking. To address finite-size effects resulting from the ring-network geometry, we present a self-consistent variational approach. Our analysis shows that even in the regime where the infinite-size theory predicts strongly-coupled pair tunneling, the induced phase-locking scale remains strongly suppressed. In contrast, the SC scaling dimension continues to decrease with decreasing twist angle and remains insensitive to the pair-tunneling strength, revealing a qualitative mismatch from the infinite-size expectation. This discrepancy demonstrates that ring networks do not simply approach their infinite-size counterparts but can exhibit qualitatively distinct collective behavior. Our study highlights how the interplay of confinement effects and ring-network geometry can reshape SC correlations.

</details>


### [33] [High-Resolution Probing of Molecular Junctions: Vibrational Fingerprinting and Parameter Extraction via Current Noise Spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22663)
*Rani Arielly*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper introduces a high-resolution noise spectroscopy method using suspended-wire molecular junctions to probe charge transport in molecular devices. By analyzing voltage-dependent current noise instead of average current, it overcomes thermal broadening limitations and extracts molecular parameters such as mode energies, anharmonicities, dissociation energies, and reorganization energies, establishing noise spectroscopy as a sensitive platform for chemical sensing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional I-V measurements and Inelastic Electron Tunneling Spectroscopy (IETS) are limited by temperature-dependent line-width broadening, hindering precise characterization of charge transport mechanisms and the interplay between electronic and nuclear degrees of freedom in molecular electronic devices.

Method: The study employs suspended-wire molecular junctions (SWMJs) based on self-assembled monolayers (SAMs) of 1-decanethiol (C10) and 1,1',4',1''-terphenyl-4-thiol (TPT). It probes voltage-dependent current noise (ΔI) and uses a fast-convolution-based Landauer-Büttiker transport model fitted to experimental data to map vibrational manifolds.

Result: Electronic noise spectroscopy circumvents thermal degradation by probing transition rates between vibrational manifolds instead of simple conductance channel additions, enabling sub-thermal feature mapping. The method extracts mode energies, anharmonicities (x_e), dissociation energies (D_e), and local environment reorganization energies (E_r).

Conclusion: Noise spectroscopy provides parameter-dense molecular fingerprints, establishing it as a highly sensitive platform for chemical sensing and discrimination in advanced quantum devices.

Abstract: The precise realization of molecular electronic devices requires a comprehensive understanding of charge transport mechanisms and the specific interplay between electronic and nuclear degrees of freedom. While average current measurements (I-V characteristics) and conventional Inelastic Electron Tunneling Spectroscopy (IETS) offer valuable insights, they are fundamentally limited by temperature-dependent line-width broadening. This study presents a high-resolution spectroscopic methodology utilizing suspended-wire molecular junctions (SWMJs) based on self-assembled monolayers (SAMs) of 1-decanethiol (C10) and 1,1',4',1''-terphenyl-4-thiol (TPT). By systematically probing the voltage-dependent current noise ($ΔI$), we demonstrate that electronic noise spectroscopy circumvents thermal degradation by probing transition rates between vibrational manifolds rather than simple additions of conductance channels, which enables sub-thermal feature mapping. Leveraging a fast-convolution-based Landauer-Büttiker transport model fitted to experimental data, we map complex vibrational manifolds, including high-energy overtones. This allows for the direct extraction of crucial nanoscale molecular parameters, including mode energies, anharmonicities ($x_e$), dissociation energies ($D_e$), and local environment reorganization energies ($E_r$). These parameter-dense noise signatures act as a unique molecular fingerprint, establishing noise spectroscopy as a highly sensitive platform for chemical sensing and discrimination in advanced quantum devices.

</details>


### [34] [Deterministic control of the probabilistic phase dynamics in injection-locked spin-torque nano-oscillators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22208)
*Abderrazak Hakam,Chloé Chopin,Nhat-Tan Phan,Nicolas Mollard,Franck Badets,Louis Hutin,Luana Benetti,Alex S. Jenkins,Ricardo Ferreira,Ursula Ebels,Philippe Talatchian*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that spin-torque nano-oscillators under second-harmonic injection-locking, despite intrinsic thermal phase fluctuations, can be programmed to continuously control the probability of occupying two degenerate phase states (separated by π). By applying a weak RF perturbation at the free-running frequency, the phase-jump rates between attractors are tuned, enabling deterministic probability control from unbiased limits to near 0 or 1, providing two complementary control knobs for probabilistic computing applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To harness the inherent stochasticity of spin-torque nano-oscillators for probabilistic computing, rather than treating thermal phase fluctuations as a nuisance, by demonstrating that the probability distribution of phase states can be deterministically programmed.

Method: Experimental investigation of a vortex-based STNO under second-harmonic injection-locking; application of a weak radio-frequency perturbation at the free-running frequency to bias phase-jump rates; use of a phase-reduced description based on an effective quasipotential to model the observed behavior.

Result: Continuous probability control from the unbiased limit to values approaching 0 or 1; the bias phase selects which attractor is favored, and the bias amplitude sets the strength of the imbalance; two complementary control knobs demonstrated within a single nanoscale device.

Conclusion: Injection-locked STNOs can serve as programmable stochastic elements, providing a hardware primitive for probabilistic computing, Ising machines, and brain-inspired computing architectures.

Abstract: Spin-torque nano-oscillators (STNOs) inherently exhibit thermally driven phase fluctuations that render their dynamics truly stochastic. Here, we demonstrate that, despite this intrinsic randomness, the probability of occupying each phase state can be deterministically and continuously programmed. We experimentally investigate a vortex-based STNO operating under second-harmonic injection-locking, where the oscillator phase settles into two degenerate attractors separated by $π$ and undergoes thermally activated phase jumps. By applying a weak radio-frequency perturbation at the free-running frequency, we tune the phase-jump rates between the two attractors without suppressing the fluctuations, achieving continuous probability control from the unbiased limit to values approaching 0 or 1. The bias phase selects which attractor is favored while the bias amplitude sets the strength of the imbalance, providing two complementary control knobs within a single nanoscale device. A phase-reduced description based on an effective quasipotential quantitatively accounts for the observations. These results establish injection-locked STNOs as programmable stochastic elements and provide a hardware primitive for probabilistic computing, Ising machines, and brain-inspired computing architectures.

</details>


### [35] [Non-equilibrium angular momentum selectivity and filtering in chiral carbon nanotubes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22235)
*Sergio Shmayev,D. R. da Costa,D. A. Bahamon*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper investigates orbital transport in chiral carbon nanotubes, finding that orbital Edelstein susceptibility is strongly dependent on chirality and diameter (not universal), that metallic and semiconducting CNTs behave differently near contacts (metallic recover quickly, semiconducting show oscillatory interference), and that chiral CNTs can act as orbital-angular-momentum filters via contact self-energy engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically probe the interplay between structural chirality and quantum transport in quasi-one-dimensional systems, specifically investigating the non-equilibrium orbital response in chiral CNTs beyond universal scaling assumptions.

Method: Systematic computational study of non-equilibrium orbital response across a broad set of metallic and semiconducting chiral CNTs, analyzing orbital Edelstein susceptibility as a function of chirality and diameter, examining contact effects, and incorporating angular correlations into contact self-energy.

Result: Orbital Edelstein susceptibility depends strongly on both chirality and diameter, forming characteristic branches determined by the chiral wrapping vector. Metallic CNTs recover intrinsic orbital response away from contacts, while semiconducting CNTs show oscillatory behavior from interference of angular-momentum channels. Chiral CNTs can function as orbital-angular-momentum filters.

Conclusion: Chiral CNTs offer a highly tunable platform for orbital transport with no single universal scaling law; they can serve as efficient orbital-angular-momentum filters, selectively transmitting orbitally textured states based on crystal angular momentum.

Abstract: Carbon nanotubes (CNTs) constitute a highly tunable platform for probing the interplay between structural chirality and quantum transport in quasi-one-dimensional systems. Here, we perform a systematic study of the non-equilibrium orbital response across a broad set of metallic and semiconducting chiral CNTs. We find that the orbital Edelstein susceptibility depends strongly on both chirality and nanotube diameter, revealing that the orbital response cannot be captured by a universal scaling law. Instead, distinct families of CNTs emerge, forming characteristic orbital-response branches uniquely determined by the chiral wrapping vector. We further investigate the role of metallic contacts on orbital-current generation and orbital selectivity. While metallic CNTs rapidly recover their intrinsic orbital response away from the contact region, semiconducting CNTs display pronounced oscillatory behavior arising from interference between transport channels carrying different angular momenta injected by wide-band metallic contacts. Finally, by incorporating angular correlations into the contact self-energy, we demonstrate that chiral CNTs can operate as efficient orbital-angular-momentum filters, selectively transmitting orbitally textured electronic states in accordance with the crystal angular momentum of the propagating bands.

</details>


### [36] [Keldysh field theory of spin- and valley-distinguished polariton nonlinearities in transition-metal dichalcogenide monolayers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22242)
*Anna M. Grudinina,Nina S. Voronova*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The authors develop a nonequilibrium field-theory approach to account for all valley and spin degrees of freedom in TMD-based exciton-polariton systems, revealing sixteen interaction terms and showing that spin-dark excitons contribute more to blueshift than bright intravalley excitons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing descriptions of bosonization and polariton interactions in TMD-based systems overlook the valley degree of freedom and various particle spin combinations, despite the rich exciton and polariton physics arising from electrons' valley and spin degrees of freedom in TMDs.

Method: Derivation of a nonequilibrium field-theory approach in the path integral formalism to track all polariton nonlinearities in the strong coupling regime.

Result: When all bright and dark exciton species are considered, TMD monolayer-based polariton systems feature sixteen different nonlinear contributions from interactions plus additional saturation-related terms. Spin-dark excitons contribute significantly more to the blueshift than bright intravalley excitons, although intravalley exciton interactions are overall dominant.

Conclusion: The valley and spin degrees of freedom introduce a rich landscape of nonlinearities in TMD polariton systems, with spin-dark excitons playing a surprisingly important role in the blueshift, which has implications for photonic device design.

Abstract: Electrons in transition-metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) possess valley and spin degrees of freedom, which leads to rich exciton and exciton-polariton physics with nontrivial scattering dynamics and enhanced nonlinearities, presenting a key mechanism for photonic devices. Yet, existing descriptions of bosonization and polariton interactions in TMD-based systems overlook the valley degree of freedom as well as the various particles' spins combinations. In this work, we derive a nonequilibrium field-theory approach in the path integral formalism that allows to track all the polariton nonlinearities in the strong coupling regime. We demonstrate that, when all the bright and dark exciton species are considered, the TMD monolayer-based polariton systems feature sixteen different nonlinear contributions due to interactions and even more saturation-related terms. Strikingly, while the interactions of excitons within one valley are overall dominant, we show that the contribution to the blueshift from spin-dark excitons is much higher than that from bright intravalley excitons.

</details>


### [37] [Second-order dc conductivity in the velocity-gauge Keldysh formalism: gauge-invariant decomposition into nonlinear Drude, Berry-curvature-dipole, and quantum-metric responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22359)
*Junya Shibata*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper derives a gauge-invariant, clean-limit decomposition of the second-order dc nonlinear conductivity in multiband tight-binding systems, identifying four contributions with distinct lifetime scalings: nonlinear Drude (τ²), Berry-curvature-dipole (τ), intraband quantum-metric-dipole (τ⁰), and interband quantum-metric-dipole (τ⁰). It shows cancellation of connection-dependent terms in the velocity-gauge Keldysh-Kubo framework and introduces a two-band model where Berry curvature vanishes but the intraband quantum-metric dipole remains finite.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To derive a gauge-invariant decomposition of the second-order dc nonlinear conductivity that cleanly separates contributions with distinct physical origins and lifetime scalings, particularly isolating quantum-metric-dipole terms from the Berry-curvature-dipole mechanism.

Method: Velocity-gauge Keldysh Green's function formalism with constant-relaxation-time approximation; band-basis expansion with covariant quantum connection and three-Berry-connection sectors; complementary projector-based derivation for cross-validation.

Result: Four contributions identified: nonlinear Drude (∝τ²), Berry-curvature-dipole (∝τ), intraband quantum-metric-dipole (∝τ⁰), and interband quantum-metric-dipole (∝τ⁰). Connection-dependent commutator terms cancel exactly between σ^C and σ^T sectors. A real two-band model demonstrates finite intraband quantum-metric dipole with vanishing Berry curvature.

Conclusion: Quantum-metric-dipole responses provide a practical route to dc nonlinear responses not reducible to Berry-curvature-dipole mechanisms; the formalism agrees with complementary projector-based results in the literature.

Abstract: We derive a gauge-invariant clean-limit decomposition of the second-order dc nonlinear conductivity in multiband tight-binding systems within the velocity-gauge Keldysh Green's function formalism. In the constant-relaxation-time approximation, the dc response separates into four contributions with distinct lifetime $τ$ scalings and physical origins: the nonlinear Drude term $σ^{\mathrm{ND}}_{ijk}\proptoτ^{2}$, the Berry-curvature-dipole term $σ^{\mathrm{BCD}}_{ijk}\proptoτ$, the intraband quantum-metric-dipole term $σ^{\mathrm{intra\text{-}QMD}}_{ijk}\proptoτ^{0}$, and the interband quantum-metric-dipole term $σ^{\mathrm{inter\text{-}QMD}}_{ijk}\proptoτ^{0}$. The intraband term is a Fermi-surface dipole of the ordinary band quantum metric, while the interband term is written, in the present representation, as a Fermi-sea-type response involving a band-normalized quantum metric. Working entirely within the velocity-gauge Keldysh--Kubo framework, we show that all connection-dependent commutator terms generated in the band-basis expansion cancel exactly between the covariant-quantum-connection sector $σ^{\mathcal{C}}_{ijk}$ and the three-Berry-connection sector $σ^{\mathcal{T}}_{ijk}$, making the role of the Peierls contact velocity vertices $V_{ij}$ and $V_{ijk}$ explicit; a complementary projector-based derivation appears in Ulrich et al., Phys. Rev. B 113, L201107 (2026), and our Fermi-surface dc-limit expression agrees with that reference after accounting for index and convention differences. As a diagnostic illustration, we introduce a real two-band model in which the Berry curvature and hence the BCD response vanish identically while the intraband quantum-metric dipole remains finite, establishing a practical route to quantum-metric dc responses not reducible to the Berry-curvature-dipole mechanism.

</details>


### [38] [Hysteresis-Driven Radiative Mpemba Effect in Phase-Change Nanostructures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22552)
*Florian Herz*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper investigates the Mpemba effect (hotter things cooling faster than colder ones) in a radiative system for the first time, using a VO₂ nanoparticle near a SiC substrate with phase-change hysteresis, and finds that latent heat acts as a thermal buffer enabling both ordinary and inverse effects, with near-field coupling enabling passive external memory storage via substrate reflection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Mpemba effect (hotter systems cooling faster than colder ones) has been observed in convective, conductive, and quantum systems, but its radiative analogue has not been explored. The authors aim to fill this gap.

Method: The authors analytically derive an onset condition and map the phase space for the Mpemba effect using a VO₂ nanoparticle near a SiC substrate, leveraging phase-change hysteresis and near-field radiative coupling.

Result: The radiative Mpemba effect is realized via phase-change hysteresis. Latent heat acts as a thermal buffer enabling both ordinary and inverse Mpemba effects. Near-field coupling governs relaxation time and enables a passive effect where memory is stored externally via substrate reflection.

Conclusion: The radiative analogue of the Mpemba effect is successfully demonstrated, with latent heat and near-field coupling playing key roles. The effect can be both ordinary and inverse, and can store memory externally.

Abstract: The Mpemba effect states that initially hotter systems cool faster than colder ones. While known in convective, conductive, and quantum systems, its radiative analogue is unexplored. Here, this anomaly is realized via phase-change hysteresis of a VO$_2$ nanoparticle near a SiC substrate. After analytically deriving an onset condition, the phase space is mapped. Crucially, latent heat acts as a thermal buffer enabling both ordinary and inverse effects. Near-field coupling governs the relaxation time and enables a passive effect where memory is stored externally via substrate reflection.

</details>


### [39] [Selectivity in tip-induced skeletal editing via heteroatom substitution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22677)
*Shantanu Mishra,Rasmus Svensson,Valentina Malave,Florian Albrecht,Manuel Vilas-Varela,Henrik Grönbeck,Leo Gross,Diego Peña*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Using scanning probe microscopy, sulfur is selectively deleted from a seven-membered heterocycle via tip-induced chemistry, achieving >99% selectivity for skeletal editing at the single-molecule scale, compared to prior oxygen-containing rings which yielded mixed outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether selective skeletal editing of a heterocyclic core can be controlled by choosing the heteroatom (sulfur vs. oxygen), and to demonstrate a general strategy for single-molecule structural transformations.

Method: Voltage pulses from a scanning probe microscope tip were applied to a sulfur-containing seven-membered heterocycle, inducing bond rearrangements; outcomes were compared to prior experiments with an analogous oxygen-containing ring.

Result: Sulfur deletion occurred in virtually all cases, showing near-perfect selectivity. This contrasts with the oxygen-containing analogue, which gave both deletion and ring-contraction rearrangement.

Conclusion: Tip-induced chemistry combined with heteroatom selection is a powerful strategy for single-molecule skeletal editing, enabling highly selective transformations of heterocyclic frameworks.

Abstract: Skeletal editing enables precise structural modifications of molecules at late stages of a synthetic sequence, with applications in drug discovery and materials science. We recently demonstrated skeletal editing on the single-molecule scale. Voltage pulses applied by the tip of a scanning probe microscope to an oxygen-containing seven-membered heterocycle led to both oxygen deletion and ring-contraction rearrangement reactions. An open question is whether selective skeletal editing of a heterocyclic core can be achieved by an appropriate choice of the heteroatom. Here, we show that tip-induced reactions of an analogous sulfur-containing seven-membered ring results in sulfur deletion in virtually all cases. Our results demonstrate that the combination of tip-induced chemistry and heteroatom selection in the molecular design is a powerful strategy for single-molecule skeletal editing, with the potential to enable diverse structural transformations of heterocyclic frameworks.

</details>


### [40] [Modulating radiative heat and momentum transfer via the thermal Purcell effect](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22746)
*Liyao Jiao,Yaohua Liu,Gaomin Tang*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper investigates how a Fabry-Pérot cavity modifies radiative heat, linear-momentum, and angular-momentum exchange between a magneto-optic nanoparticle and the cavity using fluctuational electrodynamics, revealing selective control over energy and momentum transfer through cavity engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how geometric confinement in a Fabry-Pérot cavity selectively modifies different radiative transport quantities (heat, linear momentum, angular momentum) via the thermal Purcell effect, enabling control over nanoscale energy and momentum transfer.

Method: Fluctuational electrodynamics combined with analytical derivations of spectral densities for nonequilibrium radiative heat, linear-momentum, and angular-momentum exchange between a magneto-optic nanoparticle and a Fabry-Pérot cavity.

Result: Sub-wavelength confinement enhances radiative heat and angular-momentum transfer but suppresses lateral force; all transfer quantities oscillate spatially with particle position due to cavity mode interference; at the cavity center, the lateral force vanishes due to mirror symmetry while heat transfer and torque remain finite.

Conclusion: Cavity engineering can selectively control nanoscale energy and momentum transfer via structured electromagnetic fluctuations, with different transport quantities exhibiting distinct responses to confinement and symmetry.

Abstract: The thermal Purcell effect describes the modification of the local density of states of the fluctuating electromagnetic field induced by a Fabry-Pérot cavity, leading to the enhancement or suppression of radiative transport quantities. Using fluctuational electrodynamics, we investigate nonequilibrium radiative heat, linear-momentum, and angular-momentum exchange between a magneto-optic nanoparticle and a Fabry-Pérot cavity. Analytical expressions for the spectral densities reveal that geometric confinement modifies the electromagnetic local density of states, producing distinct behaviors for different transport quantities. Specifically, sub-wavelength confinement enhances radiative heat and angular-momentum transfer, but suppresses the lateral force. Additionally, interference between cavity modes causes all transfer quantities to oscillate spatially with particle position. At the cavity center, mirror symmetry enforces a parity decomposition of electromagnetic fluctuations resulting in a vanishing lateral force, whereas heat transfer and torque remain finite through combined even and odd modal contributions. These results demonstrate that cavity engineering provides selective control over nanoscale energy and momentum transfer via structured electromagnetic fluctuations.

</details>


### [41] [Strongly anisotropic Rytova-Keldysh interaction and the ground state of 2D excitons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23033)
*Andrei Galiautdinov*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper provides the analytical foundation for understanding the strongly anisotropic Rytova-Keldysh interaction in 2D materials by deriving closed-form asymptotic expressions for the potential across all spatial regimes, and uses this to obtain an analytical expression for the exciton binding energy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide an analytical foundation for the observation that an effective isotropic approximation remains robust for highly anisotropic 2D materials, and to derive closed-form expressions for the anisotropic Rytova-Keldysh potential.

Method: Applied the method of steepest descent and momentum-space coordinate scaling to the exact one-dimensional integral representation of the anisotropic Rytova-Keldysh potential to derive closed-form asymptotic expressions.

Result: Intermediate-range screening exhibits non-trivial amplitude scaling driven by the direction of weakest polarizability; short-range limit produces an anisotropic logarithmic well governed by the geometric mean of principal polarizabilities; derived closed-form analytical expression for exciton ground-state binding energy.

Conclusion: The analytical expressions explicitly capture the competition between effective mass and dielectric tensors in determining exciton binding energies in anisotropic 2D materials.

Abstract: The classic Rytova-Keldysh potential describes the non-local dielectric screening of Coulomb interactions in ultrathin two-dimensional (2D) materials. Recently, the corresponding potential for arbitrary in-plane anisotropy was derived in integral form, with numerical studies suggesting that an effective isotropic approximation remains robust for highly directional systems. In this paper we provide the analytical foundation for these observations by mapping the complete spatial landscape of the strongly anisotropic Rytova-Keldysh interaction. By applying the method of steepest descent and momentum-space coordinate scaling to the exact one-dimensional integral representation, we derive closed-form asymptotic expressions for the potential across all spatial regimes. We find that the intermediate-range screening exhibits a non-trivial amplitude scaling driven by the direction of weakest polarizability, while the short-range limit produces an anisotropic logarithmic well governed by the geometric mean of the principal polarizabilities. Finally, utilizing this short-range confinement, we implement an anisotropic Gaussian variational ansatz to solve the Wannier equation, providing a closed-form analytical expression for the exciton ground-state binding energy that explicitly captures the competition between the effective mass and dielectric tensors.

</details>


### [42] [Tomography of Transport Pathways in Selective-Area-Grown Nanowires Using Angle-Resolved Conductance Fluctuations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23053)
*Christian E. N. Petersen,Damon J. Carrad,Daria Beznasyuk,Thomas S. Jespersen*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper applies conductance fluctuation tomography to InAs nanowires, using magnetic-field-dependent interference features to map phase-coherent transport pathways. Results distinguish bulk, near-surface, and facet-confined transport, showing normal devices favor apex transport while superconductor hybrids show facet-dependent behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the spatial distribution of carriers is important for interpreting transport in nanoscale devices.

Method: Apply conductance fluctuation tomography to planar selective-area-grown InAs nanowires in normal-normal and normal-superconductor geometries, tracking conductance-interference features as a function of magnetic-field strength and orientation to extract geometry of phase-coherent transport pathways.

Result: Normal-contact devices show behavior consistent with coherent transport across the nanowire apex; hybrid normal-superconductor devices exhibit signatures of more facet-dependent transport. Measurements are consistent with transport dominated by a near-surface accumulation layer in InAs.

Conclusion: Universal conductance fluctuations can be used as a tomographic probe of phase-coherent transport pathways in semiconductor nanostructures.

Abstract: Understanding the spatial distribution of carriers is important for interpreting transport in nanoscale devices. Here, we apply conductance fluctuation tomography to planar selective-area-grown InAs nanowires in both normal-normal and normal-superconductor device geometries. By tracking the evolution of conductance-interference features as a function of magnetic-field strength and orientation, we extract information about the geometry of phase-coherent transport pathways. Using theory to distinguish between bulk-dominated transport, coherent near-surface transport across facets, and transport confined to individual facets. The measurements are consistent with transport dominated by a near-surface accumulation layer in InAs. Devices with normal contacts show behavior consistent with coherent transport across the nanowire apex, whereas hybrid normal-superconductor devices exhibit signatures of more facet-dependent transport. These results demonstrate how universal conductance fluctuations can be used as a tomographic probe of phase-coherent transport pathways in semiconductor nanostructures.

</details>


### [43] [Universality of dimensional crossovers in topological insulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23074)
*Lumen Eek,Zeb Osseweijer,Cristiane Morais Smith*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper investigates dimensional crossovers in 3D topological insulators under geometric confinement, revealing that reducing system size leads to a non-monotonic sequence of topological phase transitions (3D → 2D quantum spin Hall → 1D end states) that is highly sensitive to surface termination. Surprisingly, 2D and 1D topological phases can emerge even when the 3D bulk is topologically trivial, suggesting a universal framework for topology under dimensional reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To correct the incomplete picture that thin films of 3D topological insulators simply crossover to a 2D quantum spin Hall phase via surface state hybridization, by fully accounting for bulk confinement effects and boundary termination.

Method: Minimal tight-binding lattice models of 3D topological insulators under geometric confinement, with systematic analysis of system size reduction, surface termination, and topological phase transitions.

Result: (1) A strongly non-monotonic dependence of topology on thickness and microscopic parameters. (2) A cascade of dimensional reduction: 3D TI → 2D QSH → 1D phase with end states of Kramers pairs protected by inversion symmetry. (3) 2D and 1D topological phases can emerge even when the corresponding 3D bulk is topologically trivial.

Conclusion: The findings reveal unexpected universality in 3D-to-2D and 2D-to-1D crossover phase diagrams, pointing toward a unified framework for understanding topology under dimensional reduction.

Abstract: We investigate dimensional crossovers in minimal tight-binding models of three-dimensional (3D) topological insulators subject to geometric confinement. While thin films are commonly understood to host a crossover from a 3D strong topological insulator to a two-dimensional (2D) quantum spin Hall phase via hybridization of surface states, we demonstrate that this picture is incomplete once bulk confinement effects and boundary termination are fully taken into account. Using lattice models, we show that reducing the system size induces a strongly non-monotonic dependence of the topology on thickness and microscopic parameters, leading to a sequence of topological phase transitions that is highly sensitive to surface termination. In particular, we find a cascade of dimensional reduction from a 3D topological insulator to a 2D quantum spin Hall phase and ultimately to a one-dimensional phase consisting of end states of Kramers pairs protected by inversion symmetry. Remarkably, we show that both the 2D and 1D topological phases can emerge even when the corresponding 3D bulk phase is topologically trivial. Our results reveal an unexpected universality in the phase diagrams of 3D-to-2D and 2D-to-1D crossovers, pointing toward a unified framework for topology under dimensional reduction.

</details>


### [44] [Fingerprints of an Imbert-Fedorov-like effect in the tunneling transmission of Rarita-Schwinger semi-metals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23095)
*Otman Bouladiane,Ahmed Jellal,Hocine Bahlouli,A. Al Luhaibi,Michael Vogl*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper theoretically studies tunneling through a square barrier in a rotationally symmetric Rarita-Schwinger semi-metal, discovering an Imbert-Fedorov-like effect that causes asymmetric transmission due to phase interference between spin-1/2 and spin-3/2 channels, not from band anisotropy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the origin of asymmetric tunneling in multiband systems and demonstrate that such asymmetry can arise from internal phase structures of multichannel scattering states rather than explicit band anisotropy, thereby identifying an analog of the Imbert-Fedorov effect in transport.

Method: Analytical derivation of transmission amplitudes for both single-channel (spin projections m=1/2 and m=3/2) and coherent mixed-incidence cases, comparing transmission symmetry properties under mirror transformation of incident angle.

Result: (1) High-energy tunneling shows bias towards spin-3/2 contributions. (2) Single-channel transmission remains symmetric under mirror transformation. (3) Coherent superposition exhibits directional asymmetry T(k_y)≠T(-k_y). (4) Asymmetry arises from phase differences between the two spin projection sectors interfering coherently, not from dispersion anisotropy.

Conclusion: Asymmetric tunneling can occur in idealized isotropic multiband systems due to interface-induced phase differences between internal wave components, providing an analog of the Imbert-Fedorov effect. This suggests asymmetric transport should not be automatically attributed solely to band anisotropy.

Abstract: We study tunneling through a square barrier in a rotationally symmetric Rarita-Schwinger semi-metal and identify fingerprints of an Imbert-Fedorov-like effect in the tunneling transmission. The problem is intrinsically multichannel at all energies because there exist two propagating sectors with spin projections $m=1/2$ and $m=3/2$. We derive the transmission amplitudes analytically and compare the single-channel and coherent mixed-incidence cases in the two channels. Interestingly, we observe that tunneling at high energy shows a bias towards scattering into spin projection $3/2$ contributions. For single-channel injection, we find that the transmission remains symmetric under a mirror transformation of the incident angle. In contrast, for the coherent superposition, we find a directional asymmetry in the transmission probability $T(k_y)\neq T(-k_y)$. Importantly, this effect does not originate from an anisotropy of the dispersion. Instead, it arises from the phase structure of the multicomponent scattering states. The two spin projection sectors exhibit different scattering and barrier-propagation phases, which enter as interference terms when both channels appear as a coherent superposition. This interference term is identified as the cause of the broken mirror symmetry. We therefore discover an analog of the Imbert-Fedorov effect, at the level of interface-induced phase differences between internal wave components. Our result demonstrates that asymmetric tunneling can already occur in transport experiments, even in an idealized, isotropic multiband system, and should therefore not be automatically attributed solely to explicit band anisotropy.

</details>


### [45] [Reactive Force Field for P/Sn/I System: Atomistic Insight into the Early Stage of Black Phosphorus and Phosphorene Synthesis Process](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23328)
*Djuric Brice Talonpa Tchoffo,Ismail Benabdallah,Petr Neugebauer,Anouar Belhboub,Abdelouahad El Fatimy*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This study develops a ReaxFF-based reactive force field for the PSnI system and uses molecular dynamics simulations to investigate the atomistic mechanisms of phosphorus transport and pre-nucleation processes in black phosphorus/phosphorene chemical vapor deposition synthesis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The atomistic mechanisms underlying phosphorus transport and early-stage processes in chemical vapor deposition synthesis of phosphorene and its allotropes are not well understood and are difficult to probe experimentally.

Method: A reactive force field (ReaxFF) for the PSnI system was developed, trained on a comprehensive quantum mechanical dataset covering bond dissociation, angle/torsion profiles, tin condensed phase equation of state, and cluster formation energies. Molecular dynamics simulations were then used to explore the pre-nucleation phase of BP-phosphorene growth.

Result: Iodine and density together control phosphorus recombination. Low density yields atomic P with minimal clustering. Adding I₂ increases P-P recombination, forms PxIy motifs and transient SnxPyIz compounds. Higher density favors larger Px clusters and ternary SnxPyIz compounds. At highest density, Hittorf-like P structures and BP-like hexagons stabilized by iodine form at cluster edges, potentially acting as nucleation seeds.

Conclusion: The study provides an atomistic view of transport and early-stage steps in BP synthesis and offers a practical reactive model for studying growth conditions and additive effects in BP-phosphorene vapor synthesis.

Abstract: Black phosphorus and its two dimensional counterpart, phosphorene, are typically synthesized through chemical vapor transport using Sn and I2 additives. Chemical vapor deposition synthesis of phosphorene and allotropes is still yet not well understood. Investigating the atomistic mechanisms underlying phosphorus transport and early stage processes is difficult experimentally. In this study, a reactive force field for the PSnI system was developed and applied using ReaxFF based molecular dynamics to explore the early stage phase of the pre nucleation relevant to BP-phosphorene growth. The force field parameters were trained on a comprehensive quantum mechanical dataset covering bond dissociation, angle and torsion profiles, and tin condensed phase equation of state and cluster formation energies, showing strong agreement in both gas and condensed phases. We demonstrate that iodine and density together control phosphorus recombination. Under low density, atomic phosphorus dominates with minimal clustering. Adding I2 greatly increases P-P recombination, promotes the formation of PxIy motifs, and transient SnxPyIz compounds. Higher density systems favor the formation of larger Px clusters and support the development of ternary SnxPyIz compounds that grow by capturing transported phosphorus. At the highest density, the system produces condensed, Hittorf like phosphorus structures at the edges of SnxPyIz clusters, along with BP-like hexagons stabilized by iodine that may act as nucleation seeds. These results offer an atomistic view of transport and early stage steps in BP synthesis and provide a practical reactive model for studying growth conditions and additive effects in BP phosphorene vapor synthesis.

</details>


### [46] [Quantum Geometry Driven Finite-Momentum Exciton Fluctuations in Flat-Band Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23598)
*Yi-Chun Hung,Xiaoting Zhou,Arun Bansil*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Quantum geometry in flat bands (kagome/Lieb lattices) drives a purely quantum-geometry-driven excitonic insulator phase, where the quantum metric maps onto macroscopic free energy via the Ginzburg-Landau framework. Under strong interactions, it induces a finite-momentum superfluid density fluctuation state observable as modulated magnetization fluctuations, with pathways for realization in COFs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demonstrate that lattice-geometry-induced flat bands (kagome and Lieb lattices) can realize a purely quantum-geometry-driven excitonic insulator phase, establishing a rigorous link between flat-band quantum geometry and dynamic collective excitonic states.

Method: Theoretical framework combining Ginzburg-Landau analysis with quantum metric mapping: applying an out-of-plane Zeeman field to lift spin degeneracy, mapping electron-hole wavefunction-overlap onto the flat-band quantum metric, and analyzing the resulting macroscopic free energy, coherence length, and phase stiffness.

Result: Under strong interactions, the quantum metric induces a negative effective kinetic coefficient for the amplitude mode, softening amplitude fluctuations at finite momentum rather than destabilizing the uniform condensate, leading to a finite-momentum superfluid density fluctuation state observable as periodically modulated in-plane magnetization fluctuations.

Conclusion: Flat-band quantum geometry directly drives both the excitonic insulator and associated superfluid phases, with the quantum metric governing coherence length and phase stiffness. The FMSDF state offers observable signatures, and covalent-organic frameworks are promising platforms for experimental realization.

Abstract: Quantum geometry is instrumental in stabilizing exotic phenomena in systems ranging from topological insulators to superconductors. In dispersionless flat bands, where the kinetic energy is quenched, the quantum metric emerges as the fundamental driver of macroscopic collective phenomena. Here, we theoretically demonstrate that lattice-geometry-induced flat bands, such as those in kagome and Lieb lattices, provide a fertile platform for realizing a purely quantum-geometry-driven excitonic insulator (EI) phase. By applying an out-of-plane Zeeman field to lift spin degeneracy without spin-orbit coupling, we establish a Ginzburg-Landau framework in which the electron-hole wavefunction-overlap directly maps the flat-band quantum metric onto the macroscopic free energy. This mapping plays a key role in both the EI and the associated superfluid phases, with the coherence length and phase stiffness emerging directly from the quantum metric. Our analysis reveals that under strong interactions, the quantum metric induces a negative effective kinetic coefficient for the amplitude mode. Rather than destabilizing the uniform condensate, this softens the amplitude fluctuations at a finite momentum, giving rise to a finite-momentum superfluid density fluctuation (FMSDF) state. This state is observable as a periodically modulated magnitude of in-plane magnetization fluctuations. Our findings establish a rigorous link between flat-band quantum geometry and dynamic collective excitonic states, with promising pathways for realization in covalent-organic frameworks (COFs).

</details>

### [47] [Where Is My Physics Wrong? Localized and Identifiable Discovery of Model Discrepancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23215)
*Yifan Wang*

Main category: physics.data-an

TL;DR: LISDD is a framework that localizes model errors to specific operating regimes, identifies sparse symbolic forms for missing mechanisms, and certifies discoveries with exact finite-sample tests, significantly outperforming global-discrepancy and black-box baselines in parameter bias, localization F1, symbolic recovery, and false-discovery control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sparse-discovery and discrepancy-learning methods fit one global correction, which can spread local errors into clean regimes, bias trusted physical parameters, and lack calibrated significance for selected terms.

Method: LISDD fits known physics on an automatically detected clean regime, flags discrepant regions with a calibrated residual-energy statistic, selects local missing terms by exhaustive holdout over a candidate library, and confirms significance via a sample-split F-test. A false-discovery-rate extension handles multiple discrepant regions.

Result: Physical-parameter bias: 0.002 (LISDD) vs 0.43 (baselines); localization F1: 0.80 vs 0.44; correct symbolic form recovery with probability one; exact detection; controlled multi-region false-discovery rate.

Conclusion: LISDD provides a calibrated diagnostic tool for grey-box building-energy models when a fixed physical law silently breaks in one operating regime.

Abstract: Hybrid models combine trusted physics with data-driven correction, but a physical model is rarely wrong everywhere or in the same way. The key diagnostic question is local: where does the model fail, what missing mechanism explains the failure, and is the evidence statistically real? Existing sparse-discovery and discrepancy-learning methods usually fit one global correction, which can spread a local error into clean regimes, bias trusted physical parameters, and provide no calibrated significance for selected terms. We introduce LISDD, Localized, Identifiable Sparse Discovery of Discrepancy, a framework that localizes model error to an operating regime, identifies a sparse symbolic form for the missing mechanism, and certifies the discovery with an exact finite-sample test. LISDD fits the known physics on an automatically detected clean regime, flags discrepant regions with a calibrated residual-energy statistic, selects the local missing term by exhaustive holdout over a candidate library, and confirms significance with a sample-split $F$-test. A false-discovery-rate extension handles multiple discrepant regions with different missing mechanisms. In controlled experiments, LISDD keeps physical-parameter bias at 0.002 versus 0.43 for global-discrepancy and black-box baselines, raises localization $F_1$ from 0.44 to 0.80, recovers the correct symbolic form with probability one, attains exact detection, and controls the multi-region false-discovery rate while recovering every planted mechanism. The result is a calibrated diagnostic tool for grey-box building-energy models when a fixed physical law silently breaks in one operating regime.

</details>
