### [48] [LLM-Guided Test-Time Discovery of Quantum-Chemical Approximation Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20729)
*Masaya Hagai,Yuta Suzuki,Tomoya Murata,Shuhei Kurita,Masaki Adachi*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: LADeQ is an LLM-guided workflow that autonomously discovers, implements, and benchmarks approximation algorithms inside quantum chemistry codes at test-time, accelerating CCSD/CISD calculations while keeping correlation-energy errors within user-specified tolerances, without requiring task-specific pretraining or curated data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum chemistry simulations are computationally expensive and rely on fixed approximation schemes; existing foundation models require large-scale pretraining limiting adaptability, and agentic AI systems remain constrained by predefined tools—there is a need for adaptive, test-time discovery of approximation algorithms that can operate in sparse-data regimes at the frontier of chemical space.

Method: LADeQ uses an out-of-the-box LLM to construct candidate approximation schemes on demand, drawing techniques from spatial statistics, circuit simulation, and kernel methods; it integrates directly into existing quantum chemistry codes (CCSD/CISD solvers) without task-specific pretraining or curated data, producing transparent implementations with explicitly traceable approximation errors.

Result: LADeQ accelerates CCSD and CISD calculations while keeping correlation-energy errors within user-specified tolerances, demonstrating autonomous, objective-driven discovery of approximation algorithms inside existing electronic-structure solvers.

Conclusion: LLM-guided workflows can autonomously discover novel approximation algorithms at test-time, bridging the gap between fixed approximation schemes and adaptive computation in quantum chemistry, with implications for broader scientific computing.

Abstract: Quantum chemistry simulations underpin modern materials discovery, yet their impact is limited by steep computational cost and dependence on fixed approximation schemes. Foundation models, such as machine-learned interatomic potentials, have accelerated parts of this workflow, but their reliance on large-scale pretraining restricts adaptability at the frontier of chemical space, where methodological innovation and sparse data are the norm. Agentic AI systems can automate existing simulation pipelines, yet they remain constrained by the predefined tools and algorithms they orchestrate. In response, we introduce LADeQ, an LLM-guided workflow that discovers, implements, and benchmarks candidate approximation algorithms at test-time within existing quantum chemistry codes. Rather than selecting from a predefined repertoire, LADeQ constructs candidate approximation schemes on demand, drawing on techniques from disciplines such as spatial statistics, circuit simulation, and kernel methods that have had little prior presence in electronic-structure theory. Because it builds on an out-of-the-box language model, LADeQ requires no task-specific pretraining or curated data, and the resulting implementations are transparent and inspectable, with explicitly traceable approximation errors that enable principled control of accuracy--efficiency trade-offs. We show that LADeQ accelerates coupled cluster singles and doubles (CCSD) and configuration interaction singles and doubles (CISD) calculations while keeping correlation-energy errors within user-specified tolerances, demonstrating autonomous, objective-driven discovery of approximation algorithms inside existing electronic-structure solvers.

</details>


### [49] [Empowering Polymeric Materials Discovery by Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20753)
*Chenyao Ma,Linda Zhang,Yuheng Chen,Wei Du,Shangwen Fang,Zihao Jiang,Chuanyu Liu,Xinyu Ma,Rui Su,Gang Wang,Muyao Yu,Dong Zhong,Jie Zhu,Weibo Gong,Huan Gu,Limin Li,Chen Shen,Rui Wu,Zhenghao Wu,Kan Xu,Min Zhou,Donglin He,Xiayun Huang,Shan Jiang,Pengfei Ou,Jiayu Peng,Yuwei Zhang,Jie Zhao,Di Zhang,Piao Ma,Zhenghao Li,Hao Li*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Polymer science is transitioning from labor-intensive experimentation and fragmented modeling to autonomous discovery ecosystems, where databases, AI models, agents, and automated labs form self-improving feedback loops that integrate molecular design, processing, validation, and translation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rational design of polymeric materials is exceptionally challenging due to complex interactions across molecular composition, chain architecture, processing history, and hierarchical structure across multiple scales. Traditional labor-intensive experimentation and fragmented modeling limit mechanistic understanding and innovation efficiency.

Method: Convergence of polymer databases, machine learning, large AI models, laboratory automation, and AI agents into interconnected discovery ecosystems that enable hypothesis generation, material design, experiment execution, and model refinement within self-improving feedback loops.

Result: A shift from isolated predictive accuracy improvements to reliable decision-making, adaptive learning, and seamless integration across computation, experimentation, and scientific reasoning.

Conclusion: Polymer science is entering an autonomous discovery era where self-improving feedback loops unify molecular design, process optimization, experimental validation, and industrial translation, establishing a more predictive, reproducible, and scalable innovation paradigm.

Abstract: Polymeric materials underpin modern technologies spanning energy storage, microelectronics, healthcare and sustainable manufacturing. Yet their rational design remains exceptionally challenging because material performance emerges from complex interactions among molecular composition, chain architecture, processing history and hierarchical structural evolution across multiple length and time scales. Consequently, polymer research has long relied on labor-intensive experimentation and fragmented modeling approaches, limiting both mechanistic understanding and innovation efficiency. Recent advances in data infrastructure, machine learning, large artificial intelligence (AI) models and laboratory automation are beginning to reshape this landscape. Rather than functioning as isolated tools, polymer databases, predictive models, AI agents and automated laboratories are increasingly converging into interconnected discovery ecosystems. As a result, the central challenge is shifting from improving predictive accuracy alone to enabling reliable decision-making, adaptive learning and seamless integration across computation, experimentation and scientific reasoning. We argue that polymer science is entering an era of autonomous discovery, in which data, simulation, reasoning and experimentation operate within self-improving feedback loops that continuously generate hypotheses, design materials, execute experiments and refine predictive models. By unifying molecular design, process optimization, experimental validation and industrial translation, such autonomous ecosystems establish a more predictive, reproducible and scalable paradigm for polymer innovation, fundamentally transforming how polymer research is conducted.

</details>


### [50] [A large-scale foundation model enables simulation-to-real adaptation for nuclear magnetic resonance-based molecular structure analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20756)
*Chen Yang,Zheng Fang,Hanyu Sun,Fanjie Xu,Hongxin Xiang,Hanyu Gao,Xiangxiang Zeng,Yuqiang Li,Xiaojian Wang,Jun Xia*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: UltraNMR is a large-scale foundation model pre-trained on 158 million simulated ¹H and ¹³C NMR spectra using domain-specific objectives, achieving state-of-the-art performance across diverse molecular structure analysis tasks on experimental data and enabling structure-aware retrieval over 94 million molecules, including successful real-world structural elucidation of two unknown natural products.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Experimental NMR datasets are scarce, limiting deep learning models to narrow, task-specific applications without broad generalization capabilities.

Method: Collected 158 million paired simulated ¹H and ¹³C NMR spectra; trained using multiple domain-specific pre-training objectives that capture both intra-spectral and inter-spectral dependencies; designed for simulation-to-real adaptation.

Result: State-of-the-art performance on multiple experimental NMR tasks, outperforming variants without simulation pre-training; constructed a spectral vector library covering 94 million molecules enabling structure-aware retrieval; successfully elucidated two unknown natural products from Chinese Pharmacopoeia.

Conclusion: Large-scale simulation pre-training effectively bridges the simulation-to-real gap, enabling robust and generalizable molecular structure analysis of real-world NMR spectra.

Abstract: Nuclear Magnetic Resonance (NMR) spectroscopy is a powerful tool for molecular structure analysis, and spectral artificial intelligence offers great potential for its rapid and automated interpretation. However, the scarcity of experimental NMR datasets has constrained deep learning in this domain to narrow, task-specific applications that lack broad generalization. Here, we introduce UltraNMR, a large-scale foundation model for NMR that leverages the intrinsic properties of NMR spectra to learn generalizable spectral representations. We collected 158 million paired simulated $^{1}$H and $^{13}$C NMR spectra to train UltraNMR, employing multiple domain-specific pre-training objectives. UltraNMR captures both intra-spectral and inter-spectral dependencies, enabling seamless simulation-to-real adaptation. We demonstrate that adapting UltraNMR to a range of molecular structure analysis tasks on experimental NMR spectra consistently yields state-of-the-art performance and clearly outperforms UltraNMR variants trained directly on downstream data without simulation pre-training. We also construct a large-scale NMR spectral vector library by encoding simulated NMR spectra using UltraNMR, covering 94 million unique molecules and enabling effective structure-aware retrieval. In real-world applications, UltraNMR facilitates the structural elucidation of two previously unknown natural products from Chinese herbal medicines recorded in the Chinese Pharmacopoeia. These results suggest that large-scale simulation pre-training can effectively bridge the simulation-to-real gap, enabling robust and generalizable molecular structure analysis of real-world NMR spectra.

</details>


### [51] [Rothe's Method for Quantum Dynamics in Atoms and Molecules with Gaussian Wavepackets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20947)
*Simon Elias Schrader,Håkon Emil Kristiansen,Aleksander P. Wozniak,Ludwik Adamowicz,Simen Kvaal,Thomas Bondo Pedersen*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This chapter reviews numerical challenges of using fully flexible Gaussian wavepackets in time-dependent variational principles and presents Rothe's method as a more stable alternative. Proof-of-concept simulations show Gaussian wavepackets achieve grid-based accuracy for electronic and rovibrational dynamics, including continuum processes like high-harmonic generation, with remarkably few basis functions, though challenges remain regarding squared Hamiltonian matrix elements and Coulomb cusps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gaussian wavepackets are attractive for simulating laser-driven quantum dynamics (bound and continuum), but their propagation within conventional time-dependent variational principles suffers from severe numerical instability, motivating the search for alternative approaches.

Method: The chapter reviews Rothe's method as an alternative to conventional time-dependent variational principles for propagating fully flexible Gaussian wavepackets, and presents proof-of-concept simulations comparing Gaussian wavepacket results against grid-based methods.

Result: Rothe's method yields numerically stable propagation of Gaussian wavepackets. Simulations demonstrate accuracy on par with grid-based methods for both electronic and rovibrational quantum dynamics, including ultrafast nonlinear continuum processes like high-harmonic generation, using remarkably few Gaussian wavepackets.

Conclusion: Gaussian wavepackets with Rothe's method offer a promising route toward efficient simulations of electronic, rovibrational, and fully coupled electron-nuclear dynamics with reduced memory demands, but practical challenges such as squared Hamiltonian matrix elements and Coulomb cusp treatment remain to be addressed.

Abstract: Capable of capturing both bound and continuum quantum dynamics, Gaussian wavepackets are highly attractive basis functions for simulating laser-driven processes in atoms and molecules. Unfortunately, fully flexible Gaussian wavepackets are exceedingly challenging to propagate in a numerically stable manner within the framework of conventional time-dependent variational principles. In this chapter, we discuss the sources of the numerical issues and review an alternative approach, Rothe's method, that offers a route to improved numerical stability. Recent proof-of-concept simulations based on Rothe's method indicate that Gaussian wavepackets provide results on par with highly accurate grid-based methods for both electronic and rovibrational quantum dynamics, including ultrafast nonlinear processes that involve the continuum such as high-harmonic generation. Remarkably few Gaussian wavepackets are needed to achieve the high accuracy of grid-based approaches, indicating that further algorithmic developments and efficient implementations may enable efficient simulations of not only electronic and rovibrational phenomena but also fully coupled electronic-nuclear quantum dynamics with significantly reduced memory demands. We also point out remaining practical challenges, including matrix elements of the squared Hamiltonian and the treatment of Coulomb cusps.

</details>


### [52] [A Unified Generative Framework for Scalable Chemical Reaction Network Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21002)
*Zechang Sun,Chenxi Hu,Kailai Lin,Jin Li,Changsu Cao,Dingshun Lv,Ji Chen,Weiluo Ren,Hung Q. Pham*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: ByteCRN is an end-to-end framework for computational chemical reaction network exploration that uses generative rectified flow for transition state modeling, achieving 10-100x acceleration over traditional workflows while pruning 70-90% of enumerated reactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computational exploration of chemical reaction networks is limited by combinatorial growth of chemical space, unreliable reaction path screening, and high cost of evaluating thermodynamic and kinetic properties.

Method: ByteCRN combines chemically informed reaction enumeration with a generative rectified flow architecture for transition state generation and reaction validation: it maps reactant-product pairs to candidate transition states and verifies connectivity via bidirectional mapping.

Result: Achieves 10-100x acceleration over traditional workflows; prunes ~70-90% of enumerated reactions; successfully discovered novel cyanoacetaldehyde pathways and modeled the challenging γ-ketohydroperoxide network.

Conclusion: ByteCRN demonstrates a practical, scalable approach to autonomous chemical exploration by replacing expensive iterative transition state search and intrinsic reaction coordinate validation steps with a unified generative strategy.

Abstract: Chemical reaction networks (CRNs) are crucial for understanding reaction mechanisms and guiding chemical synthesis, yet the computational exploration remains limited by the combinatorial growth of chemical space, the reliability of reaction path screening, and the cost of evaluating thermodynamic and kinetic properties. Here, we present ByteCRN, an end-to-end framework for computational CRN exploration that combines chemically informed reaction enumeration with generative transition state modeling. A key component of our framework is a generative rectified flow architecture for both transition state generation and reaction validation, where it maps reactant-product pairs to candidate transition state structures and verifies connectivity by mapping back to reactants and products. This unified generative strategy replaces the most expensive steps of conventional computational workflows, namely iterative transition state search and intrinsic reaction coordinate validation, within a complete CRN construction pipeline. ByteCRN delivers a 10--100-fold acceleration over traditional workflows while maintaining high predictive fidelity for individual reactions. At the network scale, it effectively prunes $\sim$70-90% of the enumerated reactions, streamlining the exploration of complex reaction space. Its utility is illustrated through the discovery of novel pathways involving cyanoacetaldehyde and the successful modeling of the challenging $γ$-ketohydroperoxide network, demonstrating a practical, scalable approach to autonomous chemical exploration.

</details>


### [53] [Generalized Mpemba effect in diffusion-controlled spin-dependent delayed fluorescence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21003)
*Kazuhiko Seki*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates a generalized Mpemba effect in magnetic-field-dependent delayed fluorescence of triplet-fusion systems, where delayed-fluorescence trajectories at different magnetic fields cross due to spin-selective kinetics and geminate recombination, with a kinetic criterion linking intermediate decay rates and long-time power-law amplitudes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To show that magnetic-field-dependent delayed fluorescence in triplet-fusion systems can exhibit a generalized Mpemba effect (where a state initially farther from equilibrium relaxes faster than a closer one), driven by external control of spin-selective kinetics.

Method: A diffusion-controlled geminate-recombination extension of the Johnson–Merrifield model is used; delayed-fluorescence trajectories at different magnetic fields are analyzed; a compact kinetic criterion for trajectory crossing is derived based on competition between intermediate-time decay rates and long-time power-law amplitudes.

Result: Delayed-fluorescence trajectories obtained at different magnetic fields cross when geminate fusion is effective. The generalized Mpemba effect arises from redistribution of transient relaxation pathways associated with geminate recombination, while bulk diffusion-controlled processes only contribute to final relaxation.

Conclusion: The generalized Mpemba effect can emerge in spin-dependent photokinetics governed by diffusion-controlled geminate recombination and non-Markovian kinetics, linking anomalous relaxation to modern formulations of the Mpemba effect.

Abstract: The Mpemba effect is commonly associated with anomalous thermal relaxation, in which a system prepared at a higher temperature reaches equilibrium faster than one prepared at a lower temperature. In modern formulations, however, its defining feature is broader: a state initially farther from equilibrium can relax faster than a closer one, or, equivalently, relaxation trajectories can cross.
  Here, we show that magnetic-field-dependent delayed fluorescence in triplet-fusion systems realizes a generalized Mpemba effect driven by external control of spin-selective kinetics. Using a diffusion-controlled geminate-recombination extension of the Johnson--Merrifield model, we demonstrate that delayed-fluorescence trajectories obtained at different magnetic fields cross when geminate fusion is effective. We further derive a compact kinetic criterion for such crossing in terms of the competition between intermediate-time decay rates and long-time power-law amplitudes. This criterion captures the interplay between effective fast and slow relaxation contributions. Within this kinetic framework, the final state is independent of the external control parameter and is defined as the fully relaxed state in which no separated triplet population remains. The generalized Mpemba effect therefore arises from the redistribution of transient relaxation pathways associated with geminate recombination, whereas bulk diffusion-controlled processes contribute only to the final relaxation.
  These results link anomalous relaxation in spin-dependent photokinetics to modern formulations of the generalized Mpemba effect and show how trajectory crossing can emerge in systems governed by diffusion-controlled geminate recombination and non-Markovian kinetics.

</details>


### [54] [QBioFusion-QSAR: Morgan-Anchored Quantum Multiple Kernel Learning for Small-Data Ligand Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21213)
*Azadeh Alavi,Fatemeh Kouchmeshki,Muhammad Usman,Jessica Holien*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: QBioFusion-QSAR uses quantum multiple kernel learning (QMKL) to augment Morgan/Tanimoto fingerprint models with quantum fidelity kernels. On a 54-molecule benchmark, it modestly improves accuracy (0.815→0.833) and MCC (0.613→0.645), particularly for activity-cliff molecules. However, over multiple random partitionings, QMKL does not significantly outperform Morgan/Tanimoto, indicating limited and inconsistent gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small QSAR studies struggle when structurally similar molecules have different activity labels (activity cliffs). The paper investigates whether quantum kernels can add useful similarity information that classical fingerprint models miss.

Method: Quantum multiple kernel learning (QMKL) combining a Morgan/Tanimoto kernel with a quantum fidelity kernel from fold-local components (RDKit, Mordred, Deep-PK features), plus linear/RBF descriptor kernels as controls. Audited via matched-regularization and bootstrap intervals.

Result: On PsychLight-A (54 molecules), QMKL improved accuracy from 0.815→0.833 and MCC from 0.613→0.645. Activity-cliff MCC rose from 0.07→0.22. However, over ten random partitionings, mean MCC did not exceed Morgan/Tanimoto, and bootstrap intervals spanned zero.

Conclusion: QBioFusion-QSAR provides an auditable QMKL framework that can attribute quantum kernel contributions locally, but the improvements are modest and not statistically significant over repeated evaluations.

Abstract: Small quantitative structure-activity relationship (QSAR) studies are difficult when close molecular analogues have different activity labels. This paper asks whether a quantum kernel can add similarity information to a Morgan/Tanimoto fingerprint model, and which molecules account for the change. QBioFusion-QSAR uses quantum multiple kernel learning (QMKL): a support vector machine combines a Morgan/Tanimoto kernel with a quantum fidelity kernel constructed from fold-local components derived from RDKit and Mordred descriptors and Deep-PK features. Linear and radial basis function descriptor kernels are included as classical controls. On the 54-molecule PsychLight-A benchmark, Morgan/Tanimoto was the strongest single representation. In the primary stratified five-fold evaluation, QMKL increased accuracy from 0.815 to 0.833 and Matthews correlation coefficient (MCC) from 0.613 to 0.645. Matched-regularization auditing attributed the change to N-Me-5-HT and N-Me-tryptamine changing from false-negative to true-positive predictions; activity-cliff subset MCC increased from 0.07 to 0.22. Repeating the five-fold protocol over ten random partitionings showed that learned QMKL did not exceed Morgan/Tanimoto on mean MCC; paired held-out bootstrap intervals for the matched comparison also span zero. These results support QBioFusion-QSAR as an auditable QMKL framework for identifying localized residual quantum-kernel contributions in small-data, activity-cliff-aware ligand classification.

</details>


### [55] [Property-Specific Molecular Representations via Feature-Space Transfer Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21311)
*Ali Banjafar,Guido Falk von Rudorff*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Proposes a method to sub-select and re-weight molecular representations adapted to specific properties, reducing dimensionality by 72% median (36-98%) while retaining accuracy, and improving data efficiency by up to 19% training data for dipole moments, using cheap semi-empirical data instead of high-quality labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Molecular representations are typically property-agnostic and over-complete, where different parts of the representation are relevant for different physical properties. The goal is to adapt representations to specific properties to make them shorter and more accurate.

Method: A feature selection and re-weighting method that sub-selects representation components based on property relevance, using cheap semi-empirical surrogate data rather than high-quality labeled data. Evaluated on four properties (total energy, heat capacity, dipole moment, polarizability) across three representations (cMBDF, FCHL19, MACE-MP-0 descriptors) and two datasets (QM9, VQM24).

Result: Median dimensionality reduction of 72% (range 36-98%) while maintaining accuracy. For dipole moments, tuning for accuracy achieved the same accuracy with only 19% of the training data.

Conclusion: The approach yields data-driven interpretations of feature importance, lossless compact representations, and increased data efficiency using only expendable surrogate data.

Abstract: In many machine learning applications, molecules need to be transformed into representations, i.e. mathematical objects. Those representations are typically considered to be property-agnostic and as such are expected to be over-complete: for different physical properties, different parts or the representation may be relevant. In this work, we propose a method to sub-select and re-weight the representation by adapting it to the property in question. We find that in most cases this makes representations shorter and more accurate at the same time. The feature selection itself uses cheap semi-empirical data instead of high-quality labels. We study four properties (total energy, heat capacity, dipole moment, and polarizability) for three representations (cMBDF, FCHL19, and MACE-MP-0 descriptors) on two datasets (QM9 and VQM24). We can reduce the number of dimensions of a representation in the median by 72\,\% (range 36-98\,\%) while retaining the accuracy. Tuning for accuracy instead we can increase the learning efficiency for dipole moments such that the same accuracy can be reached with 19\,\% of the training data. Our approach yields data-driven interpretations of feature importance, lossless compact representations, and increased data efficiency, requiring only expendable surrogate data.

</details>


### [56] [Controlling the phase behaviour of ultraconfined water via bilayer graphene stacking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21490)
*Yixuan Pu,Benjamin X. Shi,Pavan Ravindra,Chris Pickard,Angelos Michaelides,Venkat Kapil*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Using machine learning interatomic potentials, this study shows that a lateral shift of just 1.4 Å in bilayer graphene stacking (AA vs AB) dramatically alters the phase behavior, melting temperature, proton transfer, and superionic onset of nanoconfined water, demonstrating that confinement topology alone is insufficient to predict nanoconfined water properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge the prevailing assumption that geometric confinement and reduced dimensionality alone determine nanoconfined water behavior, and to investigate whether subtle atomistic variations in confining walls (beyond topology) can qualitatively reshape water's physical and chemical properties.

Method: Machine learning interatomic potentials with first-principles accuracy were used to compute the density-temperature phase diagram of water confined within bilayer graphene nanocapillaries, comparing AA and AB stacking arrangements differing by a 1.4 Å lateral shift.

Result: AA stacking stabilizes different ice polymorphs, increases melting temperature by >100 K, enhances proton transfer, and alters superionic onset compared to AB stacking. These effects arise from stacking-induced changes in the hydrogen-bond network due to modifications in the lateral free energy landscape and neighboring O-O separations.

Conclusion: Even subtle atomistic variations (1.4 Å shift) in confining walls can qualitatively reshape nanoconfined water behavior, with implications for interpreting and controlling fluids under angstrom-scale confinement.

Abstract: Water confined within nanoscale capillaries exhibits phase behaviour and transport properties that differ substantially from bulk, and these effects are commonly interpreted as consequences of geometric confinement and reduced dimensionality. Here we show that confinement topology alone is insufficient to predict the behaviour of nanoconfined water. Using machine learning interatomic potentials with first-principles accuracy, we compute the density-temperature phase diagram of water confined within bilayer graphene nanocapillaries and compare AA and AB stacking arrangements, which differ only by a lateral shift of 1.4 Å. Despite this minimal structural change, AA stacking can stabilise different ice polymorphs, can increase the melting temperature by more than 100 K, can enhance proton transfer, and alters the onset of superionic behaviour relative to AB stacking. We trace these effects to stacking-induced changes in the hydrogen-bond network associated with modifications to the lateral free energy landscape and neighbouring O-O separations. Our results demonstrate that even subtle atomistic variations in the confining walls can qualitatively reshape the physical and chemical behaviour of nanoconfined water, with implications for the interpretation and control of fluids under angstrom-scale confinement.

</details>


### [57] [Strictly localized orbitals from spatial partitioning with the discontinuous Galerkin method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21746)
*César Feniou,Julien Toulouse*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a rigorous electronic-structure theory using strictly localized orbitals with discontinuities at domain interfaces, enabled by the interior-penalty discontinuous Galerkin method, demonstrating variational calculations on 1D diatomic models that agree with conventional methods and yield chemically intuitive valence-bond-like wave functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a rigorous theory for strictly localized orbitals that remain well-defined in the complete basis-set limit, enabling spatially partitioned electronic-structure calculations without the basis-set completeness issues that plague traditional localized orbital approaches.

Method: Strictly localized orbitals are defined via spatial domain partitioning of the one-electron Hilbert space, with discontinuities at domain interfaces handled by the interior-penalty discontinuous Galerkin (IPDG) method for variational calculations.

Result: Numerical tests on 1D diatomic model systems show variational calculations using strictly localized orbitals achieve good agreement with conventional calculations, and the orbitals naturally produce chemically intuitive valence-bond-like representations.

Conclusion: Strictly localized orbitals with controlled discontinuities are a viable basis for variational electronic-structure calculations, bridging localized orbital methods and chemically transparent wave function representations.

Abstract: We present a rigorous electronic-structure theory of strictly localized orbitals associated with a spatial partition of the one-electron Hilbert space that remains well defined in the complete basis-set limit. Each strictly localized orbital is supported on a spatial domain and may be discontinuous at domain interfaces. Using the interior-penalty discontinuous Galerkin method, these strictly localized orbitals can be employed in variational electronic-structure calculations despite their discontinuities at domain interfaces. As a proof of concept, we present numerical illustrations on one-dimensional diatomic model systems. They show that variational calculations can be carried out in a basis of strictly localized orbitals while maintaining good agreement with conventional calculations. Moreover, these strictly localized orbitals naturally lead to chemically intuitive representations of many-electron wave functions in the spirit of valence-bond theory.

</details>


### [58] [The impedance of a charged flat-plate electric double-layer capacitor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21980)
*Adrian L. Usler,David Fertig,Mathijs Janssen*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper uses FEM simulations of modified PNP equations to study EDL capacitor impedance, revealing that slanted lines in Nyquist plots arise from ambipolar salt diffusion due to either asymmetric bias or unequal diffusion coefficients. The authors disprove prior interpretations of the slanted line's width and slope, and instead propose two equivalent circuits (Warburg short for D-≠D+, Warburg open for Ubias≠0) with quantitative relations linking Warburg prefactors to ambipolar diffusion, chemical capacitance, and differential charge efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To clarify the physical origin of slanted lines in EDL capacitor impedance spectra and correct prior misinterpretations regarding the resistance and time-scale interpretations of these features.

Method: Finite Element Method simulations of modified Poisson–Nernst–Planck equations for a flat-plate EDL capacitor, combined with equivalent circuit analysis.

Result: Slanted lines arise from ambipolar salt diffusion under bias or unequal diffusion coefficients. Prior claims about R_sl representing EDL resistance and k_sl measuring τ_diff/τ_c are disproven. Two equivalent circuits are validated: Warburg short (antisymmetric perturbations, D-≠D+) and Warburg open (symmetric perturbations, Ubias≠0).

Conclusion: Provides a theoretical framework linking Warburg prefactors to ambipolar diffusion, chemical capacitance, and differential charge efficiency, explaining saturation of slanted-line width at large biases, its vanishing at high ion packing fractions, and how finite ion count limits overall capacitance.

Abstract: We calculate the impedance of a flat-plate electric double-layer (EDL) capacitor by means of Finite Element Method simulations of modified Poisson--Nernst--Planck equations. In Nyquist representation, the impedance spectra show a slanted line at intermediate frequencies if the capacitor is biased by a voltage, $U_\mathrm{bias}\neq 0$, or if the cation and anion diffusion coefficients differ, $D_-\neq D_+$. By inspecting the concentration perturbations in the relevant frequency range, we confirm that the slanted line is in both cases related to ambipolar salt diffusion. On the basis of our impedance data, we disprove two previously made claims: 1) that the width $R_\mathrm{sl}$ of the slanted-line region represents an EDL resistance; and 2) that the slope $k_\mathrm{sl}$ of the slanted line is a measure of the ratio of the diffusion and charging time scales, $τ_\mathrm{diff}/τ_\mathrm{c}$. For the quantitative analysis of flat-electrode EDL capacitor impedance, we propose instead two equivalent circuits, for the cases $D_-\neq D_+$ and $U_\mathrm{bias}\neq 0$. These two cases give rise to antisymmetric and symmetric salt perturbations, which are best described by a Warburg short and Warburg open element, respectively. From our circuit analysis, we obtain quantitative relations that link the Warburg prefactors to the ambipolar diffusion coefficient, the chemical capacitance of the bulk electrolyte, and the differential charge efficiency of the EDLs. We thus provide a theoretical framework that explains why the width of the slanted line saturates at large biases, why it vanishes for large ion packing fractions, and how the system's overall capacitance is limited by the finite amount of ions in a closed system.

</details>


### [59] [Preparation and control of electronic wave packets in neutral molecules via attosecond x-ray processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22102)
*Emanuele Rossi,Stasis Chuchurka,Kaushik D. Nanda,Anna I. Krylov,Nina Rohringer,Robin Santra*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: A perturbative framework combining EOM-CC and second-order ISXRS theory is developed to simulate attosecond x-ray-induced electronic wave packet dynamics in neutral molecules (OCS and oxazole), revealing that core-excited states dominate early dynamics while valence-excited states govern longer timescales, with pulse polarization and atom-specific ISXRS controlling charge migration symmetry and initial localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a computationally tractable, first-principles framework for simulating coherent electronic dynamics initiated by attosecond x-ray pulses in neutral molecules, capturing both core-excited and valence-excited contributions via ISXRS.

Method: Perturbative expansion of wave packet dynamics; EOM-CC for electronic structure; compact sum-over-states representation for second-order ISXRS; time-dependent difference electron density decomposition; applied to OCS and oxazole molecules.

Result: Core-excited states dominate initial dynamics; valence-excited states dominate longer timescales; pulse polarization controls state symmetry; ISXRS atom-specificity determines initial wave packet localization and subsequent charge migration trajectory.

Conclusion: The framework successfully captures multi-timescale charge migration in neutral molecules, with ISXRS providing atom-specific initiation and polarization offering symmetry control, enabling targeted attochemistry.

Abstract: We present a perturbative framework for computing the dynamics of an electronic wave packet launched by an attosecond x-ray pulse in a neutral molecule. The x-ray pulse excites the molecule via both x-ray absorption and Impulsive Stimulated X-ray Raman Scattering (ISXRS), coherently populating both the core-excited states and valence-excited states. We describe the electronic structure within the Equation-of-Motion Coupled-Cluster framework, adopting a compact representation of the sum over states expression characterizing the second-order perturbative description of ISXRS. We study the coherent electronic dynamics in OCS and oxazole utilizing the time-dependent difference electron density, decomposing it in terms of its perturbative components. While the core-excited components dominate the initial stages of the dynamics, the valence-excited components dominate the electronic dynamics on a longer time scale. The pulse polarization controls the symmetry of the states included in the wave packet. We show how the spatial symmetry of the states plays a role in shaping the spatial properties of charge migration. The atom-specificity of the ISXRS process translates directly into the initial localization of the wave packet. This determines the starting point of charge migration, shaping its subsequent evolution.

</details>


### [60] [Reaction-Network-Level Discovery of Ammonia Synthesis Catalysts via Ten-Million-Scale Generative Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22926)
*Ruili Li,Rui Qi,Shuoqi Zhang,Qingli Tang,Qingqing Mao,Ritankar Das,Beien Zhu,Yi Gao*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: A ten-million-scale generative framework using adsorbate-specific Transformers and machine learning potentials explores reaction-network-level catalyst discovery for ammonia synthesis, identifying 279 candidate materials by mapping multi-intermediate compatibility across four critical surface intermediates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Catalyst discovery for ammonia synthesis is inherently a reaction-network challenge governed by multi-intermediate compatibility across competing pathways, but navigating ultra-large chemical spaces under such constraints is a bottleneck for conventional screening workflows.

Method: Coupling adsorbate-specific generative Transformers with high-throughput machine learning potentials, generating ~15 million configurations per adsorbate (N*, NH*, NNH*, HNNH*), followed by structural compression and ML-potential predictions to identify compatible materials.

Result: Identified 279 highly potential target materials from ~60 million total configurations. Recovered traditional Fe- and Ru-based motifs and uncovered new families. Fe-V lowers N2 dissociation barrier (dissociative pathway); Al-Pd-Zr stabilizes associative intermediates (associative pathway).

Conclusion: Multi-intermediate reaction-network compatibility is a robust criterion for discovering advanced ammonia synthesis catalysts from multi-million generative chemical spaces.

Abstract: Catalyst discovery for ammonia synthesis is inherently a reaction-network challenge because catalytic performance is governed not by a single adsorbed intermediate, but by a surface's orchestrated compatibility with multiple distinct intermediates across competing dissociative and associative pathways. However, navigating ultra-large chemical spaces under such multi-intermediate constraints remains a formidable bottleneck for conventional screening workflows. Here, we report a reaction-network-level catalyst discovery framework driven by ten-million-scale generative exploration. By coupling adsorbate-specific generative Transformers with high-throughput machine learning potentials, we systematically map the structure-property landscapes of four critical intermediates (N*, NH*, NNH*, and HNNH*). Scale-dependent overlap analysis shows that the full four-intermediate compatibility space remains strongly under-sampled at conventional 105-106 generative scales, emerging exclusively under ten-million-scale exploration. By generating approximately 15 million configurations per adsorbate, followed by structural compression and machine-learning-potential predictions, we identified 279 highly potential target materials. This sparse compatibility space successfully recovers traditional Fe- and Ru-based motifs while uncovering previously unexplored catalyst families. Representative DFT calculations validate pathway-dependent mechanisms: Fe-V emerges as a dissociative-pathway lead by significantly lowering the initial N2 dissociation barrier, whereas Al-Pd-Zr efficiently stabilizes associative intermediates as an associative-pathway lead. These findings establish multi-intermediate reaction-network compatibility as a robust criterion for discovering advanced catalysts from multi-million generative chemical spaces.

</details>


### [61] [Local and Charge-Transfer Excitation of Pentacene-Buckminsterfullerene complexes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23084)
*Ala Aldin M. Hani M. Darghouth,Omar A. Shareef,Firas A. AL-Lolage,Mark E. Casida*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper compares CAM-B3LYP, B3LYP, and DFTB methods for calculating charge transfer and local excited states in four pentacene-buckminsterfullerene organic solar cell models with different interface configurations, finding CAM-B3LYP (without optimally tuned parameters) yields charge transfer energies close to the more expensive OPT-wB97XD method, while B3LYP and DFTB underestimate them by about 1 eV.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate the accuracy of different computational methods (CAM-B3LYP, B3LYP, DFTB) for describing charge transfer and excited states in pentacene-buckminsterfullerene organic solar cells with varying donor-acceptor interface configurations.

Method: Four pentacene-C60 interface models were studied using time-dependent density functional theory (TD-DFT) with three functionals: CAM-B3LYP (long-range separated hybrid with Coulomb-attenuating method), B3LYP (standard hybrid), and DFTB (density functional tight binding). Results were benchmarked against the optimally tuned range-separated hybrid functional OPT-wB97XD with many-body dispersion correction.

Result: CAM-B3LYP without optimally tuned parameters produces charge transfer state energies very close to OPT-wB97XD. B3LYP and DFTB both underestimate charge transfer energies by approximately 1 eV across all four interface models.

Conclusion: CAM-B3LYP is a computationally efficient and sufficiently accurate alternative to optimally tuned range-separated hybrids for studying charge transfer in pentacene-buckminsterfullerene systems, while B3LYP and DFTB are inadequate for quantitative charge transfer energy calculations in these organic solar cell models.

Abstract: The charge transfer state, the local excited state on pentacene, and the local excited state on buckminsterfullerene have been studied for four models of the pentacene-buckminsterfullerene organic solar cell. These models have different interface configurations between the donor and the acceptor. The study has been done by using different functionals and time-dependent density functional theory-namely, the long range-separated hybrid with coulomb-attenuating method approach (CAM-B3LYP functional), the popular B3LYP (Becke, three-parameter, Lee- Yang-Parr) exchange-correlation functional, and the density functional tight binding (DFTB) method. The charge transfer state energy obtained by using CAM-B3LYP without optimally tuned range-separated hybrid parameters are very close to those obtained by using a many-body dispersion-corrected, optimally tuned range-separated hybrid functional (OPT-wB97XD). Both the B3LYP functional and the DFTB method fail to describe correctly the charge transfer energy for all models. Each of them is underestimating around 1 eV compared with range-separated hybrid functional.

</details>


### [62] [Reduced-Alphabet QUBO/Ising Formulation for Constraint-Driven Cyclic Peptide Sequence Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23253)
*Yan Zhou,Yuqi Wang,Xin Li*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a reduced-alphabet QUBO/Ising formulation for constraint-driven cyclic peptide sequence design, balancing local residue preferences with ring-forming chemistry, topology, target compatibility, and developability constraints using binary residue-class assignment variables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cyclic peptide design requires balancing multiple competing constraints including local residue preferences, ring-forming chemistry, residue spacing, topology, target compatibility, and developability, motivating a unified mathematical framework to handle these simultaneously.

Method: A QUBO/Ising formulation is developed where amino acids are grouped into physicochemical/interaction-based residue classes, represented by binary assignment variables. The objective combines one-hot sequence validity, cyclization constraints, target-compatibility terms, motif/composition rules, and developability proxies. An eight-class alphabet based on MJ interaction-profile clustering is used as the default representation.

Result: The framework can represent head-to-tail, disulfide-bridged, stapled, and bicyclic peptide designs by modifying constraint terms. The solver-agnostic QUBO/Ising objective can be explored using classical or quantum-compatible binary optimization procedures, producing low-energy residue-class sequences for downstream validation.

Conclusion: The model serves as an early-stage search-space reduction and prioritization layer, generating residue-class sequences rather than final candidates, which require amino-acid decoding, cyclization-aware construction, and downstream structural/experimental validation.

Abstract: Cyclic peptide design requires balancing local residue preferences with constraints from ring-forming chemistry, residue spacing, topology, target compatibility, and developability. Here, we present a reduced-alphabet quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO)/Ising formulation for constraint-driven cyclic peptide sequence design. Amino acids are grouped into physicochemical or interaction-based residue classes, and peptide positions are represented by binary residue-class assignment variables. The objective combines one-hot sequence validity, cyclization constraints, optional target-compatibility terms, motif and composition rules, and coarse developability proxies. By modifying the relevant constraint terms, the same framework can represent head-to-tail, disulfide-bridged, stapled, and bicyclic peptide designs. A resource-aware eight-class alphabet motivated by MJ interaction-profile clustering is used as a default representation to balance coarse interaction-pattern preservation with encoding cost. The resulting QUBO/Ising objective is solver-agnostic and can be explored using classical or quantum-compatible binary optimization procedures. The model is intended as an early-stage search-space reduction and prioritization layer: it produces low-energy residue-class sequences rather than final molecular candidates, which require amino-acid decoding, cyclization-aware construction, and downstream structural or experimental validation.

</details>


### [63] [Impurity-Preserved Density Matrix Embedding Theory for Local Electronic Excitations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23379)
*Teng Zhang,Ze-Wei Li,Zhe-Bin Guan,Hong Jiang*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new local-orbital-based partitioning scheme for density matrix embedding theory (DMET) that preserves the impurity space of atomic orbitals while maintaining computational efficiency, achieving accuracy close to AO-DMET for excited states of lanthanide complexes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DMET methods struggle with accurate excited-state calculations for strongly correlated systems like lanthanide complexes due to significant errors relative to all-electron results; the goal is to improve accuracy while retaining computational efficiency.

Method: A new LO-based partitioning scheme that fully preserves the impurity space spanned by corresponding AOs, inspired by the AO-DMET framework but retaining orthogonal partition and computational efficiency.

Result: Demonstrated through excitation energy calculations for several representative lanthanide complexes; the method achieves accuracy closely matching AO-DMET.

Conclusion: Establishes an efficient and accurate partitioning scheme for describing excited states in strongly correlated systems within the DMET framework.

Abstract: Density matrix embedding theory (DMET), which is usually based on a Schmidt decomposition of Slater determinants by partitioning the full system into impurity and environment in terms of local orthogonal orbitals (LOs), has demonstrated considerable promise in electronic structure studies because it enables the extraction of local properties using a high-level solver within an embedded impurity subsystem with greatly reduced degrees of freedom, thereby achieving a balance between accuracy and computational cost. However, its application to excited states of strongly correlated systems, such as lanthanide complexes, remains challenging because the errors relative to all-electron results can still be significant. Motivated by the success of the previously developed atomic orbitals (AOs) based DMET framework (Ai, Li, and Jiang, Phys. Rev. Lett. 2025, 135, 026502.), termed AO-DMET, which attains improved accuracy by constructing the embedded subspace based on a non-orthogonal decomposition of the Slater determinant in terms of AOs, we propose a new LO-based partitioning scheme that fully preserves the impurity space spanned by corresponding AOs and can achieve accuracy closely matching that of AO-DMET while retaining the orthogonal partition and its associated computational efficiency. The performance of the proposed method is demonstrated through excitation energy calculations for several representative lanthanide complexes. These results establish an efficient and accurate partitioning scheme for describing excited states in strongly correlated systems within the DMET framework.

</details>
