### [64] [Fully Scalable Polarization-Reconfigurable S/X-Band Shared-Aperture Phased Array for Ultra-Low Axial-Ratio Scanning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20973)
*Mohamed Räsänen,Juha Ala-Laurinaho,Samuel de Jésus Ndimubandi,Eugenio Cano Muñoz,Xiaoliang Sun Wang,Alfonso Tomás Muriel-Barrado,Andrea Di Giovanni,Raffaele Di Bari,Marco Alessandrini,José Manuel Fernández González,Ville Viikari*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: A modular S-/X-band shared-aperture phased-array antenna for SATCOM ground-station reception is presented, using a repeatable unit cell for independent dual-band operation, polarization reconfigurability (LP, RHCP, LHCP), and achieving axial ratios below 0.1 dB over a ±50° scan range, validated via passive and active measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a shared-aperture phased-array antenna capable of operating in both S- and X-bands for multi-mission SATCOM ground terminals, reducing aperture size and design burden while supporting polarization reconfigurability.

Method: A repeatable unit cell supporting independent S- and X-band operation is used, combined with dual-polarized radiators and calibrated complex receive coefficients to synthesize LP, RHCP, and LHCP. Theoretical estimates for scan matching and inter-band isolation are used before full shared-aperture verification. Prototypes are validated via passive (individual element) and active (RF system-on-chip simultaneous channel) measurements.

Result: Axial ratios below 0.1 dB in target S- and X-band receiving bands over a ±50° scan range. Wide-angle scanning, very high polarization purity, and polarization-reconfigurable operation are confirmed.

Conclusion: The proposed SAPAA architecture enables arbitrary aperture scaling and provides wide-angle scanning, high polarization purity, and polarization reconfigurability suitable for multi-mission SATCOM ground terminals.

Abstract: This paper presents a modular S-/X-band shared-aperture phased-array antenna (SAPAA) for satellite-communication ground-station reception. The proposed architecture uses a repeatable unit cell that supports independent S- and X-band operation within the same physical aperture and enables arbitrary aperture scaling. Dual-polarized radiators are combined with calibrated complex receive coefficients to synthesize linear polarization (LP), right-hand circular polarization (RHCP), and left-hand circular polarization (LHCP). The design burden of the electrically large shared aperture is reduced by using theoretical estimates for scan matching and inter-band isolation before full shared-aperture verification. Simulated and measured results demonstrate axial ratios below 0.1 dB in the target S- and X-band receiving bands over a +/-50 deg scan range. The prototypes are validated using two approaches: passive measurements, where the element responses are measured individually, and RF system-on-chip-based active measurements, where all available receive channels are measured simultaneously. The results confirm that the proposed SAPAA provides wide-angle scanning, very high polarization purity, and polarization-reconfigurable operation for multi-mission SATCOM ground terminals.

</details>


### [65] [Physics-Constrained Synthetic Training for Sub-Terahertz Channel Rainfall Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21857)
*Wanzhu Chang,Yuheng Song,Jiabiao Zhao,Mingxia Zhang,Jie Yang,Kefeng Huang,Kaixin Sun,Hong Ling,Jianjun Ma*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes RainFormer, a hybrid attention-convolution network trained on physics-constrained synthetic data (combining ITU-R and Mie-theory rain attenuation with multiple DSD priors), for estimating rainfall rate from short received-power sequences in THz channels, achieving zero-shot rank-consistent estimation with uncertainty bounds derived from DSD prior spread.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: THz channel rainfall estimation lacks large operational datasets for supervised learning, unlike satellite, radar, and commercial microwave link retrieval methods, creating a severe data scarcity problem.

Method: Conducted outdoor campaign over 41.5 m THz channel at 140 GHz and 229 GHz; synthesized physics-constrained training data using ITU-R P.838-3 and Mie-theory rain attenuation across four raindrop-size-distribution priors with controlled stochastic fluctuations; designed RainFormer (hybrid attention-convolution network) that fuses local fluctuation structure, long-range temporal dependencies, and explicit physical-statistical descriptors from short received-power sequences.

Result: Synthetic-trained models applied zero-shot to measured rainfall produce rank-consistent, physically interpretable estimates; the inter-prior spread bounds uncertainty from unobservable path DSD; explicit descriptors and temporal-order information carry most predictive information, with convolution and attention as complementary refinements.

Conclusion: DSD-bracketed synthetic training is a viable foundation for THz rainfall sensing under severe data scarcity.

Abstract: Terahertz channels can serve as opportunistic rainfall sensors because rain-induced extinction couples received power to rainfall intensity. Unlike satellite, radar, and commercial microwave link retrieval, THz channel rainfall estimation lacks large operational datasets that supervised learning requires. This article uses an outdoor campaign over a 41.5 m THz channel at 140 GHz and 229 GHz to calibrate the channel statistical properties, then synthesizing physics constrained training data that combine the ITU-R P.838-3 and Mie-theory rain attenuation across four raindrop-size-distribution priors with controlled stochastic fluctuations. RainFormer, a hybrid attention-convolution network, maps a short received-power sequence to rainfall rate by fusing local fluctuation structure, long-range temporal dependencies, and explicit physical-statistical descriptors. Ablation shows that the explicit descriptors and temporal-order information carry most of the predictive information, with convolution and attention acting as complementary refinements. Applied zero-shot to measured rainfall, the synthetic-trained models produce rank-consistent, physically interpretable estimates whose inter-prior spread bounds the uncertainty arising from the unobservable path DSD (raindrop size distribution), establishing DSD-bracketed synthetic training as a viable foundation for THz rainfall sensing under severe data scarcity.

</details>


### [66] [Revisiting Theoretical Modeling of Seebeck Coefficient of Semiconductors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21879)
*Yuchao Hua*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: The paper exposes thermodynamic flaws in existing Seebeck coefficient models (confusion of potentials, improper distribution gradient, incorrect heat flux) and proposes a new reversible model derived from drift-diffusion with Soret effect, which eliminates velocity/relaxation terms, gives a theoretical lower bound, and matches experimental data for highly-n-doped Silicon.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing closed-form Seebeck coefficient models contain fundamental thermodynamic errors: confusing electrochemical potential with electrical potential, neglecting the dependence on conduction band bottom and Fermi level in the distribution function spatial gradient, and using improper heat flux presentation.

Method: The Seebeck coefficient is derived from the drift-diffusion equation incorporating the Soret effect under open-circuit conditions. The reversible model recovers the band term by eliminating electron velocity and relaxation terms in the near-equilibrium case, while the phonon-drag term matches the Boltzmann transport equation (BTE) form. A case study on highly-n-doped Silicon compares reversible, ballistic (Landauer), and BTE models against experimental data.

Result: The reversible model provides fairly good predictions for experiments on highly-n-doped Silicon. It theoretically gives the lower bound of the Seebeck coefficient. The phonon-drag term is identical to the BTE-based one.

Conclusion: Given its theoretical soundness, simple form, and low computational cost, the reversible model is a concise alternative for evaluating thermoelectric properties in both reversible and near-equilibrium cases.

Abstract: We revisit the closed-form models of Seebeck coefficient, and identify the thermodynamic flaws in those extensively-used methodologies, essentially including the confusion of electrochemical potential and electrical potential within the definition, arbitrarily neglecting the dependence on conduction band bottom and Fermi level when calculating distribution function spatial gradient, and the improper heat flux presentation. Here, an alternative methodology is presented, which derives the Seebeck coefficient model based on the drift-diffusion equation with the Soret effect in the open circuit condition. The reversible model that eliminates the electron velocity and relaxation terms in the formulation is recovered for the band term in the near-equilibrium case, and it can give the lower bound of Seebeck coefficient in theory, while the phonon-drag term is of the identical form to the Boltzmann transport equation(BTE)-based one. A case study is performed for highly-n-doped Silicon, where the reversible, ballistic(based on Landauer formulation) and BTE models are compared with each other and with the experimental data. The reversible model gives the fairly-good predictions for the experiments. Given its theoretical-soundness, simple form and low computation cost, the reversible model can serve as a concise alternative for evaluating thermoelectric properties in both the reversible and near-equilibrium cases.

</details>


### [67] [Steerable Radiation Forces with Frequency-Detuned Acoustic Metasurfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22081)
*Sam Keller,Matthew Stein,Ognjen Ilic*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: Acoustic waves with small frequency detuning can induce controlled, reversible translation and rotation of macroscopic objects via patterned metasurfaces, demonstrated experimentally at ultrasonic frequencies with 3D-printed structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve steerable, remote-controlled motion of large objects using acoustic waves by converting small frequency changes into directional forces via metasurface design, enabling programmable dynamic behaviors without complex actuation.

Method: Design of force-optimal acoustic metasurface topologies; detuning incident frequency by ±δω (δω=2.5 kHz) around a base frequency ω=22.5 kHz; experimental validation at ultrasonic frequencies using 3D-printed metasurfaces.

Result: Reversible, real-time directional motion: positive detuning (ω+δω) moves the object one way, negative detuning (ω-δω) moves it the opposite way; demonstrated experimentally for objects larger than the acoustic wavelength.

Conclusion: The concept is scalable across frequencies and materials, providing a building block for complex, remote-controlled dynamical behaviors programmable through surface pattern reconfiguration.

Abstract: We demonstrate that acoustic waves can induce controlled translation and rotation of macroscopic objects through small, but deliberate, detuning of the driving wave frequency. When an object is patterned with a suitably designed acoustic metasurface, small changes in the incident frequency $ω\pm δω$ are converted into directional radiation forces and torques, enabling steerable motion even for objects much larger than the acoustic wavelength. We present the concept of a force-optimal metasurface topology and show that it enables fully reversible forces in real time: the object is moved in one direction for positively detuned incident frequency $ω+δω$ and in the opposite direction for negatively detuned frequency $ω-δω$, where $ω=22.5 \textrm{ kHz}$ and $δω=2.5 \textrm{ kHz}$ for a proof of concept at inaudible frequencies. This mechanism is demonstrated experimentally at ultrasonic frequencies with 3D-printed metasurfaces. The proposed concept is scalable across frequencies and materials, offering a building block for realizing complex, remote-controlled, dynamical behaviors that can be programmed by reconfiguring material surface patterns.

</details>


### [68] [Structure-driven analog optical control in ion-pumped SrFeO$_{3-δ}$ thin-film devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22154)
*Alicia Ruiz-Caridad,Paul Nizet,Francesco Chiabrera,Xavier Vea,Philipp Langner,Alex Morata,Albert Tarancon*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates structure-driven analog optical control in oxygen-based MIEC thin-film devices (SrFeO₃₋δ) via reversible phase transitions between brownmillerite and perovskite structures, enabling continuous and reversible modulation of optical transmittance and color when integrated with an Al₂O₃ interference layer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ECDs face limitations in operating voltage, switching speed, color tunability, and long-term stability. Oxygen-based MIECs offer enhanced durability and compatibility but remain underexplored for electrochromic and photonic applications.

Method: The authors demonstrate an ion-pumped SrFeO₃₋δ thin-film device that undergoes reversible oxygen-driven phase transitions between brownmillerite and perovskite structures, integrated with an optically passive Al₂O₃ interference layer for continuous analog optical modulation.

Result: Reversible phase transitions between brownmillerite and perovskite structures are achieved, accompanied by pronounced changes in electronic structure and optical constants. Continuous and reversible modulation of optical transmittance and color is demonstrated.

Conclusion: This work provides a general framework for ion-driven analog photonic and electrochromic devices, highlighting oxygen-based MIECs as promising for next-generation ionochromic systems compatible with silicon-based photonic platforms.

Abstract: Electrochromic devices (ECDs) offer a compelling route toward low-power, non-emissive optical modulators with nonvolatile states. However, their widespread implementation is hindered by limitations in operating voltage, switching speed, color tunability, and long-term stability. Mixed ionic-electronic conductors (MIECs) provide a promising alternative platform, enabling optical modulation through ion-driven redox and structural transformations. Oxygen-based MIECs offer enhanced durability, environmental robustness, and compatibility with oxide electronics and silicon photonics, yet remain largely underexplored for electrochromic and photonic applications. Here, we demonstrate structure-driven analog optical control in an ion-pumped SrFeO$_{3-δ}$ thin-film device by undergoing reversible oxygen-driven phase transitions between brownmillerite and perovskite structures. Phase transition is accompanied by pronounced changes in its electronic structure and optical constants. By harnessing these ion-induced structural transformations and integrating an optically passive Al$_2$O$_3$ interference layer, we achieve continuous and reversible modulation of optical transmittance and color. These results provide a general framework for ion-driven analog photonic and electrochromic devices and highlight the potential of oxygen-based MIECs for next-generation ionochromic systems compatible with silicon-based photonic platforms.

</details>


### [69] [Defect-Width-Tunable Resonant Elastic-Wave Transmission in Micro-Pillar Arrays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22264)
*Alok Pokharel*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: This paper numerically investigates defect-engineered resonant transmission in periodic tungsten micro-pillar arrays on silicon substrates. Using finite-element simulations, the study shows that one-, two-, and three-pillar defects enable defect-width-dependent transmission modulation and localized resonant wave redistribution. Floquet analysis reveals flat resonant branches with low-group-velocity modes, and tungsten outperforms copper due to higher inertial contrast, providing a platform for frequency-selective elastic-wave control in metamaterials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve effective control of elastic-wave propagation through localized resonant interactions using periodic micro-pillar lattices on elastic substrates, and to explore defect engineering as a means for frequency-selective wave manipulation.

Method: Finite-element simulations with two- and three-dimensional frequency-domain analyses; Floquet dispersion analysis of periodic unit cells; comparative study between tungsten and copper resonators.

Result: Defect-width-dependent transmission modulation and localized resonant elastic-wave redistribution were observed. Floquet analysis showed multiple nearly flat resonant branches with low-group-velocity modes. Tungsten exhibited enhanced resonant confinement over copper due to larger inertial contrast with the substrate.

Conclusion: Defect-engineered micro-pillar lattices provide an effective approach for frequency-selective elastic-wave control and localized resonant wave manipulation in elastic metamaterial systems.

Abstract: Periodic micro-pillar lattices integrated on elastic substrates provide a promising plat-form for controlling elastic-wave propagation through localized resonant interactions. In this work, defect-engineered resonant transmission in periodic tungsten micro-pillar arrays deposited on silicon substrates is numerically investigated using finite-element simulations. Two-dimensional frequency-domain analyses were performed to evaluate the influence of one-, two- and three-pillar defects on elastic wave transmission characteristics. The results reveal strong defect-width-dependent transmission modulation together with the localized resonant elastic-wave redistribution within the periodic lattice. Full three-dimensional simulations further confirm the presence of defect-sensitive resonant localization and modified elastic-wave transport pathways. Floquet dispersion analysis of a periodic unit cell reveals multiple nearly flat resonant branches associated with low-group-velocity elastic-wave modes, indicating predominantly subwavelength locally resonant behavior. A comparative study between tungsten and copper resonators demonstrates enhanced resonant confinement in tung-sten-based structures due to their larger inertial contrast with the supporting sub-strate. The proposed defect-engineered micro-pillar lattices provide an effective approach for frequency-selective elastic-wave control and localized resonant wave ma-nipulation in elastic metamaterial systems.

</details>


### [70] [Physics-governed executable modelling of triboelectric nanogenerators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23051)
*Hongfa Zhao,Baiqiao Wang,Tiancong Zhao,Chun Jin,Hanlin Zhou,Mingrui Shu,Minyi Xu,Liwei Lin,Wenbo Ding,Zhong Lin Wang*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces TENG-CLAW, a charge-defined physics-governed simulation platform that unifies fragmented predictive modelling approaches for triboelectric nanogenerators (TENGs) into a self-consistent electrostatic hierarchy, enabling traceable, reproducible simulation across analytical and finite-geometry formulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predictive modelling of TENGs is fragmented across analytical theories, finite-geometry solvers, and disconnected simulation workflows, lacking a unified executable framework for quantitative research.

Method: A charge-defined modelling framework is proposed and implemented as TENG-CLAW, establishing a self-consistent electrostatic hierarchy where triboelectric charges, pre-charging charges, and compensating electrode charges serve as defining state variables. This bridges infinite plate analytical limits with finite-geometry numerical formulations, and converts user-defined research requests into physically admissible simulation tasks.

Result: The framework connects analytical and numerical formulations, producing outputs tied to explicit charge states, boundary conditions, solver routes, and reusable artifacts across spatial, temporal, field-level, comparative, and reporting workflows.

Conclusion: This work establishes a rigorous computational basis for interpreting TENG mechanisms and provides reproducible research infrastructure for simulation and physics-guided device design.

Abstract: Predictive modelling of triboelectric nanogenerators (TENGs) remains fragmented across analytical theories, finite-geometry solvers and disconnected simulation workflows. These disparate approaches must be unified into an executable framework to advance quantitative TENG research.Here we introduce a charge-defined modelling framework and implement it as TENG-CLAW, a physics-governed platform for traceable TENG simulation. The framework establishes a self-consistent electrostatic hierarchy in which triboelectric charges, pre-charging charges and compensating electrode charges serve as defining state variables.This hierarchy connects the infinite plate analytical limit for near-uniform fields with finite-geometry numerical formulations required for edge-dominated devices. Built on this basis, TENG-CLAW converts user-defined research requests into physically admissible simulation tasks, so that generated outputs are tied to explicit charge states, boundary conditions, solver routes and reusable artifacts across spatial, temporal, field-level, comparative and reporting workflows. This work establishes a rigorous computational basis for interpreting TENG mechanisms and provides reproducible research infrastructure for simulation and physics-guided device design.

</details>


### [71] [High performance construction materials fracture and high cycle fatigue assessment based on accelerated PF-CZM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23468)
*Jiaqi Li,Zhihua Xiong,Xuyao Liu,Hongyu Liu*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Phase-Field Cohesive Zone Model (PF-CZM) framework to simulate fracture and high-cycle fatigue behavior of Ultra-High Performance Concrete (UHPC) and High Strength Steel (HSS), incorporating a stress-based failure criterion, fatigue degradation function, and an acceleration algorithm using envelope loading to avoid cycle-by-cycle simulation. The model is validated against experiments for mixed-mode fracture and high-cycle fatigue.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fracture and high-cycle fatigue behavior of high-performance materials (UHPC, HSS) currently relies on costly, time-consuming, and limited experimental methods, motivating the need for an efficient computational framework.

Method: The method integrates (1) a generic stress-based failure criterion for UHPC derived from biaxial tensile tests to compute crack driving force, (2) a fatigue degradation function, and (3) an acceleration algorithm using envelope load approximation and adaptive cyclic increment to simulate the three-stage fatigue process within the PF-CZM framework.

Result: The proposed model successfully captures mixed-mode fracture and high-cycle fatigue behavior of both UHPC and HSS, with experimental comparisons validating its accuracy.

Conclusion: The PF-CZM framework with the fatigue acceleration algorithm provides an efficient and generalizable computational approach for simulating fracture and high-cycle fatigue in high-performance materials, reducing reliance on purely experimental methods.

Abstract: Given the widespread application of high-performance materials in cyclic loaded infrastructure, a thorough understanding of the fracture and high-cycle fatigue behavior of high-performance materials is of critical importance. However, these behaviors predominantly rely on experimental methods, which are often costly, time-consuming and limited in generalizability. To address these issues, this paper proposes the constitution and high-cycle fatigue of Ultra-High Performance Concrete (UHPC) and High Strength Steel (HSS) within the Phase-Field Cohesive Zone Model (PF-CZM) framework. A generic stress-based failure criterion for UHPC is derived from the biaxial tensile test results to calculate crack driving force. Furthermore, a fatigue degradation function and an acceleration algorithm are integrated into the PF-CZM framework to enable efficient high-cycle fatigue simulations. In the acceleration algorithm, the envelope load is used to approximate the real cyclic load to avoid the simulation of each cycle, and the three-stage fatigue process is simulated through adjusting the cyclic increment adaptively. The proposed model successfully captures mixed-mode fracture and high-cycle fatigue behavior of UHPC and HSS, with validation provided through relevant experimental comparisons.

</details>


<div id='physics.atom-ph'></div>

# physics.atom-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [72] [Direct evidence for projectile electronic structure effects in slow multielectron capture collisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21285)
*Akash Srivastav,Sumit Srivastav,Bhas Bapat*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This study shows that the electronic structure of projectile ions (N^q+ vs O^q+) significantly influences the kinetic energy release distributions (KERDs) in CO₂ fragmentation after multielectron capture collisions at 0.31 a.u. impact velocity, with isoelectronic projectiles of different charge states producing notably different KERDs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate how the electronic structure of the projectile affects ionization and subsequent fragmentation of CO₂ via multielectron capture, aiming to demonstrate that projectile identity matters beyond simple charge or velocity scaling.

Method: Measurements of kinetic energy release distributions (KERDs) for the CO₂³⁺ → O⁺:C⁺:O⁺ break-up channel at 0.31 a.u. impact velocity, comparing N^q+ and O^q+ projectiles across two categories: same-charge (q=4,6) and isoelectronic (N^q+ vs O^{(q+1)+} with q=3,5,7) projectiles.

Result: Same-charge N^q+ and O^q+ projectiles yield broadly similar KERDs but differ significantly from earlier Ar^q+ results. Isoelectronic N^q+ and O^{(q+1)+} projectiles produce pronounced differences in KERDs.

Conclusion: Projectile electronic structure plays a critical role in multielectron capture collisions, as evidenced by the distinct KERDs between isoelectronic species.

Abstract: We investigate the role of the electronic structure of the projectile in ionization and subsequent fragmentation of CO$_2$ induced by multielectron capture in collisions at 0.31 a.u. impact velocity. Focusing on the $\text{CO}_2^{3+} \rightarrow \text{O}^+:\text{C}^+:\text{O}^+$ break-up channel as a representative channel, we report kinetic energy release distributions (KERDs) for collisions with equi-velocity N$^{q+}$ and O$^{q+}$ projectiles. We consider two complementary categories of measurements. In the first category, in which different projectiles of the same charge are considered, we find that KERDs obtained with N$^{q+}$ and O$^{q+}$ impact ($q=4,6$) are broadly similar, but they differ significantly from the earlier reported KERD with Ar$^{q+}$ impact. In the second category, pronounced differences are observed between the KERDs obtained with isoelectronic N$^{q+}$ and O$^{(q+1)+}$ ($q=3,5,7$) projectiles. These results provide direct evidence that projectile electronic structure plays a critical role in multielectron capture collisions.

</details>


### [73] [Quantitative analysis of resonant ionization by smooth laser pulses: Connection between effective Hamiltonian theory and strong-field dressed continua](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21526)
*Jakob Nicolai Bruhnke,Jan Marcus Dahlström*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper resolves the debate on asymmetric Autler-Townes doublets in resonant photoionization by unifying previous interpretations under a common effective Hamiltonian framework, and shows that a time-dependent essential-state model rigorously coupling electrons to the continuum matches ab initio simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the ongoing debate about the origin of extremely asymmetric Autler-Townes doublets in intense high-frequency resonant photoionization.

Method: Development of a common effective Hamiltonian framework unifying previous interpretations, and a time-dependent essential-state model with rigorous electron-continuum coupling, validated against ab initio time-dependent Schrödinger equation simulations for helium.

Result: The time-dependent essential-state model achieves excellent agreement with ab initio simulations, while the strong-field approximation obscures the underlying mechanism.

Conclusion: The asymmetric Autler-Townes doublets arise from the coherent interplay of resonant and nonresonant pathways within a dressed-state stabilization framework, not from either interference or continuum dressing alone.

Abstract: Resonant photoionization in the intense high-frequency regime can exhibit extremely asymmetric Autler-Townes doublets whose origin remains debated. It has been attributed either to interference between perturbative ionization pathways or to a non-perturbative dressing of the continuum. Here we show that these interpretations arise from a common effective Hamiltonian framework, in which dressed-state stabilization is governed by the coherent interplay of resonant and nonresonant pathways. We demonstrate that further simplification, using the strong-field approximation, obscures this mechanism. In contrast, our time-dependent essential-state model, where electrons are rigorously coupled to the continuum, achieves excellent agreement with ab initio simulations of the time-dependent Schrödinger equation for helium.

</details>


### [74] [Capture velocities for direct loading of heavy molecules into conveyor-belt magneto-optical traps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21944)
*Shoukang Yang,Shuhua Deng,Zixuan Zeng,Bo Yan*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether conveyor-belt magneto-optical traps (CB-MOTs), which use blue-detuned polarization-gradient forces, can effectively load molecular beams beyond the specific molecule (BaF-138) used in initial demonstrations. Using optical-Bloch-equation force calculations and classical trajectory propagation, the authors show that CB-MOTs retain broad capture velocity regions for BaF-137 (complex hyperfine structure) and BaH-138 (narrower linewidth), confirming that this dipole-force-dominated mechanism is a practical loading route for heavy laser-coolable molecules with otherwise limited MOT performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether the direct CB-MOT loading strategy demonstrated for BaF-138 generalizes to other heavy laser-coolable molecules that face limitations from photon recoil, scattering rate, or hyperfine complexity.

Method: Established optical-Bloch-equation force calculations combined with classical trajectory propagation to simulate CB-MOT capture velocities for BaF-138 (validation), BaF-137 (dense hyperfine structure), and BaH-138 (narrower linewidth, longer wavelength).

Result: The CB-MOT capture velocity trend for BaF-138 matches experimental observations. Both BaF-137 and BaH-138 exhibit broad regions of nonzero capture velocity, indicating effective direct CB-MOT loading.

Conclusion: The dipole-force-dominated conveyor-belt mechanism provides a practical loading route for heavy laser-coolable molecules whose MOT performance is otherwise limited by photon recoil, scattering rate, or hyperfine complexity.

Abstract: Conveyor-belt magneto-optical traps (CB-MOTs) use blue-detuned polarization-gradient forces to provide simultaneous cooling, confinement, and loading on type-II molecular transitions. Recent experiments with \baf{138} showed that this mechanism can directly load a slowed molecular beam with an efficiency exceeding that of a conventional red-detuned MOT. Here we use established optical-Bloch-equation force calculations and classical trajectory propagation to ask whether this direct-loading strategy should extend beyond the specific molecule used in the first demonstration. For \baf{138}, the calculation reproduces the experimentally observed trend that the CB-MOT capture velocity increases with laser intensity. We then apply the same framework to two closely related but experimentally distinct cases: \baf{137}, whose dense hyperfine structure complicates a conventional dual-frequency MOT, and \bah{138}, whose narrower linewidth and longer wavelength reduce the available radiative force. In both cases, the CB-MOT retains a broad region of nonzero capture velocity. These results identify the molecular conditions under which direct CB-MOT loading should remain effective and show that the dipole-force-dominated conveyor-belt mechanism provides a practical loading route for heavy laser-coolable molecules whose MOT performance is otherwise limited by photon recoil, scattering rate, or hyperfine complexity.

</details>


### [75] [An \Al clock with $1.6\times10^{-18}$ systematic uncertainty and its frequency ratios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23151)
*Fabian Dawel,Johannes Kramer,Derwell Drapier,Lennart Pelzer,Kai Dietze,Mirza A. Ali,Marek Hild,Vincent Barbé,Steven A. King,Joshua Klose,Kilian Stahl,Johannes Rahm,Navraj Poudel,Sören Dörscher,Stefan Weyers,Erik Benkler,Christian Lisdat,Piet O. Schmidt*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a full evaluation of an aluminum ion (Al⁺) optical clock with a total systematic fractional frequency uncertainty of 1.6×10⁻¹⁸, measures its absolute frequency as 1,121,015,393,207,859.19(24) Hz via comparison with two primary caesium fountain clocks, and determines the Al⁺/Sr frequency ratio as 2.611,701,431,781,462,668(36), which deviates from BACON collaboration results by 8.6σ (2021) and 1.2σ (2025).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Advances in optical clocks motivate a redefinition of the second, which requires rigorous evaluations of systematic uncertainties and robust consistency among different optical clocks.

Method: Full evaluation of systematic frequency shifts of an Al⁺ single-ion clock; measurement of its absolute frequency by comparison with two primary caesium fountain clocks at PTB; determination of the frequency ratio between the Al⁺ and a Sr optical lattice clock.

Result: Total systematic fractional frequency uncertainty: 1.6×10⁻¹⁸. Absolute frequency of Al⁺ clock: ν_Al⁺ = 1,121,015,393,207,859.19(24) Hz. Al⁺/Sr frequency ratio: 2.611,701,431,781,462,668(36). The ratio differs from BACON collaboration results by 8.6σ (2021) and 1.2σ (2025).

Conclusion: These results contribute toward a future redefinition of the second using optical clocks and underscore the importance of independent measurements of clock-candidate frequency ratios across different institutions.

Abstract: Advances in optical clocks motivate a redefinition of the second, requiring rigorous evaluations of systematic uncertainties and robust consistency among the clocks. Here, we report the full evaluation of the systematic frequency shifts of an \AlP single-ion clock, and the measurement of its absolute frequency and frequency ratio with a \Sres optical lattice clock at PTB. The evaluated total systematic fractional frequency uncertainty is \num{1.6e-18}, mainly limited by the accuracy of the relevant atomic coefficients and by background gas collisions. The absolute frequency of the clock has been measured to be $ν_{\mathrm{Al}^+}=1 121 015 393 207 859.19(24)\,$Hz, obtained by comparison with two primary caesium fountain clocks at PTB. The frequency ratio between the \Al and \Sr optical clocks has been determined to be $ν_{\mathrm{Al}^+}/ν_{\mathrm{Sr}}=2.611 701 431 781 462 668(36)$, limited by the accuracy of the \Sr clock. This ratio differs by $8.6σ$ and $1.2σ$ from the 2021 and 2025 frequency ratio published by the BACON collaboration, respectively. These results represent an important contribution toward a future redefinition of the second using optical clocks, and underscore the importance of independent measurements of clock-candidate frequency ratios across different institutions.

</details>


### [76] [Vortex photoelectron holography in strong-field tunneling ionization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23318)
*Yongkun Chen,Oleg I. Tolstikhin,Toru Morishita,Yueming Zhou,Peixiang Lu*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This paper extends strong-field photoelectron holography (SFPH) from plane-wave to vortex rescattering, using vortex electrons generated via tunneling ionization. By solving the TDSE, the vortex scattering phase is extracted from SFPH fringes, enabling structurally sensitive imaging with phase-engineered photoelectrons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend conventional strong-field photoelectron holography (SFPH) from plane-wave rescattering to vortex rescattering, enabling direct access to the vortex scattering phase for structurally sensitive imaging.

Method: The authors introduce vortex photoelectron holography, investigate rescattering dynamics of vortex photoelectrons by the parent ion, and solve the time-dependent Schrödinger equation (TDSE) to extract the vortex scattering phase from SFPH fringes.

Result: The vortex scattering phase extracted from SFPH fringes shows excellent agreement with scattering calculations.

Conclusion: This work provides direct access to the vortex scattering phase, paving the way for applying SFPH to structurally sensitive imaging with phase-engineered photoelectrons.

Abstract: Vortex electrons, characterized by a helical phase front, offer unique advantages for probing material structures. Such electrons can be generated via tunneling ionization in strong laser fields. In this paper, we investigate the rescattering dynamics of vortex photoelectrons by the parent ion. Specifically, we introduce vortex photoelectron holography, extending conventional strong-field photoelectron holography (SFPH) from plane-wave to vortex rescattering. By solving the time-dependent Schrödinger equation, we extract the vortex scattering phase from the SFPH fringes, showing excellent agreement with scattering calculations. Thus, our work provides direct access to the vortex scattering phase, paving the way for applying SFPH to structurally sensitive imaging with phase-engineered photoelectrons.

</details>

# cond-mat.str-el [[Back]](#toc)

### [77] [Partition function zeros of quantum many-body systems: perturbative results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20808)
*Muhammad Sedik,B Sriram Shastry*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: A perturbation-based method for finding Yang-Lee zeros in quantum systems is applied to the 1D Hubbard model, validated against exact diagonalization, and an experimental detection scheme for a Hubbard ring is proposed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically compute Yang-Lee partition function zeros of quantum many-body systems using perturbation theory at finite temperatures, extending the method introduced in arXiv:2504.01880.

Method: The method identifies wave-vector and temperature-dependent complex virtual energies from the thermal electronic Green's function, with zeros obtained by collecting virtual energies over all k-vectors. It is applied to the 1D Hubbard model under different boundary conditions up to second-order perturbation theory and compared with exact diagonalization.

Result: Results up to second-order perturbation theory are compared with exact diagonalization; a quantity for experimental detection is proposed and demonstrated via exact diagonalization of an 8-site Hubbard ring.

Conclusion: The perturbation method can effectively yield Yang-Lee zeros for the Hubbard model, and the proposed experimental detection scheme shows promise for detecting these zeros in Hubbard rings.

Abstract: A systematic method to find the Yang-Lee partition function zeros of quantum many-body systems based on perturbation theory at finite temperatures was recently introduced in arXiv:2504.01880. This method identifies wave-vector and temperature-dependent complex virtual energies obtainable from the thermal electronic Greens function. The collection of virtual energies over all $\vec{k}$ yield the Yang-Lee zeroes. We apply this approach to the one-dimensional Hubbard model for different boundary conditions. We compare the results obtained by this method up to second-order in perturbation theory with the results found by exact diagonalization. We also propose a quantity that could be used for experimental detection of these zeros of a Hubbard ring. An example of the detection method is presented using exact diagonalization of the 8-site Hubbard ring.

</details>


### [78] [Displacement-Field-Driven Semimetal-Superconductor Transition in Magic-Angle Twisted Trilayer Graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20849)
*Bokai Liang,Jing-Yu Zhao,Ya-Hui Zhang*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Magic-angle twisted trilayer graphene (MATTG) exhibits displacement-field-tuned correlated phenomena. Contrary to expectations of heavy fermion physics, the dominant effect of the displacement field is to induce self-doping into the TBG sector via an energy shift of the Dirac cone. Using slave-particle theory at filling ν=±2, the study shows that increasing displacement field drives a semimetal-to-superconductor transition, with enhanced superconductivity near ν=±2 and particle-hole asymmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the mechanism behind displacement-field-tuned correlated phenomena in MATTG, particularly why the naive expectation of D-driven heavy fermion physics fails due to the momentum-selective Mott gap and the invalidity of the rigid local moment description.

Method: Slave-particle theory applied at filling ν=±2 in MATTG, analyzing the effect of displacement field D on the Dirac cone energy shift and self-doping into the TBG sector.

Result: Increasing displacement field D drives a semimetal-to-superconducting state transition. Superconductivity is enhanced by D near ν=±2, and the phase diagram exhibits particle-hole asymmetry.

Conclusion: The displacement field's primary role is inducing Dirac cone energy shift and self-doping, not heavy fermion physics. The findings provide a unified picture for electric-field-tunable superconductivity, Mottness, and heavy-fermion-like behavior in MATTG.

Abstract: Magic-angle twisted trilayer graphene(MATTG) hosts versatile displacement-field-tuned correlated phenomena. MATTG consists of a dispersive Dirac cone which hybridizes with the flat band from a twisted bilayer graphene (TBG) sector. The hybridization strength increases with the displacement field $D$ and naively one may expect D-driven heavy fermion physics. However, the TBG Hubbard bands have a momentum-selective Mott gap, which is small at the $Γ$ point due to the band topology, and a rigid local moment description as in the familiar Kondo lattice model is invalid. Here we show that the dominant effect of the displacement field is to induce an energy shift of the Dirac cone and self-doping into the TBG sector. We illustrate this picture in a concrete calculation using a slave-particle theory at the filling $ν=\pm 2$. We find that increasing $D$ drives a transition from a semimetal into a superconducting state. We also discuss the enhancement of the superconductivity by $D$ near $ν=\pm2$ and the particle-hole asymmetry of the phase diagram. Our results provide a unified picture for electric-field-tunable superconductivity, Mottness, and heavy-fermion-like behavior in MATTG.

</details>


### [79] [Importance of effective Coulomb interactions for $T_c$ in cuprates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21323)
*Jakša Vučičević,Upendra Kumar,Chia-Nan Yeh,Miguel A. Morales,Malte Rösner*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper systematically studies ~40 cuprate superconductors using DFT bandstructure calculations, downfolding to single-band Hubbard and three-band Emery models, and statistical analysis to identify crystal structure properties that most strongly correlate with superconducting Tc.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The superconducting Tc varies significantly among different cuprates, but it is unclear which crystal structure properties affect Tc most strongly. Understanding this is essential for high-Tc materials design.

Method: Data collection from literature and databases for ~40 cuprate compounds; fully automated DFT bandstructure computation; downfolding to single-band Hubbard and three-band Emery models using MLWFs and cRPA; thorough statistical analysis of correlations between experimental Tc and computed model parameters.

Result: Tc correlates well with simple functions of model parameters. Confirms t' (next-nearest neighbor hopping) correlates with Tc, with Tc vanishing below a finite t' value. Most importantly, Tc correlates most strongly with Coulomb coupling on p-orbitals in the 3-band model, highlighting oxygen site importance.

Conclusion: More sophisticated models may be needed for unified description of all cuprates, but simple model parameters (especially p-orbital Coulomb coupling in the 3-band model and t' in the single-band model) show clear correlations with experimental Tc, emphasizing the critical role of oxygen sites in copper-oxide planes.

Abstract: Cuprate superconductors exhibit the highest observed superconducting $T_c$ at atmospheric pressure. However, the magnitude of $T_c$ varies significantly between different cuprates. At present, it is unclear what properties of the crystal structure affect $T_c$ most strongly, yet such an understanding must underpin any efforts toward high-$T_c$ materials design. To address this issue, we perform a large scale systematic study, employing a combination of data collection, state-of-the-art numerical methods, and statistical analysis. We identify about 40 different cuprate compounds, and we compile detailed data about their $T_c$'s and crystal structures from literature and the available databases. Using a fully automated procedure, for each compound we compute the DFT bandstructure and downfold it to two of the most commonly studied low-energy lattice models, namely the single-band Hubbard and the three-band Emery models. The downfolding is based on the approach of MLWFs and cRPA. Finally, we apply a thorough and unbiased statistical analysis to investigate the correlations between the experimentally measured $T_c$'s and the computed parameters of our theoretical models. Our data indicates that more sophisticated models might be needed to describe all cuprates on the same footing. Nevertheless, we find that $T_c$ scales well with simple functions of model parameters. We confirm a previously observed trend that $t'$ in the single-band model correlates with the experimental $T_c$, and we find that $T_c$ appears to vanish below a finite value of $t'$, in agreement with recent ground-state calculations for the Hubbard model. However, we find that the coupling strength also plays a role: throughout our entire dataset, $T_c$ correlates the most with the Coulomb coupling on the $p$-orbitals in the 3-band model, highlighting the importance of the oxygen sites in the copper-oxide planes.

</details>


### [80] [Localization pattern of a mobile impurity in the disordered Kitaev chain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21499)
*A. V. Sadovnikov,A. N. Rubtsov*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: A mobile impurity coupled to a Kitaev chain with disorder shows different localization behavior in topological vs trivial regimes: partial localization in the topological regime and sharp single-site localization in the trivial regime, with edge preference near the sweet spot explained by Majorana-dimer structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether a mobile impurity can distinguish different regimes (topological vs trivial) of a disordered Kitaev chain host system.

Method: Exact diagonalization for small periodic chains and DMRG for open chains at strong interaction, analyzing impurity localization via IPR and density distributions.

Result: In the deep topological regime, impurity localizes only partially with smooth IPR increase; in the deep trivial regime, sharp transition to nearly single-site localization. Near the Kitaev sweet spot, edge-localized impurity density is observed, which spreads into the bulk with increasing chemical potential. Disorder competes with edge bias and can pin the impurity in the bulk.

Conclusion: The impurity is sensitive to the host regime, but there is no strict one-to-one correspondence between impurity localization and host topology—only an indirect correlation via disorder-averaged behavior.

Abstract: We study a mobile impurity coupled to a Kitaev chain with chemical-potential disorder and ask whether the impurity behavior distinguishes different regimes of the host system. Exact diagonalization calculations for small periodic chains shows that in the deep topological regime the impurity localizes only partially, with a smooth increase of $\mathrm{IPR}_d$, whereas in the deep trivial regime it undergoes a much sharp transition to nearly single-site localization. For open chains at strong interaction, DMRG shows edge-localized impurity density near the Kitaev sweet spot. With increasing chemical potential, the impurity weight spreads into the bulk and eventually becomes almost uniform. We explain the edge preference analytically from the Majorana-dimer structure: a bulk impurity rearranges two neighboring dimers, while an edge impurity affects only one. Disorder competes with this clean edge bias and can pin the impurity in the bulk. Thus, the impurity is sensitive to the regime of the host system, although we do not find a strict one-to-one correspondence between the impurity localization pattern and the host topology. Instead, the disorder-averaged behavior suggests only an indirect correlation between impurity localization and the underlying phase of the chain.

</details>


### [81] [Many-body quantum geometric effects and entanglement at the 3D metal-insulator quantum phase transition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21648)
*Jason Y. Yan,Xiaoyu Guo,R. Bhandia,T. F. Rosenbaum,Natalia Drichko,N. P. Armitage*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper shows that the first negative moment of optical conductivity—linked to quantum Fisher information and multipartite entanglement—probes quantum geometry across the 3D metal-insulator transition in phosphorus-doped silicon. A quantum geometric length ℓ is extracted, which jumps discontinuously to infinity at the critical point rather than diverging continuously, revealing UV-dominated sum rules and providing a quantum foundation for the Herzfeld metallization criterion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demonstrate that quantum geometry can be experimentally probed via optical conductivity in disordered interacting systems, specifically across the metal-insulator transition, and to understand how quantum geometric quantities behave at criticality.

Method: Experimental measurement of the first negative moment of optical conductivity in phosphorus-doped silicon across the metal-insulator transition; extraction of a quantum geometric length ℓ from the data; analysis of its scaling behavior relative to correlation length and dielectric constant.

Result: The quantum geometric length ℓ coincides with the Bohr radius far from the transition, enhances approaching the critical point, but exhibits a discontinuous jump to infinity rather than continuous divergence. This UV domination in 3D prevents sensitivity to critical fluctuations.

Conclusion: Quantum geometry provides a foundation for the Herzfeld metallization criterion; the quantum geometric corrected Clausius-Mossotti description better captures the diverging dielectric response; the sum rule is UV-dominated in 3D, making it insensitive to critical fluctuations.

Abstract: Quantum geometry has emerged as a unifying concept across condensed matter physics, underlying phenomena from nonlinear topological response to flat-band superconductivity. While usually formulated within band theory, quantum geometry remains meaningful in disordered interacting systems~\cite{resta1999electron}. Here we show that the first negative moment of the optical conductivity -- proportional to the zero temperature quantum Fisher information as a bound on the multipartite entanglement -- provides an experimental probe of quantum geometry across the three-dimensional metal-insulator quantum phase transition in phosphorus-doped silicon. We extract a quantum geometric length $\ell$ that characterizes the local wavefunctions. Far from the transition, this length is almost coincident with the Bohr radius of the hydrogenic phosphorus donors, reflecting their atomic-scale quantum geometry. Approaching the transition, $\ell$ is enhanced, but does not diverge continuously like a correlation length; it jumps discontinuously to infinity at the critical point. This reflects the UV domination of the sum rule in three dimensions that renders it insensitive to the critical fluctuations driving the diverging dielectric constant and correlation length. Its enhancement demonstrates a ``puffing" of the donor polarizability volume of quantum geometric origin, which yields a quantum geometric corrected Clausius-Mossotti description in closer agreement with the diverging dielectric response and provides a quantum mechanical foundation for the century-old Herzfeld metallization criterion.

</details>


### [82] [Trial wavefunction for fractional quantum spin Hall insulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21652)
*Omri Lesser,Chao-Ming Jian*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper proposes and evaluates a variational wavefunction for a fractional quantum spin Hall state with Z_4 topological order in conjugate Landau levels, arising from condensation of an anyonic exciton in a pair of conjugate Moore-Read Pfaffian states. Using Monte Carlo sampling, the Z_4 state is shown to be energetically favorable over decoupled Pfaffian states and an exciton condensate in a sizable region of parameter space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fermions with opposite spins occupying half-filled conjugate Chern bands exhibit distinct interaction physics from multi-component Landau-level counterparts due to unavoidable inter-species collisions that preclude Halperin-type wavefunctions. Previous twisted MoTe₂ experiments suggested a fractional spin Hall conductance of 1/2, and the Z_4 topological order was identified as the minimal topological order compatible with charge conservation, S_z conservation, time-reversal symmetry, and this conductance.

Method: The construction is based on condensing an anyonic exciton formed by neutral fermionic excitations in a decoupled pair of a Moore-Read Pfaffian state and its conjugate. By coupling chiral and anti-chiral Ising conformal field theories for the two spin species, a variational mass parameter is introduced that captures inter-species s-wave pairing of composite fermions alongside intra-species p-wave pairing. Energetics are assessed via Monte Carlo sampling on a spherical geometry, with explicit evaluation of kinetic energy penalties from Landau-level mixing.

Result: The phase diagram reveals that the proposed Z_4 state becomes energetically favorable in a sizable region of parameter space, over both the decoupled pair of conjugate Pfaffian states and an alternative exciton condensate state.

Conclusion: The results provide a concrete microscopic wavefunction realization of the Z_4 fractional quantum spin Hall phase and propose a route to constructing additional families of such states.

Abstract: Fermions with opposite spins occupying half-filled conjugate Chern bands exhibit interaction physics distinct from their multi-component Landau-level counterparts with the same chirality. This is largely due to unavoidable inter-species collisions that preclude the Halperin-type wavefunctions available in multi-component Landau levels. In this work, we propose and evaluate a variational wavefunction for a fractional quantum spin Hall state with Z_4 topological order in a pair of conjugate Landau levels. This Z_4 topological order has previously been shown to be the minimal topological order compatible with charge conservation, $S_z$ conservation, time-reversal symmetry, and the fractional spin Hall conductance 1/2 suggested by previous twisted MoTe$_2$ experiments. Our construction is based on the condensation of an anyonic exciton formed by the neutral fermionic excitations in a decoupled pair of Moore-Read Pfaffian state and its conjugate. By coupling the chiral and anti-chiral Ising conformal field theories associated with the two spin species, we introduce a variational mass parameter in the Z_4 trial wavefunction that captures the inter-spin-species s-wave pairing of composite fermions alongside the intra-spin-species p-wave pairing. We assess the energetics of this trial state using Monte Carlo sampling on a spherical geometry. Because the coupled state intrinsically involves Landau-level mixing, we explicitly evaluate the resulting kinetic energy penalty. Our phase diagram reveals that the proposed Z_4 state becomes energetically favorable in a sizable region of parameter space, over both the decoupled pair of conjugate Pfaffian states and an alternative exciton condensate state. These results provide a concrete microscopic wavefunction realization of this Z_4 fractional quantum spin Hall phase, and propose a route to constructing additional families of such states.

</details>


### [83] [Quantum oscillations and Dirac dispersion in tunable kagome lattice Lu$_{1-y}$Y$_y$(Nb$_{1-x}$Ta$_x$)$_6$Sn$_6$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21694)
*Keenan Avers,Phineas Sobel,Lochlan Joyce,Jared Dans,Prathum Saraf,Ram Kumar,Shanta Saha,Peter Zavalij,Johnpierre Paglione*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper reports the synthesis and characterization of the Lu$_{1-y}$Y$_y$(Nb$_{1-x}$Ta$_x$)$_6$Sn$_6$ double-alloy system, revealing CDW suppression, quantum oscillations, and Dirac-like dispersion through Fermi surface evolution across the alloy series.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kagome lattice systems exhibit symmetry-protected band structure features (flat bands, van Hove singularities, Dirac/Weyl points) that enable strongly correlated electron physics. The authors are motivated by the rich properties and CDW evolution across the 1-6-6 series of compounds.

Method: Single crystal growth using a novel technique producing high-quality crystals with extended length along the crystallographic $c$-axis; electrical transport, magnetoresistance, and magnetization measurements; analysis of Fermi surfaces, effective masses, and quasiparticle dispersion through the alloy series.

Result: Gradual suppression of CDW order with Y substitution; observation of quantum oscillations throughout the series; decrease in Fermi surface size with both substitutions; scaling between effective mass and Fermi wavevector suggesting Dirac-like dispersion.

Conclusion: The double-alloy system enables fine-tuning of crystallographic, ground state, and electronic dispersion properties with minimal disorder impact, providing a pathway to further understand kagome lattice physics and its novel quantum states.

Abstract: Kagome lattice crystal systems present interesting symmetry-protected band structure features such as flat bands, van Hove singularities, and linearly dispersing Dirac/Weyl points that provide a rich playground for strongly correlated electron physics. Motivated by the rich properties and charge density wave evolution through the 1-6-6 series of compounds, we present our results in single crystal growth and characterization of the of Lu$_{1-y}$Y$_y$(Nb$_{1-x}$Ta$_x$)$_6$Sn$_6$ double-alloy system, including evolution of the charge density wave transition, electrical transport behavior and resultant phase diagrams. Using a novel growth technique, the synthesis of high quality crystals with extended length along the crystallographic $c$-axis allows us to follow the gradual suppression of charge density wave (CDW) order with Y substitution, and observe quantum oscillations in both magnetoresistance and magnetization throughout the series. We review the evolution of Fermi surfaces, effective masses and quasiparticle dispersion through the alloy series, revealing a decrease in size of Fermi surfaces that trends with both substitutions, and a scaling between effective mass and Fermi wavevector that suggests a regime with Dirac-like dispersion. The ability to fine-tune crystallographic, ground state and electronic dispersion properties of the \lit\ system with minimal impact of disorder opens a path torward further understanding the nature of the kagome lattice and its novel states and interactions.

</details>


### [84] [Spectroscopic fingerprints of a ferroaxial charge density wave](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21872)
*Jiangchang Zheng,Zhongyi Zhang,Fazhi Yang,Josh Leeman,Luanjing Li,Zihan Lin,Zijian Fei,Tianhao Guo,Siyu Heng,Xin Liang,Leslie M. Schoop,Junzhang Ma,Hoi Chun Po,Berthold Jäck*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Using ARPES and STM-based quasiparticle interference mapping with atomic scattering centers, the authors reveal a mixed inter-orbital charge density wave with ferroaxial component (breaking all vertical mirror symmetries) in LaTe₃, establishing a spectroscopic pathway for characterizing hidden multi-component electronic orders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While ferroaxial CDWs (hidden orders arising from charge-orbital coupling) have been reported in rare-earth tritellurides, spectroscopic insight into their underlying electronic structure and explicit order parameter symmetry has remained elusive.

Method: Linearly polarized ARPES mapped spectral gaps across the reconstructed Fermi surface; STM-based quasiparticle interference (QPI) mapping, enhanced by selective deposition of atomic scattering centers, probed orbital character; QPI analysis was interpreted in terms of order parameter symmetry within the orbital subspace.

Result: ARPES revealed a complex landscape of spectral gaps; QPI directly showed an inter-orbital CDW with mixed pₓ-p_z orbital character; analysis suggests a mixed CDW phase with substantial ferroaxial component breaking all vertical mirror symmetries.

Conclusion: The work establishes a powerful spectroscopic pathway using scattering off individual atoms to identify and characterize hidden, multi-component electronic orders in quantum materials via STM and ARPES.

Abstract: Unconventional charge density waves (CDWs) with complex order parameters can host exotic collective modes and non-trivial topologies. They have emerged as a new frontier in the study of quantum matter. Recent experiments on rare-earth tritellurides have reported evidence for a ferroaxial CDW through the detection of characteristic Raman modes. This phase, often regarded as a hidden order, has been recognized to arise from the coupling between charge and orbital degrees of freedom in these materials. Yet, spectroscopic insight into its underlying electronic structure and the explicit form of its order parameter symmetry has remained elusive. Here, we present results from linearly polarized angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) and scanning tunneling microscopy (STM) measurements of the CDW phase in LaTe$_3$. Our ARPES measurements reveal a complex landscape of spectral gaps across the reconstructed Fermi surface, while our STM-based quasiparticle interference (QPI) mapping, enhanced through the selective deposition of atomic scattering centers, directly reveals an inter-orbital CDW with mixed $p_x$-$p_z$ orbital character. The detailed analysis of the QPI characteristics in terms of the order parameter symmetry within the orbital subspace of the Fermi surface suggests a mixed CDW phase with substantial ferroaxial component, which breaks all vertical mirror symmetries. More broadly, our work establishes a powerful spectroscopic pathway, based on scattering off individual atoms, for identifying and characterizing hidden, multi-component electronic orders in quantum materials using STM and ARPES measurements.

</details>


### [85] [Magneto-ionic control of topological transport in SrRuO3 via band topology engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21999)
*Xuanchi Zhou,Xiaohui Yao,Xiaomei Qiao,Guowei Zhou,Wenjing Huo,Shuang Li,Huihui Ji,Xiaohong Xu*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates magneto-ionic control (protonation/oxygen vacancies) to dynamically engineer topological Hall and anomalous Hall effects in SrRuO₃ ferromagnetic films, enabling reversible switching of Hall anomalies and disentangling THE from AHE through band filling control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Active, reversible control over topological transport phenomena (AHE and THE) in ferromagnets is a central goal in spintronics, but disentangling their respective contributions to the net Hall effect remains challenging.

Method: Magneto-ionic control via controllable protonation or oxygen vacancy incorporation in 4d-orbital SrRuO₃ films, enabling band filling control that shifts the Fermi level relative to avoided band crossings.

Result: Tunable reversal temperature of AHE polarity was achieved; hump-like Hall anomalies emerged through extensive ionic doping and could be reversibly switched regardless of AHE polarity, indicating THE driven by broken inversion symmetry rather than two-channel AHE.

Conclusion: Magneto-ionic control provides a viable Berry curvature engineering knob for on-demand control of topological transports in strong SOC ferromagnets, enabling low-power, reconfigurable all-oxide spintronic devices.

Abstract: The interplay between spin-orbit coupling (SOC) and nontrivial band topology in ferromagnets gives rise to a rich landscape of topological transport phenomena such as anomalous Hall effect (AHE) and topological Hall effect (THE). One central goal in modern spintronics lies in the realization of the active control over topological transport phenomena in a reversible fashion, while unambiguously disentangling respective contributions of THE and AHE to the net Hall effect remains a formidable challenge. Here we establish magneto ionic control as a powerful paradigm for dynamically engineering topological transports in a 4d-orbital SrRuO3 system with sizable SOC and itinerant ferromagnetism. Harnessing controllable protonation or oxygen vacancy incorporation, the Fermi-level upshift relative to avoided band crossings are realized through band filling control, giving rise to tunable reversal temperature of AHE polarity. Of particular note is the emergence of hump like Hall anomalies through extensive ionic doping that can be reversibly switched, irrespective of AHE polarity, providing evidence for a THE signal driven by broken inversion symmetry rather than a two channel AHE. Our findings provide a viable tuning knob for Berry curvature engineering, enabling on demand control of topological transports in strong SOC ferromagnets for low power, reconfigurable all oxide spintronic devices.

</details>


### [86] [Field-tunable quadruple-$Q$ states driven by momentum-space frustration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22133)
*S. Hayami*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper studies multiple-Q magnetism in itinerant electron systems using a minimal momentum-space spin model on a square lattice. It identifies various single-Q, double-Q, and quadruple-Q states under an out-of-plane magnetic field, revealing rich internal structures in the quadruple-Q manifold with distinct phase locking, amplitude distribution, and noncoplanarity, leading to diverse real-space textures and scalar spin chirality patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and classify the rich variety of multiple-Q spin textures (especially quadruple-Q states) in centrosymmetric itinerant magnets, and to establish a general framework for stabilizing higher-order spin crystals via momentum-space frustration and biquadratic coupling.

Method: A minimal momentum-space spin model on a square lattice with four symmetry-related ordering wave vectors, including bilinear and biquadratic interactions under an out-of-plane magnetic field, studied via simulated annealing to obtain the field-dependent phase diagram.

Result: Successive transitions among single-Q, double-Q, and multiple inequivalent quadruple-Q states were identified. The quadruple-Q manifold shows rich internal structures: states sharing the same wave vectors differ in phase locking, amplitude distribution, and noncoplanarity, leading to distinct real-space textures and scalar spin chirality patterns.

Conclusion: Momentum-space frustration and biquadratic coupling provide an efficient route to stabilizing diverse quadruple-Q spin crystals, offering a general framework for higher-order spin textures in centrosymmetric itinerant magnets.

Abstract: Multiple-$Q$ magnetism in itinerant electron systems enables complex spin crystals and noncoplanar textures even in centrosymmetric settings. We study a minimal momentum-space spin model on a square lattice with four symmetry-related ordering wave vectors, including bilinear and biquadratic interactions under an out-of-plane magnetic field. Using simulated annealing, we obtain the field-dependent phase diagram and identify successive transitions among single-$Q$, double-$Q$, and multiple inequivalent quadruple-$Q$ states. The quadruple-$Q$ manifold exhibits rich internal structures: the states sharing the same wave vectors differ in phase locking, amplitude distribution, and noncoplanarity, leading to distinct real-space textures and scalar spin chirality patterns. Our results demonstrate that momentum-space frustration and biquadratic coupling provide an efficient route to stabilizing diverse quadruple-$Q$ spin crystals, offering a general framework for higher-order spin textures in centrosymmetric itinerant magnets.

</details>


### [87] [Van Hove Singularity and Phase Instability: Exploring the Role of Electron Correlation in the Magnetic Behavior of $\mathrm{Fe}_{16}\mathrm{N}_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22256)
*Peter Stoeckl,Przemyslaw Wojciech Swatek,Jian-Ping Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper investigates the magnetic properties of α''-Fe₁₆N₂ using GGA+U DFT, showing that Hubbard parameters significantly affect calculated saturation magnetization and magneto-crystalline anisotropy by revealing a van Hove singularity near the Fermi level linked to phase instability, providing a framework to reconcile theory with experiment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fe₁₆N₂ is a promising rare-earth-free permanent magnet candidate, but first-principles calculations have failed to consistently reproduce its experimentally observed high saturation magnetization and magneto-crystalline anisotropy.

Method: Density Functional Theory under the GGA+U framework, systematically varying Hubbard parameters U and J to study their effect on the electronic structure and magnetic properties of Fe₁₆N₂.

Result: The electronic structure is highly sensitive to U and J; a van Hove singularity near the Fermi level is uncovered, which is tied to structural/thermal phase instability. Tuning U adjusts M_s and MCA toward experimental values and reveals the electronic mechanism behind metastability.

Conclusion: The correlation-driven magnetic behavior of Fe₁₆N₂ can be understood through the GGA+U framework, offering a pathway to optimize its stability and performance for practical permanent magnet applications.

Abstract: The ordered iron nitride phase $α''-\mathrm{Fe}_{16}\mathrm{N}_2$ is a promising candidate for environment-friendly, rare-earth-free permanent magnets due to its demonstrated giant saturation magnetization ($M_s$). However, first-principles electronic-structure calculations have struggled to consistently reproduce experimentally-observed high $M_s$, and have yielded highly variable magneto-crystalline anisotropy (MCA) values. In this work, we employ Density Functional Theory under the GGA+$U$ framework to study the effect of the Hubbard parameters $U$ and $J$ on the magnetic properties of $\mathrm{Fe}_{16}\mathrm{N}_2$. We demonstrate that the electronic structure exhibits high sensitivity to these parameters, specifically uncovering a van Hove singularity near the Fermi level ($E_F$), inherently tied to the material's structural and thermal phase instability. By linking this topological anomaly to the calculated magnetic properties, we demonstrate that the selection of $U$ not only tunes $M_s$ and MCA energy towards experimental values but also reveals an underlying electronic mechanism potentially responsible for the phase's metastability. This provides a framework for understanding the correlation-driven magnetic behavior of $\mathrm{Fe}_{16}\mathrm{N}_2$ and offers a pathway for optimizing its stability and performance in practical applications.

</details>


### [88] [Magnetovolume effect in SrRu1-xCoxO3 (x = 0.0, 0.05)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22257)
*M. Manikandan,R. Mahendiran*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Polycrystalline SrRu1-xCoxO3 and CaRuO3 were rapidly synthesized via microwave irradiation (<1 hour), exhibiting ferromagnetic transitions, negative magnetoresistance, and invar-like thermal expansion. The magnetovolume effect reaches ~60 ppm near TC and is linked to competition between RuO6 octahedral tilting and spin-orbit interaction from Co doping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the rapid microwave synthesis of SrRuO3-based perovskites and explore the magnetovolume effect (magnetostriction and thermal expansion) in relation to Co doping, which has not been systematically studied in these materials.

Method: Microwave-assisted solid-state synthesis (<1 hour) of polycrystalline SrRu1-xCoxO3 (x=0, 0.05) and CaRuO3; characterization via magnetic, magnetoresistance, linear thermal expansion, and magnetostriction measurements.

Result: Microwave-synthesized SrRuO3 shows ferromagnetic TC=160 K, metallic resistivity, and negative magnetoresistance comparable to conventional samples. Invar-like spontaneous expansion in the ferromagnetic state; positive magnetovolume effect with isotropic expansion under field. Volume magnetostriction: 40 ppm at 10 K/50 kOe, peaking at ~60 ppm near TC. Co doping suppresses spontaneous thermal expansion and introduces anisotropic magnetostriction at low T, recovering isotropic behavior above 80 K.

Conclusion: The magnetovolume effect in SrRu1-xCoxO3 arises from competition between robust RuO6 octahedral tilting/rotation and spin-orbit interaction of doped Co2+ ions.

Abstract: Polycrystalline SrRu1-xCoxO3 (x = 0.0, 0.05) and CaRuO3 were rapidly synthesized (< 1 hour) by microwave irradiation of oxide powders, and their magnetic, magnetoresistance, thermal expansion, and magnetostriction properties were investigated. The microwave-synthesised SrRuO3 exhibits a ferromagnetic transition at TC = 160 K, metallic-type resistivity, and negative magnetoresistance, with magnitudes comparable to those of a sample synthesized by conventional heating over 24 hours. Upon lowering the temperature from 300 K, the linear thermal expansion shows a transition from the usual contraction in the paramagnetic state to spontaneous expansion in the ferromagnetic state (invar-like effect). The application of an external magnetic field at a fixed temperature results in isotropic expansion of the length, implying a positive magnetovolume effect. The volume magnetostriction is 40 ppm at 10 K in a magnetic field of 50 kOe, and it reaches a maximum value of 60 ppm close to TC. The spontaneous thermal expansion is diminished in SrRu0.95Co0.05O3. While the magnetostriction is anisotropic at 10 K, the isotropic behaviour is recovered above 80 K, and the maximum value of the positive magnetovolume is comparable to that of the parent compound. Our results suggest that the magnetovolume effect in SrRu1-xCoxO3 is related to competition between robust tilting/rotation of RuO6 octahedra and spin-orbit interaction of the doped Co2+ ions

</details>


### [89] [Evolution of anisotropic magnetostriction in LaMn1-xCoxO3 (x= 0.1-0.9)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22268)
*M. Manikandan,R. Mahendiran*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: LaMn1-xCoxO3 (x=0.1-0.9) samples were synthesized via microwave irradiation. Anisotropic magnetostriction dominates over volume magnetostriction, peaking at 1221 ppm for x=0.5, attributed to high-spin Co2+ ions with non-zero orbital moment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the magnetic and magnetostrictive properties of LaMn1-xCoxO3 polycrystalline samples over a wide composition range, focusing on the origin of large anisotropic magnetostriction.

Method: Microwave irradiation synthesis of polycrystalline LaMn1-xCoxO3 (x=0.1-0.9); measurement of parallel and perpendicular magnetostriction to extract anisotropic and volume contributions.

Result: λ_anis >> λ_vol for all compositions; λ_anis peaks at 1221 ppm at x=0.5 (10 K, 50 kOe); composition dependence is asymmetric about x=0.5; saturation magnetization decreases monotonically with an abrupt change at x=0.6.

Conclusion: The largest anisotropic magnetostriction at x=0.5 is due to high-spin Co2+ ions with non-zero orbital moment; composition dependence arises from changes in structure, valence states, and magnetic interactions.

Abstract: Polycrystalline samples of LaMn1-xCoxO3 series over a wide compositional range (x = 0.1 - 0.9) were synthesized by microwave irradiation of oxide precursors and their magnetic and magnetostrictive properties were investigated. Magnetostrictions parallel (l_par) and perpendicular (l_per) to the applied magnetic field were measured to estimate anisotropic(l_anis) and volume magnetostrictions(l_vol). In all the compositions, l-par (l_per) is negative (positive) and (l_anis) >> (l_vol), suggesting dominance of anisotropic lattice distortion under a magnetic field. The value of l_anis at 10 K for H = 50 kOe initially increases with x from 178 ppm for x = 0.1 to a maximum of 1221 ppm at x = 0.5 before decreasing for higher x. The composition dependence of magnetostriction is asymmetric about x = 0.5, the decrease for x > 0.5 is more steeper than for x < 0.5 whereas saturation magnetization decreases monotonically with increasing x except for an abrupt change at x = 0.6. The largest anisotropic magnetostriction observed for x = 0.5 is attributed to the presence of high-spin Co2+ ions with a non-zero orbital moment in the maximum fraction whereas Mn3+/4+ or Co3+ ions play minor roles. The composition dependence of magnetostriction is suggested to arise from changes in the structure, valence states of Mn and Co ions and magnetic interactions among them.

</details>


### [90] [Neural Polaron: Learning Quasiparticle Operators in Quantum Many-Body Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22644)
*Shang-Shun Zhang*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el
