TL;DR: The paper introduces a neural polaron ansatz that represents quasiparticle excitations via neural many-body operators acting on a ground state, enabling accurate computation of dynamical properties (e.g., magnon dispersions) in quantum many-body systems without learning independent excited-state wave functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding dynamical properties of quantum many-body systems is challenging because excitations require information beyond the ground-state wave function.

Method: The method parameterizes a local dressing operator through a compact neural head on the feature map of a pretrained ground-state network, building in translation symmetry, momentum resolution, and quasiparticle locality while separating ground-state correlations from excitation-specific dressing.

Result: Benchmarked on the square-lattice J1-J2 Heisenberg model, the method accurately reproduces magnon dispersions and spectral weights over a broad range of frustration, capturing the (π,0) anomaly and its progressive softening with increasing J2/J1.

Conclusion: Neural operators provide a physically transparent route for extending neural quantum states from ground-state properties to dynamical response.

Abstract: Understanding dynamical properties of quantum many-body systems remains a central challenge because excitations generally require information beyond a ground-state wave function. Here we introduce a neural polaron ansatz that represents quasiparticle excitations by neural many-body operators acting on a correlated ground state. Instead of learning an independent excited-state wave function, the method parameterizes a local dressing operator through a compact neural head defined on the feature map of a pretrained ground-state network. This operator-based construction builds in translation symmetry, momentum resolution, and quasiparticle locality, while separating ground-state correlations from excitation-specific dressing. We benchmark the method on the square-lattice $J_1$-$J_2$ Heisenberg model, where it accurately reproduces magnon dispersions and spectral weights over a broad range of frustration. In particular, it captures nontrivial many-body features including the $(π,0)$ anomaly and its progressive softening with increasing $J_2/J_1$. These results establish neural operators as a physically transparent route for extending neural quantum states from ground-state properties to dynamical response.

</details>


### [91] [Isometrization of Tensor Network States via Gauge Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22816)
*Zhiyu Jiang,Hiroshi Ueda*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper introduces a gauge-propagation method to convert generic tensor-network states into isometric tensor-network states (isoTNS) with a prescribed orthogonality center. In 1D it's exact via QR/SVD, but in higher dimensions residual factors are non-separable. The authors approximate local tensors/clusters by an isometric factor plus product of output-leg factors, absorbing residuals into neighbors. Refinement through more terms or larger clusters reduces errors. Benchmarks on random tensors and Kitaev spin liquid loop-gas tensors demonstrate precision improvements, positioning this as a building block for variational isoTNS algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an approximate method for converting generic tensor-network states into isometric tensor-network states (isoTNS) in higher dimensions, where exact isometrization via QR/SVD fails due to non-separable residual factors.

Method: A gauge-propagation approach that approximates a local tensor (or contracted local cluster) by structured terms: an isometric factor multiplied by a tensor product of output-leg factors. The isometric factor is retained locally; the output-leg factors are absorbed into neighboring tensors. Truncation is controlled by the number of retained terms or the size of the local cluster.

Result: On random tensors and Kitaev spin liquid loop-gas tensors, refinement (more terms or larger clusters) reduces both local residuals and accumulated propagation errors. With two structured terms, the local residual reaches numerical precision for loop-gas tensors. Enlarging clusters from 2-in-2-out to 4-in-2-out and 6-in-2-out lowers truncation and accumulated errors in finite honeycomb gauge propagation.

Conclusion: Propagation-compatible local decomposition is a useful building block for approximate isometrization and can serve as an initializer or preconditioner for variational isoTNS algorithms.

Abstract: We introduce a gauge-propagation approach for approximately converting generic tensor-network states into an isometric tensor-network state form with a prescribed orthogonality center. In one dimension, this propagation is exact because the non-isometric factor produced by a QR or singular-value decomposition is supported on a single virtual bond. In higher-dimensional networks, however, a local step can have several outgoing directions, and the residual factor is generally not separable into independent single-bond contributions. We address this local obstruction by approximating a local tensor, or a contracted local cluster, by structured terms consisting of an isometric factor multiplied by a tensor product of output-leg factors. The isometric factor is retained at the current site or cluster, while the output-leg factors are absorbed into neighboring tensors along the propagation directions. This construction provides a local truncation criterion for gauge propagation and a practical route to refinement by increasing the number of retained terms or enlarging the local cluster. Benchmarks on random tensors and on the loop-gas tensor representation of the Kitaev spin liquid show that this refinement reduces both local residuals and accumulated propagation errors. For the loop-gas tensor, two structured terms reduce the local residual to numerical precision, and enlarging the local object from 2-in-2-out to 4-in-2-out and 6-in-2-out clusters lowers both local truncation errors and accumulated errors in finite honeycomb gauge propagation. These results identify propagation-compatible local decomposition as a useful building block for approximate isometrization and as a potential initializer or preconditioner for variational isoTNS algorithms.

</details>


### [92] [Pairing-induced Bloch oscillations in an interacting Kitaev chain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22284)
*E. S. Ma,Z. Song*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Strong nearest-neighbor interaction in the Kitaev chain suppresses hopping, enhances pairing, and creates a Wannier-Stark ladder, which maps to a transverse field Ising model on a zigzag lattice. This Stark state is a localized domain wall exhibiting Bloch oscillation without a longitudinal field, demonstrating emergent Stark many-body localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study the peculiar dynamics induced by nearest-neighbor (NN) interaction in the Kitaev chain, particularly how strong NN interaction leads to emergent Stark many-body localization.

Method: Use spin-fermion correspondence at the symmetry point to map the model to a transverse field Ising model on a zigzag lattice; perform numerical simulations of time-dependent observables.

Result: Strong NN interaction suppresses single-particle hopping, enhances pairing, and yields a Wannier-Stark ladder. The Wannier-Stark state corresponds to a localized domain wall between ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic phases, exhibiting Bloch oscillation even without a longitudinal field.

Conclusion: The findings provide an example of emergent Stark many-body localization, verified by numerical simulations.

Abstract: We study the peculiar dynamics of the Kitaev chain induced by nearest-neighbor (NN) interaction. We show that a strong NN interaction suppresses single-particle hopping but enhances pairing, resulting in a Wannier-Stark ladder. Based on the spin-fermion correspondence at the symmetry point, the model maps to a transverse field Ising model on a zigzag lattice, providing a clear physical picture and guiding experimental verification. The Wannier-Stark state corresponds to a localized domain wall between ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic phases. It exhibits Bloch oscillation even in the absence of a longitudinal field, in contrast to previous works. Numerical simulations of time-dependent observables verify these conclusions. Our findings provide an example demonstrating emergent Stark many-body localization.

</details>


### [93] [Strain-tuned orbital-dependent electronic correlations in FeTe thin films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22799)
*Hyunjee Song,Sangjae Lee,Keun-Yeol Park,Jaehyun Park,Suyoung Lee,Yeonjae Lee,Jinyoung Kim,Jaeung Lee,Celesta S. Chang,Younsik Kim,Changyoung Kim*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This study uses tensile strain on FeTe thin films to investigate orbital-selective Mott phase (OSMP) and reveals strain-induced effects distinct from chemical doping, showing spectral weight transfer between d_xy and d_z^2 orbitals via ARPES, and highlighting the role of d_xz orbital electron hopping in defining electronic properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore emergent electronic behaviors in FeTe without chemical doping impurities by applying precise tensile strain, enabling investigation of orbital selectivity and orbital-selective Mott phase.

Method: Applying tensile strain to FeTe thin films and using angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) to observe orbital-dependent electronic interactions.

Result: Observed spectral weight transfer between d_xy and d_z^2 orbitals (evidence of OSMP), and revealed strain-induced effects on d_xz orbital evolution distinct from chemical doping, demonstrating direct correlation between epitaxial strain and electronic structure evolution.

Conclusion: Epitaxial strain directly correlates with electronic structure evolution in FeTe, with orbital-dependent electron hopping mechanisms playing a key role, offering a clean tuning method to study emergent correlated behaviors.

Abstract: Iron chalcogenides exhibit rich phenomena which are governed by orbital-dependent electronic interactions and strong electronic correlation. In particular, many studies have explored orbital selectivity in FeTe through Se doping. Here, applying tensile strain to thin films allows us to precisely control the system without other impurities that may arise from chemical doping to investigate the emergent behaviors in FeTe. Using angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy, we observe a spectral weight transfer between $d_{\rm xy}$ and $d_{\rm z^{2}}$ orbitals, evidence of an orbital-selective Mott phase (OSMP). Beyond OSMP, we reveal hitherto unobserved strain-induced effects, distinct from chemical doping. The evolution of $d_{\rm xz}$ orbital demonstrates how electron hopping mechanism plays an important role in defining the electronic properties of the system. Our findings highlight a direct correlation between epitaxial strain and the evolution of electronic structures in FeTe.

</details>


### [94] [Spiral spin liquid resilient to quantization in the frustrated honeycomb antiferromagnet GdZnPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23007)
*Xun Chen,Rui Bian,Yuqian Zhao,Haijun Liao,Weiqiang Yu,Yi Cui,Yuesheng Li*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: NMR study of the spin-7/2 honeycomb antiferromagnet GdZnPO reveals a spin-liquid state with persistent low-energy dynamics in intermediate fields (3-12 T), and subtle stripe order with strong fluctuations below ~3 T and ~0.25 K, demonstrating order-by-quantum-disorder is weak and the system preserves spin-liquid phenomenology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether the frustrated magnet GdZnPO exhibits a spin-liquid state or develops magnetic order via order-by-quantum-disorder, and to understand the role of quantum fluctuations in stabilizing exotic quantum phases in high-spin systems.

Method: Nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) measurements of local magnetization and spin dynamics in single-crystal GdZnPO as a function of temperature and magnetic field.

Result: In intermediate fields (3-12 T): spatially uniform magnetization and persistent low-energy spin dynamics down to 0.033 K with no detectable symmetry breaking—spectroscopic evidence for a spin-liquid state. Below ~3 T: weak stripe order emerges below ~0.25 K, but strong fluctuations persist with a nearly temperature-independent and unusually large spin-lattice relaxation rate at low temperatures.

Conclusion: Spin-7/2 quantization only weakly lifts the spiral degeneracy, leading to subtle magnetic order while preserving robust dynamics and spin-liquid phenomenology. GdZnPO is a promising platform for exploring spin liquids in high-spin frustrated magnets.

Abstract: Frustrated magnets host strong quantum fluctuations that can suppress conventional magnetic order and give rise to exotic quantum phases such as spin liquids. In some cases, however, quantum fluctuations lift classical degeneracies and stabilize ordered states via an order-by-quantum-disorder mechanism. The spin-7/2 honeycomb antiferromagnet GdZnPO has recently been proposed as a spiral spin-liquid candidate arising from cooperative fluctuations among a subextensively degenerate manifold of spiral states. Here, we investigate the local magnetization and spin dynamics in GdZnPO using nuclear magnetic resonance. In an intermediate field regime between $\sim$3 T and the saturation field ($\sim$12 T), we observe a spatially uniform magnetization and persistent low-energy spin dynamics down to 0.033 K, with no detectable symmetry breaking, providing spectroscopic evidence for a spin-liquid state. At lower fields below $\sim$3 T, a weak stripe order emerges below $\sim$0.25 K; however, strong fluctuations persist, as indicated by a nearly temperature-independent and unusually large spin-lattice relaxation rate in the low-temperature limit. Our results demonstrate that spin-7/2 quantization weakly lifts the spiral degeneracy, stabilizing subtle magnetic order while preserving robust dynamics and spin-liquid phenomenology. These findings establish GdZnPO as a promising platform for exploring spin liquids in high-spin frustrated magnets down to the lowest accessible temperatures.

</details>


### [95] [Angular-time evolution and edge-spin dynamics in the Haldane phase of the S=1 bilinear-biquadratic chain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23117)
*Takuma Kaise,Kouichi Okunishi*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper studies angular-time evolution (driven by entanglement Hamiltonian) in the S=1 bilinear-biquadratic chain's ground state. Using MPS, they compute angular-time spin correlations in the Haldane phase, identifying a dominant oscillation mode from near-degenerate entanglement spectrum due to Z2×Z2 symmetry. They also verify correspondence between this mode and effective edge-spin dynamics under a magnetic field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the dynamics generated by the entanglement Hamiltonian (angular time) in symmetry-protected topological phases, specifically how the nearly degenerate entanglement spectrum manifests in real-time correlation functions and relates to physical edge-spin dynamics.

Method: Matrix-product-state (MPS) representation of the ground state; calculation of angular-time (τ) spin correlation functions ⟨S_n^α(τ)S_{n'}^α(0)⟩; comparison of subsystem-size dependence of angular-time oscillation amplitudes with those of effective edge-spin dynamics under a uniform magnetic field.

Result: Identified a dominant oscillation mode in angular-time spin correlations in the Haldane phase, arising from the nearly two-fold-degenerate entanglement spectrum protected by Z2×Z2 symmetry. Numerical verification shows correspondence between this mode and the effective edge-spin dynamics when a magnetic field is applied.

Conclusion: Angular-time evolution captures the low-energy entanglement structure of the Haldane phase, and its dominant mode directly corresponds to the physical edge-spin dynamics, providing a connection between entanglement spectra and observable dynamical properties.

Abstract: We investigate the angular-time evolution -- a parameter-time evolution generated by the entanglement Hamiltonian -- for the bipartitioned ground state of the S=1 bilinear-biquadratic chain under the open boundary condition with the up edge spin. Using a matrix-product-state representation of the ground-state wavefunction, we calculate the angular-time spin correlation functions $\langle S_n^{α}(τ)S_{n'}^{α}(0)\rangle$ in the Haldane phase, and extract its dominant oscillation mode attributed to the nearly two-fold-degenerate entanglement spectrum associated with the $\mathbb{Z}_2 \times \mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetry. We also compute the effective edge-spin dynamics under a uniform magnetic field applied to the system part and numerically verify its correspondence to the dominant angular-time mode by precisely comparing the subsystem-size dependence of their amplitudes.

</details>


### [96] [Wess-Zumino terms in 0+1 SU(N) superspin systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23234)
*J. S. Morales,M. N. Kiselev*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: A self-contained introduction to Wess-Zumino (WZ) terms in SU(N) quantum systems, covering Berry phases, topology, and condensed-matter applications including SU(N) Heisenberg models, spin-orbital systems, and multipolar orders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a unified geometric and topological understanding of WZ terms in SU(N) symmetric quantum systems, bridging abstract mathematical structures with concrete condensed-matter physics applications such as spin-orbit coupled systems and multipolar phases.

Method: Begins with SU(2) spin coherent-state path integral and Berry phase as a WZ term; generalizes to higher SU(N) via superspin coherent-state construction on CP^{N-1} phase space; derives explicit local WZ terms for SU(3) and SU(4); provides appendices with algebraic dictionaries for physical embeddings.

Result: Establishes the geometric origin of topological terms via integral cohomology classes and Berry curvature as the first Chern class; develops explicit WZ term constructions for SU(3) and SU(4); connects abstract superspin formalism to concrete multipolar and spin-orbital systems.

Conclusion: WZ terms in SU(N) quantum systems provide a powerful unified framework for understanding geometric phases, topology, and exotic ordered phases in condensed matter, with explicit connections to experimentally relevant systems like spin-orbital and multipolar magnets.

Abstract: These notes present a self-contained introduction to Wess-Zumino (WZ) terms in quantum systems with $SU(N)$ symmetry, emphasizing the interplay between geometry, topology, and condensed-matter applications. We begin with the $SU(2)$ spin coherent-state path integral, where the Berry phase appears as a WZ term encoding the symplectic structure of the Bloch sphere. This example is then used to introduce the geometric origin of topological terms, their relation to integral cohomology classes, and the role of Berry curvature as the first Chern class of the canonical $U(1)$ bundle. We next discuss physical realizations in which such geometric terms affect dynamics, including adiabatic Berry phases and geometric quantum noise in magnetic quantum dots. A substantial part of the notes is devoted to the condensed-matter motivation for higher $SU(N)$ symmetries, covering $SU(N)$ Heisenberg models, $SU(4)$ spin-orbital and spin-pseudospin systems, multipolar exchange interactions, and higher-spin multipolar orders. Finally, we develop the 0+1-dimensional $SU(N)$ superspin coherent-state construction, identify the phase space with $CP^{N-1}$, and derive explicit local WZ terms for $SU(3)$ and $SU(4)$. The appendices provide algebraic dictionaries connecting the abstract superspin language with concrete physical embeddings, including multipolar generator bases and several useful $SU(4)$ parametrizations.

</details>


### [97] [Signatures of unconventional magnetism in the layered metallic ferromagnet LaCrSb$_3$ from ferromagnetic resonance spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23413)
*J. J. Abraham,S. Samanta,R. Kolay,V. Singh,R. Nath,B. Büchner,V. Kataev,A. Alfonsov*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper reports temperature-dependent XRD, magnetization, ESR, and FMR studies on LaCrSb₃, revealing strong magneto-elastic coupling at TC ≈ 126 K, short-range correlations above TC indicative of low-dimensional magnetism, and the presence of two magnetic sublattices (orthogonal ferro- and antiferromagnetic) explaining the strong spin canting and FMR modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the unusual electronic and magnetic properties of LaCrSb₃, including its large anomalous Hall effect, strong spin canting, and spin-reorientation transition, by investigating its structural and magnetic behavior through multi-technique experiments.

Method: Temperature-dependent X-ray diffraction, static magnetization measurements, electron spin resonance (ESR), and ferromagnetic resonance (FMR) experiments conducted over a wide range of frequencies, magnetic fields, and temperatures.

Result: No structural transition down to 15 K, but strong magneto-elastic coupling across TC ≈ 126 K. ESR shows quasi-static short-range correlations above TC (low-dimensional magnetism fingerprint). FMR frequency-field diagrams confirm two magnetic sublattices, consistent with an orthogonal ferro- and antiferromagnetic sublattice model.

Conclusion: The FMR results corroborate the proposed two-sublattice scenario for strong spin canting in LaCrSb₃, calling for further development of a microscopic model of its unconventional magnetism.

Abstract: LaCrSb$_{3}$ is a metallic ferromagnet with a layered crystal structure demonstrating intriguing electronic and magnetic properties, such as large anomalous Hall effect, strong canting of the spin lattice, and a peculiar spin-reorientation transition. Here, we report the results of the temperature-dependent x-ray diffraction, static magnetization, and in particular electron spin resonance (ESR) and ferromagnetic resonance (FMR) experiments carried out over a wide range of frequencies, magnetic fields, and temperatures. Though x-ray data reveals no structural transition down to 15 K, a strong magneto-elastic coupling is detected across the ferromagnetic transition at $T_{\rm C} \simeq 126$ K. ESR results indicate a presence of the quasi-static short-range correlations extending far above $T_{\rm C}$, which is a typical fingerprint of the low-dimensional magnetism. The frequency-field diagram of the FMR modes mapped below $T_{\rm C}$ strongly suggests presence of two magnetic sublattices in LaCrSb$_{3}$. A quantitative understanding of the FMR excitations was achieved within a phenomenological model of interacting orthogonal ferro- and antiferromagnetic sublattices which was earlier proposed to explain unusually strong spin canting observed by neutron diffraction [E.~Granado et al., Phys. Rev. Lett. 89, 107204 (2002)]. The FMR results corroborate this scenario and call for the development of the underlying microscopic model of unconventional magnetism in LaCrSb$_{3}$.

</details>


### [98] [Resolving support-mismatch by local basis rotation in variational Monte Carlo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23657)
*Jia-Lin Chen,Zhen Fan,Canhui Yan,Yantao Wu,Tao Xiang*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: A local basis-rotation sampling scheme resolves the support-mismatch problem in variational Monte Carlo calculations of real-time dynamics after a local charged-operator quench, enabling accurate long-time dynamics and dynamical structure factor calculations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time dynamics after a local quench by a charged operator are crucial for spectroscopic response functions but suffer from a support mismatch in variational Monte Carlo: projective action by a charged local operator causes exponentially many configurations to vanish, biasing estimators and freezing evolution at the first step.

Method: The support mismatch is identified as an artifact of the chosen sampling basis and is itself local. The missing support is restored by a local rotation of the sampling basis without altering the underlying variational dynamics. A local basis-rotation sampling scheme is proposed and integrated into existing variational Monte Carlo algorithms.

Result: Rotation sampling accurately captures long-time quantum dynamics, enabling VMC calculations of dynamical structure factors in 1D and unbiased local-operator quench dynamics in 2D. The resolution also extends beyond real-time dynamics to ground state VMC calculations.

Conclusion: The proposed local basis-rotation sampling scheme effectively solves the support-mismatch problem, enabling variational Monte Carlo calculations of real-time dynamics that were previously inaccessible.

Abstract: Real-time dynamics after a local quench by a charged operator encodes the response functions measured in spectroscopic experiments, yet they have long posed a challenge for variational Monte Carlo calculations. The obstacle is a support mismatch: the projective action by a charged local operator forces an exponentially large number of configurations to vanish, but these configurations may still contribute to the dynamics, biasing the estimators and freezing the evolution at the very first step. This difficulty is an artifact of the chosen sampling basis, and the support mismatch generated by a charged local operator is itself local. We demonstrate that the missing support can be restored by a local rotation of the sampling basis, without changing the underlying variational dynamics. We propose a local basis-rotation sampling scheme that resolves the support-mismatch problem and can be readily incorporated into existing variational Monte Carlo algorithms. Benchmarks show that rotation sampling accurately captures long-time quantum dynamics, enabling variational Monte Carlo calculations of dynamical structure factors in one dimension and unbiased local-operator quench dynamics in two dimensions. We also show that this resolution of the support-mismatch problem extends beyond real-time dynamics, and may also be helpful for ground state variational Monte Carlo calculations.

</details>


### [99] [Emergent Andreev Reflection from a Lattice Duality Defect](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23684)
*Atsushi Ueda,Tokiro Numasawa,Boris De Vos,Masataka Watanabe*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: A lattice duality defect in the transverse-field Ising chain implements an Andreev-like charge-conjugating boundary condition via a Majorana translation, connecting to the two-channel Kondo problem, monopole scattering, and Kitaev-chain SPT interfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To show that an Andreev-like charge-conjugating boundary condition can emerge from a purely lattice duality defect, without requiring a superconducting interface, and to provide a microscopic lattice interpretation of known boundary phenomena in the two-channel Kondo problem and monopole scattering.

Method: Starting from a Majorana representation of the transverse-field Ising chain, we construct a folded lattice model with a boundary Majorana impurity that implements a one-site translation of a staggered Majorana chain. This translation becomes a chiral fermion-parity defect in the continuum, flipping the left-moving Majorana mode while preserving the right-moving one.

Result: The sign flip of the left-moving Majorana mode, when recombined into a complex fermion in the folded geometry, yields an Andreev-like boundary condition. The lattice formulation microscopically interprets the Emery–Kivelson boundary of the two-channel Kondo problem and Maldacena–Ludwig monopole scattering, identifying the boundary as a Kitaev-chain SPT–gapless chain interface. The same defect also realizes an axial U(1)_A-symmetric charge-flip boundary.

Conclusion: Lattice duality defects can generate charge-conjugating (Andreev-like) boundary conditions without superconductivity, providing new microscopic insight into known boundary phenomena and SPT–gapless interfaces.

Abstract: Andreev reflection converts an incoming fermion into an outgoing hole and is usually tied to a superconducting interface. We show that an analogous charge-conjugating boundary condition emerges from a purely lattice duality defect. Starting from a Majorana representation of the transverse-field Ising chain, we construct a folded lattice model in which a boundary Majorana impurity implements a one-site translation of a staggered Majorana chain. In the continuum, this translation becomes a chiral fermion-parity defect: it flips the sign of the only left-moving Majorana mode while leaving the right-moving mode unchanged. When the two Majorana modes are recombined into a complex fermion in the folded geometry, this sign flip becomes the Andreev-like boundary condition. Our lattice formulation gives a microscopic interpretation of the Emery--Kivelson boundary of the two-channel Kondo problem and of Maldacena--Ludwig monopole scattering, while identifying the boundary as the interface between a Kitaev-chain SPT phase and a gapless chain. The same Majorana translation defect also provides a lattice realization of an axial $U(1)_A$-symmetric charge-flip boundary.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.dis-nn'></div>

# cond-mat.dis-nn [[Back]](#toc)

### [100] [Non-ergodicity effects in 1D localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20664)
*I. M. Suslov*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: The paper revises the understanding of the log-normal distribution of dimensionless Landauer resistance in 1D disordered systems, showing that non-ergodicity effects cause the distribution to depend on the order of limits (system size L → ∞ vs. number of realizations K → ∞), and that with finite K the distribution transforms into a delta-function where ensemble average equals typical value, explaining discrepancies between theory and numerical/physical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the discrepancy between theoretical predictions for the log-normal distribution parameters of the Landauer resistance and results from numerical and physical experiments, by accounting for non-ergodicity effects and the order of limiting transitions (L→∞ vs. K→∞).

Method: Analytical analysis of the order of limiting transitions (L→∞ and K→∞) in the distribution of dimensionless Landauer resistance for 1D disordered systems, comparing the log-normal distribution behavior under different limiting sequences and relating to universal conductance fluctuations.

Result: If K→∞ first, the log-normal distribution holds for all L when ρ>>1. If K is finite, as L→∞ the distribution becomes a delta-function, making the ensemble average approximately equal to the typical value, contrary to the standard log-normal picture.

Conclusion: Non-ergodicity effects are essential for understanding the difference between theoretical predictions for log-normal distribution parameters and the results of numerical and physical experiments, and the transformation of the distribution can be observed via experimental techniques related to universal conductance fluctuations.

Abstract: It is well-known that the dimensionless Landauer resistance ρof an 1D disordered system obeys the log-normal distribution. The average value <ρ> for such distribution is not representative, since it strongly differs from the typical value ρ_{typ} in a specific sample. In fact, this conclusion should be revised due to effects of non-ergodicity. If L is the system size, and $K$ is the number of realizations of a random potential, then a situation for L\to\infty, K\to \infty depends on the order of limiting transitions. If the limit K\to \infty is taken firstly, then the log-normal distribution is valid for all L, if the condition ρ>>1 is fulfilled. If the number of realizations K is restricted, then a situation for L\to \infty is effectively described by the delta-function distribution, and <ρ>\approxρ_{typ}$. Transformation of the log-normal distribution can be observed with the use of experimental technique developed in the context of the universal conductance fluctuations. Non-ergodicity effects are essential for understanding of the difference between the theoretical predictions for the parameters of the log-normal distribution and the results of numerical and physical experiments.

</details>


### [101] [Communication Heterogeneity and Collective Consensus in Neural Cellular Automata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21202)
*Nishit Singh*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: A Neural Cellular Automaton model shows that linguistic distance between communicating sub-populations slows consensus, causes mild divergence rather than full fragmentation, and that collectively trained protocols are robust to mismatch while homogeneous ones are not, resembling Ising relaxation and human group dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how consensus emerges when agents do not share a common communication protocol, moving beyond traditional models that assume a single shared language.

Method: Introduced "languages" as sub-populations in a Neural Cellular Automaton solving density classification, with tunable "linguistic distance" via translation between protocols, tested on ring and 2D grid topologies.

Result: Linguistic distance slows consensus; produces mild divergence (not full fragmentation); collectively trained rules are robust to mismatch while homogeneously trained ones are not; effects consistent across topologies and map to Ising relaxation dynamics.

Conclusion: Distance between communication protocols is a minimal, non-language-specific mechanism sufficient to produce consensus slowdown and partial divergence patterns observed in human group studies.

Abstract: Reaching global agreement from purely local interactions is a defining problem of collective intelligence, and most models of it assume that all agents share a single communication protocol. We ask what happens when they do not. Using a Neural Cellular Automaton in which a population of cells must solve the density classification task, agreeing on a global majority that no individual can observe, we introduce ``languages'' as sub-populations that read one another's messages through a translation with a tunable ``linguistic distance''. We find that linguistic distance slows consensus, that it produces mild divergence between groups rather than full fragmentation, and that a collective whose shared rule was trained under diverse protocols is robust to mismatch; a homogeneously trained one is not. The findings hold on both a ring and a two-dimensional grid, and admit a natural reading as Ising relaxation, in which a foreign-language region acts as a boundary defect that leaves the system in a higher-energy, partially ordered state. These patterns are qualitatively consistent with effects reported in human group studies, suggesting that distance between communication protocols is a minimal mechanism sufficient to produce them, without anything language-specific.

</details>


### [102] [Multifractality at the Integer Quantum Hall Transition: Acquittal of the Parabolic Law](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21679)
*Sen Mu,Abbas Ali Saberi,Martin R. Zirnbauer*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: The paper resolves the longstanding discrepancy between theoretical predictions and numerical simulations of multifractality at the integer quantum Hall transition. By discarding the Gaussian Free Field and Abelian Fusion Hypothesis, and accounting for non-Abelian effects with background charge insertions, the authors derive exact q-moments for point-contact eigenstates. Numerical tests on the Chalker-Coddington network model show precise agreement with a level-4 current algebra conformal symmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The exact form of the multifractality spectrum at the integer quantum Hall transition has been debated: conformal field theory predicts a parabolic law, but numerical simulations show deviations. This discrepancy motivates the work.

Method: The authors point out that powers of local wave intensity do not obey the Gaussian Free Field and Abelian Fusion Hypothesis assumed earlier. Instead, due to the non-Abelian nature of the underlying effective field theory, wave intensity correlators are dressed by insertions of background charge uniformly distributed in space. An exact expression for q-moments of point-contact eigenstates is derived and tested on the critical Chalker-Coddington network model on a rectangular torus.

Result: Numerical tests show precise agreement with the predictions of conformal symmetry realized as a level-4 current algebra, resolving the discrepancy between previous theory and simulations.

Conclusion: The multifractality at the integer quantum Hall transition is described by a non-Abelian effective field theory with level-4 current algebra conformal symmetry, yielding an exact multifractal spectrum that deviates from a simple parabola.

Abstract: Stationary wave functions at the integer quantum Hall transition are known to be multifractal, but the exact form of the multifractality spectrum has remained a subject of debate. While conformal field theory arguments predict a parabolic law, numerical simulations show deviations from parabolicity. We resolve this discrepancy by pointing out that powers of the local wave intensity fail to obey the Gaussian Free Field and Abelian Fusion Hypothesis assumed in earlier analysis. Rather, due to the non-Abelian nature of the underlying effective field theory, wave intensity correlators are dressed by insertions of background charge distributed uniformly in space. An exact expression for the $q$-moments of point-contact generated eigenstates is presented. Numerical tests are performed for the critical Chalker-Coddington network model on a rectangular torus. Our results are in precise agreement with the predictions of conformal symmetry realized as a level-4 current algebra.

</details>


### [103] [Exact Accumulated-Field Determined Steady States with Boundary-Controlled Relaxation in Dissipative Quantum Link Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22065)
*Yazhuang Miao,Weizh Ma,Yong Wang,Xiaolong Zhao,Xuexi Yi*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: In globally reciprocal dissipative quantum link chains, the steady state depends solely on an accumulated field from link-resolved asymmetries, while the nonzero Liouvillian spectrum can be altered by cyclic boundary conditions that preserve the steady-state matter occupations but change relaxation rates, with cyclic closure accelerating relaxation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether changing boundary conditions in open quantum lattice systems simultaneously alters both the steady state and the nonzero Liouvillian spectrum, or if these effects can be decoupled.

Method: The authors analyze a globally reciprocal dissipative quantum link chain, introducing an accumulated field from link-resolved dissipative asymmetries and applying a gauge-generated transformation to obtain exact symmetry-resolved steady states. They construct a reciprocal cyclic boundary condition that preserves matter occupations while modifying the nonzero Liouvillian spectrum, reducing the problem to effective exclusion processes in the strong-dissipation limit.

Result: The steady state is unaffected by cyclic boundary conditions in this reciprocal dissipative quantum link chain, while the nonzero Liouvillian spectrum changes, leading to faster relaxation under cyclic closure. In the strong-dissipation limit, the relaxation difference maps to a spectral comparison of open vs. cyclic effective exclusion processes.

Conclusion: Boundary-induced changes to the steady state and the Liouvillian spectrum can be decoupled in reciprocally dissipative quantum link chains, with cyclic boundaries accelerating relaxation without altering the steady-state matter occupation profile.

Abstract: In open quantum lattice systems, changing the boundary condition would appear to alter both the steady state and the nonzero Liouvillian spectrum. Here we show that these two boundary-induced changes do not necessarily occur together in a globally reciprocal dissipative quantum link chain. The steady state is determined by an accumulated field defined by link-resolved dissipative asymmetries, and a gauge-generated transformation built from this field gives exact symmetry-resolved steady states with nonuniform, accumulated-field-dependent reduced matter occupations. We then construct a reciprocal cyclic boundary condition that preserves these matter occupations while changing the nonzero Liouvillian spectrum. Consequently, open and cyclic chains relax to the same reduced matter steady-occupation profile with different Liouvillian gaps, with the cyclic closure accelerating relaxation. In the strong-dissipation limit, this relaxation difference can be reduced to a spectral comparison of effective exclusion processes with open and cyclic boundaries.

</details>


### [104] [Percolation of Zero-Weight Paths and the Shape of the Phase Boundary in the Two-Dimensional Random-Bond Ising Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22398)
*Amirhossein Manouchehri,Kirill Shtengel*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper investigates the connection between the low-temperature boundary of ferromagnetic ordering in the 2D ±J random-bond Ising model and a geometric transition called "zero-weight percolation," finding that the percolation of paths with equal numbers of +J and -J bonds suppresses ferromagnetism. Using dynamic programming and finite-size scaling, the critical disorder is identified at p_c = 0.1000(2) with associated exponents, and the ferromagnetic phase boundary is argued to be vertical below the Nishimori point.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the interplay between disorder and ferromagnetic order in the random-bond Ising model, particularly whether a purely geometric percolation criterion (zero-weight percolation) can explain the loss of ferromagnetism without invoking thermal effects.

Method: The authors use a dynamic-programming algorithm combined with finite-size scaling to identify the critical disorder where zero-weight paths first percolate, extracting critical exponents and fractal dimension.

Result: The critical disorder is p_c = 0.1000(2), with exponents ν = 1.26(1), β/ν = 0.85(1), γ/ν = 0.264(5), and fractal dimension d_f ≈ 1.11. This p_c is below previously reported values for the loss of ferromagnetic order at zero temperature and the Nishimori point.

Conclusion: The percolation transition studied provides a new purely geometrical perspective on the stability of ferromagnetic order in disordered spin systems, suggesting the ferromagnetic phase boundary is vertical below the Nishimori point in the (p,T) plane.

Abstract: We explore the connection between the low-temperature boundary of the ferromagnetic phase in the two-dimensional $\pm J$ random-bond Ising model, where antiferromagnetic bonds occur with probability $p$ and a geometric transition dubbed ``zero-weight percolation''. We argue that the onset of this percolation characterized by the emergence of a percolating path containing an equal number of $+J$ and $-J$ bonds is incompatible with ferromagnetic ordering. Due to its purely geometrical nature, this percolation criterion is a property of a disorder realization and is independent of the temperature, which in turn suggests that the ferromagnetic phase boundary is vertical below the Nishimori point in the $(p,T)$ plane. Using a dynamic-programming algorithm combined with finite-size scaling, we identify the critical disorder at which zero-weight paths first percolate as $p_c = 0.1000(2)$, and we extract the associated critical exponents $ν= 1.26(1)$, $β/ν= 0.85(1)$, $γ/ν= 0.264(5)$, and fractal dimension $d_f \approx 1.11$. The value of $p_c$ is below the previously reported values of the critical disorder strength corresponding to the loss of the ferromagnetic order, both at zero temperature and the Nishimori point. Nevertheless, we argue that the percolation transition studied in this paper is behind the loss of ferromagnetism and thus provides a new, purely geometrical perspective on the stability of ferromagnetic order in disordered spin systems.

</details>


### [105] [Scalable Physics-Inspired Transformers for Spin Glasses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22984)
*Lu Zhong,Wenli Duan,Jing Liu,Pan Zhang,Ying Tang*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: A physics-inspired transformer with sparse attention and spin-tailored positional embeddings achieves up to 100x speedup over vanilla VANS, enabling neural-network simulations of frustrated spin glasses (SK, 2D/3D EA models) to unprecedented sizes on a single GPU by leveraging FlashAttention for parallel ancestral sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing variational models for sampling Boltzmann distributions in frustrated spin glasses fail to benefit from increased scale (violating typical scaling laws) and suffer high computational costs on large systems, negating advantages over classical methods.

Method: Develops a physics-inspired transformer with interpretable sparse attention, spin-tailored positional embeddings, and FlashAttention for efficient parallel ancestral sampling.

Result: Achieves up to two orders of magnitude speedup over vanilla variational autoregressive networks, enabling simulation of spin-glass systems to unprecedented sizes on a single GPU; resolves full probability distributions, free energies, and overlap statistics across temperatures for SK, 2D and 3D EA models where existing ML methods fail at certain temperatures.

Conclusion: Establishes a scalable neural-network paradigm for frustrated spin-glass systems, overcoming both the scaling limitation and computational bottleneck of prior variational approaches.

Abstract: Efficient sampling of the Boltzmann distribution in frustrated spin glasses is central to statistical mechanics and combinatorial optimization. Despite advances in machine-learning-based approaches, two issues persist: limited understanding of why variational models fail to benefit from increased scale, unlike the monotonic scaling law of large language models; and high computational cost on large systems that negates advantages over classical sampling methods. Here, we develop a physics-inspired transformer with interpretable sparse attention and spin-tailored positional embeddings to address these challenges. By further leveraging FlashAttention for parallel ancestral sampling, it achieves up to two orders of magnitude speedup over vanilla variational autoregressive networks, enabling neural-network simulations of spin-glass systems to unprecedented sizes on a single GPU. It can resolve full probability distributions, free energies, and overlap statistics across temperatures, for Sherrington-Kirkpatrick and 2D or 3D Edwards-Anderson models, where existing machine-learning methods encounter limitations at certain temperatures. This framework thus establishes a scalable paradigm for frustrated spin-glass systems.

</details>


### [106] [Approximating velocity fields with planted attractors via Neural-ODEs for classification purposes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23550)
*Feliciano Giuseppe Pacifico,Duccio Fanelli,Lorenzo Buffoni,Lorenzo Chicchi,Diego Febbe,Raffaele Marino*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: Neural ODEs with planted equilibrium points are used for classification, where attractors represent target classes and the velocity field shapes basins of attraction to direct inputs to their correct destinations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To leverage Neural ODEs for classification tasks by embedding equilibrium points as class indicators, enabling a dynamical systems approach to pattern recognition.

Method: Neural ODEs are equipped with a curated set of equilibrium points (planted attractors) that correspond to target classes. The velocity field is learned via the universal approximation capability of the neural architecture to shape the dynamical landscape, defining basins of attraction that map initial conditions (inputs) to their appropriate attractor (class).

Result: The planted attractors successfully serve as class indicators, and the trained velocity field effectively directs each input toward its corresponding target attractor through the learned basins of attraction.

Conclusion: Equipping Neural ODEs with planted equilibrium points is an effective strategy for classification, transforming the problem into a dynamical system where inputs evolve toward class-specific attractors.

Abstract: In this work, Neural ODEs equipped with a curated collection of equilibrium points have been successfully employed for classification tasks.The planted attractors serve as indicators for the target classes, while the velocity field leveraging the universal approximation capabilities of the architecture shapes the dynamical landscape.This process defines the basins of attraction of the trained model, effectively directing each input provided as an initial condition toward its corresponding destination target.

</details>


### [107] [Fundamental Limits of Stability Inference in High-Dimensional Complex Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23644)
*Michela Costa,Kentaro Hoshisashi,Flaviano Morone,Tim Rogers,Paolo Barucca*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: Using multivariate Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes, the paper identifies three factors governing the precision of estimating a system's distance from instability: effective measurement budget, signal-to-noise ratio, and distance to criticality. Near criticality, temporal correlations reduce effective independent observations, causing inference to break down and an optimal sampling interval to emerge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many complex systems (ecosystems, neural circuits, financial markets) operate near instability, which is thought to be functionally advantageous. However, it is unclear how precisely one can estimate a system's distance from that threshold given finite, noisy recordings.

Method: The study uses the multivariate Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process as a canonical linear model of relaxation near a stable fixed point, and analyzes the precision of estimating the distance to criticality through three governing factors: effective measurement budget, signal-to-noise ratio, and distance to criticality.

Result: As the system approaches criticality, the log-likelihood curvature flattens, causing relative uncertainty on the estimated distance to diverge. Temporal correlations drastically reduce the effective number of independent observations; inference breaks down when this effective count falls below the system dimension. An optimal sampling interval is shown to exist, which diverges near criticality.

Conclusion: Estimating proximity to instability faces fundamental limits due to the flattening of likelihood curvature and effective sample size reduction from temporal correlations, with direct implications for experimental design in systems near criticality.

Abstract: Many complex systems, including ecosystems, neural circuits, and financial markets, are inferred to operate close to a threshold of instability, at which a small perturbation can propagate across the entire system. This proximity is often interpreted as functionally advantageous, yet it poses a question common to all these fields: from a finite, noisy recording, how precisely can the distance of a system from that threshold be estimated? Using the multivariate Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process as the canonical linear model of relaxation near a stable fixed point, we show that the attainable precision is governed by three factors: an effective measurement budget, set by the number of samples relative to the system dimension and the sampling interval; the signal-to-noise ratio, given by the magnitude of deterministic interactions relative to stochastic forcing; and the distance to criticality, which simultaneously sets the system's correlation times and degrades both of the preceding factors. As the slowest dynamical mode softens near the threshold, the curvature of the log-likelihood flattens along the direction that determines stability, so that the relative uncertainty on the estimated distance diverges as that distance vanishes. Critically, temporal correlations near instability reduce the effective number of independent observations far below the nominal sample count, and inference breaks down when this effective count falls below the system dimension, even when the raw data volume appears sufficient.
  A direct consequence is the existence of an optimal sampling interval that diverges as the system approaches criticality, with practical implications for experimental design.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.stat-mech'></div>

# cond-mat.stat-mech [[Back]](#toc)

### [108] [A few remarks on hyperstatistics and some applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20735)
*Lucas Squillante,Samuel M. Soares,Guilherme Lepski,Mariano de Souza*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper introduces "hyperstatistics," a general statistical mechanics approach for non-Boltzmann-Gibbsian systems, demonstrating its high accuracy across diverse applications (RC circuit discharge, cryogenic $^4$He pumping, LHC collision data, turbulence) and extending it to Brownian motion velocity autocorrelation and brain dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a general theoretical framework for treating systems that exhibit inherent non-Boltzmann-Gibbsian behavior, which classical statistical mechanics cannot adequately describe.

Method: The authors develop "hyperstatistics," a statistical mechanics approach for non-Boltzmann-Gibbsian systems, and validate it by applying to RC circuit discharge, $^4$He cryostat pumping, LHC $p$-Pb collision midrapidity data, and turbulent acceleration distributions, then extend to Brownian motion velocity autocorrelation and brain dynamics.

Result: Hyperstatistics achieves extremely high accuracy across all tested systems and demonstrates versatility by successfully describing Brownian motion velocity autocorrelation and showing potential for modeling brain dynamics.

Conclusion: Hyperstatistics is a versatile, high-precision framework applicable to a wide range of non-Boltzmann-Gibbsian systems across physics and potentially neuroscience, meriting further theoretical and applied development.

Abstract: In a recent paper [arXiv:2604.24783 (2026)], we have proposed a general approach to treat systems with inherent non-Boltzmann-Gibbsian behaviour. Given the extremely high accuracy of our approach, we have adopted the term hyperstatistics. We have applied such a statistical mechanics approach, i.e., hyperstatistics, to the discharge of a capacitor in a RC series circuit, pumping of $^4$He of a closed cycle cryostat, midrapidity data of $p$-Pb collisions at the LHC, as well as for the distribution of accelerations in turbulent systems. Here, we discuss into more details the ground of hyperstatistics. We demonstrate the versatility of hyperstatistics upon applying it to the velocity autocorrelation function in Brownian motion and also regarding its potential to describe brain dynamics.

</details>


### [109] [Tuning ergodicity breaking: Anomalous diffusion under asymptotic power-law forcing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20807)
*Raul V. B. Morás,M. Florencia Carusela,Luciano C. Lapas*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A scaling parameter η governs phase transitions in non-Markovian systems driven by power-law forces, producing three regimes: thermalization (η>0), non-ergodic saturation (η=0), and runaway energy growth (η<0). The fluctuation-dissipation theorem becomes force-independent, and a selective ergodicity breaking emerges where microscopic fluctuations and the drive are decoupled.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how internal memory and external forcing compete in non-Markovian systems, leading to distinct dynamical phases such as thermalization, ergodicity breaking, and runaway energy growth.

Method: The study analyzes the Generalized Langevin Equation with external drives exhibiting asymptotic power-law tails, identifying a scaling parameter η that determines the phase diagram. The fluctuation-dissipation theorem is reformulated, and direct Langevin simulations in the Markovian limit are used to verify the classification.

Result: Three universal diffusive regimes are identified: thermalization (η>0), non-ergodic saturation (η=0), and force-dominated runaway phase (η<0). The FDT is independent of external force and depends only on the integral of noise density of states. Selective ergodicity breaking is revealed: microscopic fluctuations decouple from the drive, while relaxation fully encodes it.

Conclusion: The scaling parameter η provides a complete classification of dynamical phases in non-Markovian systems under power-law drives. The kinetic effective temperature is controlled by the drive encoded in relaxation. Simulations confirm the phase boundaries and capture the non-thermal plateau at the critical point η=0.

Abstract: In non-Markovian systems, distinct dynamical phases arise from the competition between internal memory and external forcing, encompassing thermalization, persistent ergodicity breaking, and runaway energy growth. This study shows that the scaling parameter $η$ governs the emergent phase diagram within a system described by the Generalized Langevin Equation, particularly when subjected to external drives with asymptotic power-law tails. Three universal regimes for diffusive processes are delineated by this parameter: thermalization ($η> 0$), non-ergodic saturation ($η= 0$), and a force-dominated runaway phase ($η< 0$). The fluctuation-dissipation theorem, within this framework, is shown to be independent of external force and determined by the integral of noise density of states. A selective breaking of ergodicity is revealed by this formulation; microscopic fluctuations are decoupled from the drive, yet the relaxation completely encodes it, which in turn controls the kinetic effective temperature. Direct Langevin simulations in the Markovian limit quantitatively confirm this classification, capturing the non-thermal plateau at the critical point.

</details>


### [110] [The Market Crystal: A Spin-Lattice Model for Collective Cryptocurrency States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22017)
*Hamidreza Oliaei-Moghadam*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper introduces a spin-lattice representation (Market Crystal) for cryptocurrency markets, mapping 169 assets onto a 13x13 lattice via correlation-based BFS, using an Ising-like Hamiltonian to characterize collective market states through magnetization and energy observables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To apply statistical-mechanical frameworks (analogous to interacting many-body systems) to analyze collective dynamics and emergent synchronized movements in cryptocurrency markets.

Method: Assets are encoded as binary spin variables based on return sign; a correlation-based breadth-first search (CBFS) embeds 169 cryptocurrencies into a 13x13 lattice; an Ising-like Hamiltonian (Market Crystal) is constructed; macroscopic observables (magnetization, energy) are used for characterization.

Result: Phase-space structure reveals regimes of strong alignment and fragmentation among assets, with energy-magnetization patterns suggesting predominantly ferromagnetic interactions.

Conclusion: The statistical-mechanical framework offers a novel viewpoint for studying collective behavior in financial systems, demonstrated on cryptocurrency markets.

Abstract: Collective dynamics in financial markets can emerge through synchronized movements of large groups of assets. Motivated by analogies with interacting many-body systems, we introduce a spin-lattice representation for analyzing collective states in cryptocurrency markets. In this framework, assets are encoded as binary spin variables according to the sign of their returns, while correlations between assets determine effective interaction strengths. A correlation-based breadth-first search (CBFS) procedure embeds 169 cryptocurrencies into a $13 \times 13$ lattice, enabling the construction of an Ising-like Hamiltonian describing the market configuration, which we call the \emph{Market Crystal}. Macroscopic observables such as magnetization and energy provide a statistical-mechanical characterization of collective market states. The resulting phase-space structure highlights regimes of strong alignment and fragmentation among assets, with an energy--magnetization pattern suggestive of predominantly ferromagnetic interactions. This framework offers a statistical-mechanical viewpoint for studying collective behavior in financial systems.

</details>


### [111] [Hydrodynamic tails in chaotic spin chains with quantum group symmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20850)
*Luca V. Delacretaz,Victor Gorbenko,Jiaozi Wang,Bernardo Zan,Aleksandr Zhabin*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Quantum group symmetry enables superdiffusive (rather than exponentially decaying) transport for U(1) charged operators in non-integrable many-body systems, as demonstrated in the XXZ model with integrability-breaking deformations, across Hamiltonian, Floquet, and classical settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge two frontiers: anomalous hydrodynamics (e.g., superdiffusion) in generic non-integrable models and the generalization of global symmetry, by showing that quantum group symmetry provides a mechanism for anomalous transport in chaotic systems.

Method: Study dynamics of local U(1) charged operators in non-integrable lattice models with quantum group symmetry, using the XXZ model with integrability-breaking deformations as a primary example, across Hamiltonian, Floquet, and classical realizations.

Result: Despite U(1) charge forbidding overlap with conventional hydrodynamic densities (which would normally cause rapid decay), quantum group symmetry protects these modes, making them long-lived. The dynamics is superdiffusive across all realizations, with unusual finite-size effects at very late times.

Conclusion: Quantum group symmetry is a novel mechanism for anomalous hydrodynamics (superdiffusion) in chaotic, non-integrable systems, challenging the expectation that such modes decay rapidly.

Abstract: The interplay between symmetry and thermalization governs the late-time dynamics of local quantum and classical many-body systems at nonzero temperature. Recently, two parallel frontiers have emerged: the search for robust anomalous hydrodynamics -- such as superdiffusion -- in generic, non-integrable models, and the formal effort to generalize the fundamental concept of global symmetry. In this paper, we bridge these frontiers by demonstrating that quantum group symmetry provides a novel mechanism for anomalous hydrodynamics in chaotic systems. We study the dynamics of local operators carrying $U(1)$ charge in non-integrable lattice models that also have quantum group symmetry. One example is transverse spin in the XXZ model with integrability breaking deformations. While such excitations are expected to decay very quickly at high temperature because their charge forbids overlap with conventional hydrodynamic densities, we find that protection by the quantum group symmetry makes these modes long-lived, despite the absence of local quantum group charge density or current. Furthermore, the dynamics is superdiffusive across Hamiltonian, Floquet, and classical realizations, and exhibits unusual finite size effects at very late times. We also revisit transverse spin dynamics in the integrable XXZ model.

</details>


### [112] [Asymptotic hydrographs and anomalous dispersion in mass-conserving storage cascades](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21095)
*Henrique Santos Lima,Márk Honti,Balázs Sándor*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Replacing exponential waiting times with q-exponential densities in a cascade of reservoirs leads to asymptotic hydrographs governed by α-stable Lévy laws, with a critical shifted Lévy density at q=5/3, demonstrating that anomalous non-Gaussian dispersion emerges from mass-conserving convolutional chains without fractional derivatives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how classical Erlang distribution from sums of exponential random variables changes when the exponential waiting-time density is replaced by the q-exponential density from nonextensive statistics, particularly in the context of mass-conserving cascades.

Method: Analytical asymptotic analysis of a mass-conserving cascade of reservoirs driven by a q-exponential waiting-time kernel, deriving the outflow behavior as the cascade size grows large.

Result: For the critical case q=5/3, the large-cascade flow rate converges to a stable Lévy density with a Galilean-type time shift. For the entire regime 1<q<2, macroscopic dynamics are governed by α-stable Lévy laws.

Conclusion: Anomalous non-Gaussian dispersion can emerge from pure mass-conserving convolutional chains without invoking fractional derivatives, with α-stable Lévy laws arising naturally from q-exponential waiting-time kernels.

Abstract: Sums of independent exponential random variables lead to the Erlang distribution, providing a direct probabilistic route from exponential waiting times to the integer-shape gamma law. This paper investigates how this classical construction changes when the exponential waiting-time density is replaced by the $q$-exponential density of nonextensive statistics. Our main result is an analytical asymptotic expression for the outflow of a mass-conserving cascade of reservoirs driven by a $q$-exponential waiting-time kernel. In the critical case $q=5/3$, the large-cascade flow rate converges to a stable Lévy density whose time argument is shifted by a Galilean-type transformation. This shifted Lévy law gives the asymptotic hydrograph of the cascade. We also found that for the entire regime $1<q<2$ the macroscopic dynamics are governed by $α$-stable Lévy laws. This proves that anomalous non-Gaussian dispersion can emerge from pure mass-conserving convolutional chains without invoking fractional derivatives.

</details>


### [113] [From Landau Equation and Large Deviations to Efficient Simulations of Dynamical Fluctuations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21318)
*Anwar El Rhirhayi,Jean-Baptiste Fouvry,Julien Barré*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper develops a Langevin equation framework, derived from Large Deviation Theory and Rostoker's principle, that extends the Landau equation to capture both mean dynamics and fluctuations in long-range interacting N-body systems, validated against direct N-body simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The deterministic Landau equation successfully describes the mean long-term evolution of dynamically hot, long-range interacting finite-N systems, but it fundamentally ignores dynamical fluctuations that occur among different realizations.

Method: The authors build upon Large Deviation Theory to construct a physically-consistent system of Langevin equations, derived from Rostoker's principle in the limit of weak two-body deflections.

Result: The Langevin equations simultaneously recover the mean Landau dynamics and accurately capture fluctuations among different realizations. Extensive validation against tailored direct N-body simulations shows exquisite agreement.

Conclusion: The proposed Langevin framework successfully extends the Landau kinetic theory to include fluctuations, with strong empirical validation from N-body simulations.

Abstract: The (deterministic) Landau equation captures the mean long-term evolution of dynamically hot long-range interacting finite-$N$ systems. Though successful, this kinetic equation fundamentally ignores dynamical fluctuations. Building upon Large Deviation Theory, we present a physically-consistent system of Langevin equations that simultaneously recovers the mean Landau dynamics and accurately captures the corresponding fluctuations among different realizations. We show in particular how these Langevin equations can be derived from Rostoker's principle in the limit of weak two-body deflections. We extensively validate these equations against tailored direct $N$-body simulations, showing an exquisite level of agreement.

</details>


### [114] [Nonreciprocal Disorder Prevents Zero-Temperature Freezing in a Ferromagnet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21582)
*Noah Grodzinski,Robert L. Jack,Sarah A. M. Loos*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A 2D Ising model with quenched nonreciprocal bonds exhibits a continuous nonequilibrium transition with finite critical density, active zero-temperature dynamics, and logarithmic coarsening.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the effects of quenched (randomly distributed) nonreciprocal interactions in extended systems, which remain largely unexplored compared to uniform nonreciprocity.

Method: Study of a 2D Ising model with randomly distributed nonreciprocal bonds at density p, using gauge-invariance arguments and mean-field theory to analyze the phase diagram.

Result: Continuous nonequilibrium transition down to T=0 with finite p_c; gauge-invariance yields p_c(T) ≤ 1/2; mean-field theory gives qualitatively correct phase diagram; zero-temperature dynamics exhibits athermal rare-region reversals and logarithmic "activated" coarsening.

Conclusion: Quenched nonreciprocity drives novel nonequilibrium critical phenomena distinct from equilibrium disordered models, with activity persisting even at zero temperature.

Abstract: Nonreciprocal interactions underpin diverse nonequilibrium phenomena, yet the effects of quenched nonreciprocity in extended systems remain largely unexplored. We study a $2d$ Ising model with randomly distributed nonreciprocal bonds at density $p$, finding a continuous nonequilibrium transition down to $T=0$ with finite $p_c$. A gauge-invariance argument yields $p_c(T)\leq1/2$, and mean-field theory predicts a qualitatively correct phase diagram. Unlike equilibrium disordered models, the zero-temperature dynamics remains active, with athermal rare-region reversals and logarithmic "activated" coarsening.

</details>


### [115] [Heavy-Tailed Dispersal Kernels from Stopped Subdiffusive Fractional Brownian Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21681)
*Luis F. Gordillo,Priscilla E. Greenwood*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper introduces a heterogeneous stopping model where subdiffusive fractional Brownian motion particles stop at Gamma-distributed rates instead of fixed exponential rates, producing both strong localized clumping and heavy-tailed dispersal kernels suitable for modeling long-distance seed dispersal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard subdiffusive fractional Brownian motion with exponentially distributed stopping times produces only exponentially decaying tails, failing to capture the heavy-tailed long-distance dispersal events observed in some seed dispersal applications.

Method: The authors propose modifying the standard model by making the stopping rate parameter Gamma distributed (rather than fixed), introducing heterogeneity in stopping rates while retaining the subdiffusive fractional Brownian motion framework.

Result: The model simultaneously produces strong localized clumping (characteristic of subdiffusion) and heavy-tailed distributions of final radial positions, combining features that were previously incompatible.

Conclusion: Heterogeneous stopping rates provide a mechanism to bridge localized aggregation and long-distance dispersal in a single subdiffusive model, offering improved realism for ecological processes like seed dispersal.

Abstract: Subdiffusive fractional Brownian motions produce localized aggregation when particles are stopped at exponentially distributed times. In applications where clumping and long-distance dispersal events are observed simultaneously, such as in some instances of seed dispersal, this model fails to describe the tails of the data. The resulting redistribution kernel has only an exponentially decaying tail, whereas a heavier tail is needed for modeling the long-distance dispersal observed. Here we propose a model in which subdiffusive particles stop at exponentially distributed times, but with a rate parameter that is Gamma distributed. This heterogeneity in stopping rates causes the density of final radial positions to have a heavy-tailed distribution. Our model retains the strong localized clumping characteristic of subdiffusive fractional Brownian motion while simultaneously generating the heavy tails required for realistic long-distance dispersal.

</details>


### [116] [Nonlocal fractional Kardar-Parisi-Zhang dynamics of grain boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21903)
*Kai Zhao*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper studies kinetic roughening of driven grain boundaries (GBs) mediated by disconnection collective behavior. Using MD simulations and nonlocal fractional KPZ theory, it shows a sharp morphology transition from quenched Edwards-Wilkinson (H≈0.33) to anomalous fKPZ regime (H≈0.8) at yield point, with 1/r nonlocal elasticity causing fractional relaxation. Avalanche noise arrests KPZ gradient catastrophe, and resulting morphology parallels dynamic fracture universality class.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the long-range 1/r elastic interactions of disconnections fundamentally distinguish grain boundary kinetic roughening from conventional growing surfaces.

Method: Large-scale molecular dynamics simulations combined with nonlocal fractional Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (fKPZ) theory.

Result: Driven GBs exhibit a sharp morphology transition at the yield point: from quenched Edwards-Wilkinson class (H≈0.33) to anomalous fKPZ regime (H≈0.8). The 1/r nonlocal elasticity manifests as fractional relaxation of GB morphology, and avalanche noise arrests the gradient catastrophe from KPZ nonlinearity.

Conclusion: Driven GB morphology breaks Gaussian self-affinity, governed by nonlocal elastic kernels, and parallels the universality class of dynamic fracture.

Abstract: The kinetic roughening of driven grain boundaries (GBs) is fundamentally mediated by the collective behavior of disconnections, whose long-range $1/r$ elastic interactions distinguish them from conventional growing surfaces. Through large-scale molecular dynamics simulations and a nonlocal fractional Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (fKPZ) theory, we demonstrate that driven GBs undergo a sharp transition of morphology at the yield point, from the quenched Edwards-Wilkinson universality class ($H \approx 0.33$) to an anomalous fKPZ regime ($H \approx 0.8$), while the 1/r nonlocal elasticity manifests as the fractional relaxation of GB morphology. The results show that the avalanche noise arrests the gradient catastrophe induced by the KPZ nonlinearity. Governed by the nonlocal elastic kernels, the resulting morphology breaks the Gaussian self-affinity and parallels the universality class of dynamic fracture.

</details>


### [117] [Internal-state criticality in Bayesian-inverse-Bayesian inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22109)
*Kazuto Sasai,Yukio-Pegio Gunji*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper proposes Bayesian-inverse-Bayesian (BIB) inference in repeated games as a minimal generative model linking Bayesian inference, statistical mechanics, and heavy-tailed statistics. BIB dynamics self-organize to a critical state with heavy-tailed power-law behavior (exponent ~1.43) without parameter tuning, unlike ordinary Bayesian inference which shows no universality. The mechanism involves continual reconstruction of the hypothesis-space boundary, complementary to self-organized criticality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a minimal generative model that bridges Bayesian inference, statistical mechanics, and heavy-tailed statistics, and to demonstrate how internal dynamics (rather than Nash-targeting algorithms) can produce non-trivial, critical behavior in repeated games.

Method: BIB inference is applied to repeated N-hand cyclic-dominance rock-paper-scissors. The method involves adding an inverse-Bayesian relaxation step (hypothesis renewal) to ordinary Bayesian inference. Simulations sweep across design, window, and opponent conditions. Argmax-persistence distributions and finite-size scaling analyses are performed across hypothesis counts (N_h).

Result: BIB dynamics remain in an internal critical state across all conditions, with argmax-persistence following a heavy-tailed power law (exponent α≈1.43 at canonical window). The exponent drifts toward universal 3/2 as finite-sample residual vanishes. Bayes-only inference shows no power law. Criticality is invariant across hypothesis count N_h, with cutoff time and posterior spread obeying finite-size scaling.

Conclusion: Adding an inverse-Bayesian relaxation step to ordinary Bayesian inference is sufficient to render dynamics critical without external parameter adjustment. BIB reaches criticality by continually reconstructing the hypothesis-space boundary, a mechanism complementary to self-organized criticality that ensures robustness across a natural parameter range.

Abstract: We propose Bayesian-inverse-Bayesian (BIB) inference in repeated games as a minimal generative model linking Bayesian inference, statistical mechanics, and heavy-tailed statistics. As a concrete instantiation we simulate repeated $N$-hand cyclic-dominance rock-paper-scissors, a discrete setting in which Nash-targeting algorithms collapse to uniform random play, so that any non-trivial dynamics must originate internally. Across a multi-axis sweep of design, window, and opponent conditions, the BIB dynamics remain in the same internal critical state, the argmax-persistence distribution staying a heavy-tailed power law with exponent $α\approx 1.43$ at the canonical window. Along the window and alphabet axes the exponent is not constant but drifts toward the universal $3/2$ as the finite-sample residual $(N-1)/(2m)$ vanishes. Bayes-only inference, which lacks the inverse step, shows no analogous universality and no power law. Because the argmax and laminar observables are first-passage reads of one driftless log-posterior walk, what is robust across conditions is the critical, zero-drift state itself, evidenced by the cross-design data collapse rather than by any particular exponent value. The state is also invariant across the hypothesis count $N_h$, with the cutoff time and posterior spread obeying finite-size scaling. Adding an inverse-Bayesian relaxation step (hypothesis renewal) to ordinary Bayesian inference is by itself enough to render the dynamics critical, with no external parameter adjustment.
