Rather than self-organizing toward an absorbing state, BIB reaches criticality by continually reconstructing the hypothesis-space boundary, a mechanism complementary to self-organized criticality that makes the criticality robust across a natural parameter range.

</details>


### [118] [Giant Fluctuations in Self-Propelled Particles with Age-Dependent Switching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22209)
*Shabnam Sohrabi,Farhad H. Jafarpour*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A model of self-propelled particles with age-dependent phase switching shows a continuous transition at a=1 between ballistic and diffusive regimes, with giant fluctuations at criticality where variance scales as T²/log T.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how age-dependent (semi-Markovian) reorientation probabilities affect transport and fluctuation properties in biased environments, particularly the breakdown of the Central Limit Theorem.

Method: Analytical approach using generating-function methods and discrete-time moment recurrences to derive exact propagator expressions and long-time asymptotics for mean displacement and variance.

Result: At the critical point a=1, variance scales ballistically with logarithmic correction: Var(x_T) ∝ T²/log T, indicating giant fluctuations beyond standard CLT behavior.

Conclusion: Slowly decaying reorientation probabilities cause a marginal breakdown of the Central Limit Theorem, enabling unusually high-variance exploratory dynamics in biased environments.

Abstract: We investigate the transport and fluctuation properties of self-propelled particles whose motion is governed by an age-dependent phase-switching mechanism. The dynamics alternate between a Markovian downstream phase with a constant switching probability $r$ and a semi-Markovian upstream phase in which the age-dependent hazard probability $a/(b+c)$ decays with the internal clock $c$, representing persistent orientation. The time-averaged velocity, as an order parameter, shows a continuous transition at $a=1$ which separates an upstream-dominated ballistic regime ($a<1$) from an ergodic diffusive regime ($a>1$). Through generating-function methods and discrete-time moment recurrences, we derive exact expressions for the propagator and determine the long-time asymptotics of the mean displacement and variance. At the critical point $a=1$, the system exhibits giant fluctuations, with the variance scaling ballistically up to a logarithmic correction, $\mathrm{Var}(x_T) \propto T^2 / \log T$. These results demonstrate how slowly decaying reorientation probabilities lead to a marginal breakdown of the Central Limit Theorem, enabling unusually high-variance exploratory dynamics in biased environments.

</details>


### [119] [Perturbative Renormalization and Universality Diagram for Long-Range Quantum Criticality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22407)
*Zhiyi Li,Zhijie Fan,Kun Chen,Youjin Deng*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper develops a perturbative renormalization-group expansion for ferromagnetic long-range quantum O(n) models with interactions decaying as 1/r^{d+σ}, using a parametrization around the LR-SR boundary (d=3-ε, σ=2-δ). Two-loop calculations yield explicit expressions for critical exponents (ν, η_ω, η_k), which interpolate between LR Gaussian scaling at σ=2d/3 and SR quantum Wilson-Fisher scaling at σ→2. A (d,σ) universality diagram is proposed to organize long-range quantum criticality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Experimental progress in quantum simulators reveals that long-range interactions reshape quantum criticality and stabilize exotic phases beyond the short-range paradigm, motivating a systematic theoretical framework to understand LR quantum criticality and its connection to SR limits.

Method: A perturbative renormalization-group expansion is developed around the LR-SR boundary by setting d=3-ε and σ=2-δ, where the full interacting LR window (2d/3<σ<2) maps to 0<δ<2ε/3, enabling controlled perturbative calculations up to two loops.

Result: Explicit two-loop expressions for correlation-length exponent ν and anomalous dimensions η_ω and η_k in terms of ε, δ, and n. The exponents reproduce LR Gaussian scaling at σ=2d/3 and SR quantum Wilson-Fisher scaling at σ→2, confirming σ_*=2 as the LR-SR boundary within the 3-ε expansion.

Conclusion: A (d,σ) universality diagram for ferromagnetic long-range quantum O(n) criticality is proposed, serving as an organizing framework for phase diagrams of long-range quantum spin chains, bridging the gap between LR and SR universality classes.

Abstract: Experimental progress in quantum simulators highlights the role of long-range (LR) interactions in reshaping quantum criticality and stabilizing exotic phases beyond the short-range (SR) paradigm. We study ferromagnetic long-range quantum $O(n)$ models with interactions decaying as $1/r^{d+σ}$ and develop a perturbative renormalization-group expansion around the LR--SR boundary by setting $d=3-ε$ and $σ=2-δ$. In this parametrization, the full interacting LR window $2d/3<σ<2$ becomes $0<δ<2ε/3$, and is therefore perturbatively controlled. A two-loop calculation yields explicit expressions, in terms of $ε$, $δ$, and $n$, for the correlation-length exponent $ν$ and for the frequency and momentum anomalous dimensions $η_ω$ and $η_k$. The resulting exponents reduce to long-range Gaussian scaling at $σ=2d/3$ and to SR quantum Wilson-Fisher scaling in the $σ\to 2$ limit, thereby identifying $σ_*=2$ as the LR--SR boundary within the controlled $3-ε$ expansion. Combining the RG results with scaling boundaries and classical LR analogies, we propose a $(d,σ)$ universality diagram for ferromagnetic long-range quantum $O(n)$ criticality and use it as an organizing framework for the phase diagram of long-range quantum spin chains.

</details>


### [120] [Deviance from a pink noise regime in the temporal organization of semantic relations in psychosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23043)
*Paola Moreno Ancalmo,Emre Bora,Rieke Roxanne Mülfarth,Svenja Seuffert,Tilo Kircher,Frederike Stein,Philipp Homan,Wolfram Hinzen*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper applies detrended fluctuation analysis (DFA) to BERT-derived semantic similarity time series from clinical transcripts across three datasets, finding that psychosis patients exhibit significantly elevated DFA scaling exponents compared to controls, indicating abnormally strong long-range temporal correlations (excessive persistence) in semantic fluctuations—a pattern parallel to departures from criticality observed in physiological and brain function in psychosis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether the temporal organization of language, specifically its scaling dynamics, exhibits alterations in psychosis analogous to the well-documented deviations from criticality (departures from pink noise toward brown noise) seen across physiological and cognitive domains in pathology.

Method: Detrended fluctuation analysis (DFA) was applied to BERT-derived continuous cosine-similarity time series that capture semantic trajectories in clinical transcripts from patients with psychosis and controls, across three independent datasets, to extract DFA scaling exponents quantifying long-range temporal correlations.

Result: Across all three datasets, patients with psychosis exhibited significantly elevated DFA scaling exponents relative to controls, indicating abnormally strong long-range temporal correlations with excessive persistence in semantic fluctuations.

Conclusion: Altered temporal scaling in speech is a signature of psychosis, consistent with deviations from criticality (shift from pink toward brown noise) found in physiological domains, suggesting a link between linguistic temporal organization and brain function alterations in psychosis.

Abstract: The notion of pink noise refers to 'scale-invariant' temporal dynamics, where fluctuations exhibit similar statistical structure across time scales. Departures from a regime associated with such scale-free organization toward uncorrelated 'white' noise or overly persistent 'brown' noise have been widely identified as markers of pathology across physiological and cognitive domains. Whether comparable alterations characterize the temporal organization of language remains largely unexplored. We address this question in the domain of psychosis, where language anomalies are pervasively documented. Specifically, we apply detrended fluctuation analysis (DFA) to quantify temporal scaling in BERT-derived continuous cosine-similarity time series capturing trajectories through semantic space, using clinical transcripts from patients and controls across three independent datasets. DFA scaling exponents were extracted to characterize the strength of long-range temporal correlations. Across all datasets, patients exhibited significantly elevated scaling exponents relative to controls, indicating abnormally strong long-range correlations with excessive persistence in semantic fluctuations. This temporal analysis opens a window into the multi-timescale organization of meaning as it unfolds in discourse. The results reveal a signature of altered temporal scaling in speech, consistent with deviations from criticality in physiological domains, paralleling known departures from criticality in brain function in psychosis and suggesting possible links between these two domains.

</details>


### [121] [Irreversibility Enhances Quantum-Enhanced Markov-Chain Monte Carlo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23350)
*Kefan Cao,Zidong Cui,Lei Wang,Ying Tang*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper introduces irreversible quantum-enhanced Monte Carlo (IQEMC), which breaks detailed balance to improve quantum MCMC sampling efficiency, outperforming reversible quantum-enhanced MCMC on spin-glass benchmarks without increasing complexity and with a spectral gap that grows with system size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While breaking detailed balance is known to accelerate classical MCMC, whether irreversibility can similarly enhance quantum MCMC remains unexplored. The authors aim to bridge this gap by introducing irreversibility into quantum-enhanced Monte Carlo methods.

Method: The authors develop an irreversible quantum-enhanced Monte Carlo (IQEMC) algorithm that breaks detailed balance via state-dependent proposals while preserving the target stationary distribution. The proposals are guided by Landau-Zener transitions, promoting large energy descents from high-energy states and stable transitions near low-energy states.

Result: On spin-glass benchmarks, IQEMC outperforms the reversible QEMC baseline without increasing computational complexity. Unlike annealing baselines, IQEMC exhibits a spectral gap that increases with system size and annealing speed.

Conclusion: Irreversibility is established as a physically grounded mechanism for enhancing quantum MCMC, offering a new route for improving sampling efficiency in quantum Monte Carlo methods.

Abstract: Detailed balance underlies conventional Markov-chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) algorithms. Yet in classical systems, breaking detailed balance generates irreversible probability currents and can accelerate sampling. Whether irreversibility can similarly enhance quantum MCMC remains an intriguing question. Here we show that irreversibility provides a new route to improving the recent quantum-enhanced MCMC (QEMC), which combines quantum proposals with classical acceptance. By introducing state-dependent proposals that break detailed balance while preserving the target stationary distribution, we develop an irreversible quantum-enhanced Monte Carlo (IQEMC). Guided by Landau-Zener transitions, IQEMC promotes large energy descents from high-energy states while maintaining stable transitions near low-energy states. On spin-glass benchmarks, IQEMC outperforms QEMC without increasing computational complexity and, unlike the annealing baseline, exhibits a spectral gap that increases with system size and annealing speed. These results establish irreversibility as a physically grounded mechanism for enhancing quantum MCMC.

</details>


### [122] [Skewness tunes the small-drift record rate of random walks and Lévy flights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23553)
*José Ricardo G. Mendonça*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A random walk with small positive drift μ sets new records at a rate λ(μ) that vanishes as μ→0. For centered steps attracted to a stable law with index 1<α≤2 and positivity parameter ρ=P(Y>0), we find λ(μ) ∼ K μ^{(1-ρ)/ν}, ν=1-1/α, as μ→0. The result is exact for Gaussian and strictly stable steps, and extends at the leading-power level to their domains of attraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the record rate of a random walk with small positive drift behaves as the drift vanishes, particularly for walks with heavy-tailed or skewed step distributions in the domain of attraction of a stable law.

Method: One Mellin transform of the harmonic sum in the Spitzer-Baxter identity, which factorizes into a kernel transform and a Riemann ζ factor whose poles yield the leading law, prefactor, and correction ladder.

Result: λ(μ) ∼ K μ^{(1-ρ)/ν} with ν=1-1/α, where the exponent depends only on α and ρ, sweeping from 1 to 1/(α-1) as skewness varies. The prefactor K is explicit for strictly stable steps. The Gaussian case recovers the linear law with slope √2; symmetric heavy tails give power μ^{α/2(α-1)}.

Conclusion: The Mellin transform approach unifies diffusive, heavy-tailed, and skewed walks, and also yields the expected maximum, recovering Kingman's heavy-traffic law for queues and Siegmund's corrected-diffusion constant.

Abstract: A random walk with small positive drift $μ$ sets new records at a rate $λ(μ)$ that vanishes as $μ\to 0$. For centered steps attracted to a stable law $Y$ with index $1 < α\leq 2$ and positivity parameter $ρ= P(Y>0)$, we find $λ(μ) \sim Kμ^{(1-ρ)/ν}$, $ν=1-1/α$, as $μ\to 0$. The result is exact for Gaussian and strictly stable steps, and extends at the leading-power level to their domains of attraction. The exponent is set by the asymmetry only through $ρ$, sweeping the interval $[1,\,1/(α-1)]$ as the skewness varies. It recovers the Gaussian linear law with slope $\sqrt{2}$ and, for symmetric heavy tails, the power $μ^{α/2(α-1)}$; beyond the stable tail ratio, distributional details enter through the prefactor $K$, which is explicit for strictly stable steps. The result follows directly from one Mellin transform of the harmonic sum in the Spitzer-Baxter identity, which factorizes into a kernel transform and a Riemann $ζ$ factor whose poles deliver at once the leading law, its prefactor, and a correction ladder, unifying diffusive, heavy-tailed, and skewed walks. The same transform also yields the expected maximum, recovering Kingman's heavy-traffic law for queues and Siegmund's corrected-diffusion constant as adjacent poles.

</details>


### [123] [Thermodynamic inference from noisy single-molecule time series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23619)
*Todd R. Gingrich,Oleg A. Igoshin,Anatoly B. Kolomeisky,Dmitrii E. Makarov*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper systematically studies how finite spatial and temporal resolution (due to the diffraction limit and photon emission/counting rates) affect the estimation of entropy production (arrow of time) from single-molecule/particle tracking trajectories. Two estimation strategies are analyzed, both shown to yield lower bounds on true entropy production. Key findings: noise-degraded observables become non-Markovian even when underlying processes are Markovian, and improving temporal resolution alone can worsen entropy production estimates in the presence of detection noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and quantify how experimental limitations (finite spatial and temporal resolution) affect our ability to discern the arrow of time (entropy production) from single-molecule/particle tracking data.

Method: The authors study two strategies for estimating entropy production from noisy experimental time series Y(t) corresponding to a microscopic variable X(t): (1) inferring statistical properties of X(t) from Y(t) then estimating entropy production, and (2) applying the entropy production estimator directly to Y(t) as a proxy. Analytical proofs and systematic analysis of spatial vs. temporal resolution effects are performed.

Result: Both estimation strategies yield lower bounds on the true entropy production. Noise-degraded observables exhibit non-Markovian dynamics even when the underlying X(t) is Markovian, making non-Markovian estimators advantageous. There is a nontrivial interplay between spatial and temporal resolution—improving temporal resolution alone can degrade entropy production estimates in the presence of detection noise.

Conclusion: Experimental noise fundamentally limits entropy production estimation; care must be taken in choosing resolution parameters, as naive improvements (e.g., higher temporal resolution) may be counterproductive.

Abstract: Single-molecule or single-particle tracking measurements inherently yield noisy microscopic trajectories, often significantly constrained by the diffraction limit and by the finite rate at which photons are emitted and counted. Here we study systematically the resulting effects of finite spatial and temporal resolution on one's ability to discern and quantify the arrow of time in microscopic trajectories. Given an experimental time series Y(t) degraded by noise, we consider the problem of estimating the entropy production associated with the corresponding microscopic variable X(t) using two strategies. The first attempts to infer the statistical properties of X(t) from those of Y(t) before estimating the entropy production. The second uses the experimental observable as a proxy for the true microscopic observable, with the entropy production estimator applied directly to Y(t). We prove that both strategies result in lower bounds on the true entropy production. Importantly, noise-degraded observables Y(t) undergo non-Markovian dynamics even when X(t) are Markovian, and non-Markovian entropy production estimators are advantageous. We further note nontrivial interplay between spatial and temporal resolution: in the presence of detection noise, improving the temporal resolution alone may lead to poorer rather than better entropy production estimates.

</details>


### [124] [Effective hyperuniformity in time-integrated stochastic Turing patterns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23677)
*Anirban Mukherjee,Hong-Yan Shih*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Demographic noise in deterministically stable reaction-diffusion systems can generate stochastic Turing patterns, producing emergent large-scale organization with hyperuniform scaling of number variance, as shown analytically and numerically in the Levin-Segel model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To uncover how demographic noise alone can induce emergent spatial order (stochastic Turing patterns) in reaction-diffusion systems that are deterministically stable, and to identify a hyperuniform regime that is fine-tuning-free in non-conserved multispecies stochastic systems.

Method: Analytical derivation and numerical verification in the Levin-Segel model; analysis of the intensive number variance scaling with window size $R$, showing a finite reaction-kinetic floor proportional to $1/R$ over a spatial range that grows near the Turing instability.

Result: The intensive number variance approaches a finite floor scaling as $1/R$ over a range that expands by orders of magnitude near the Turing instability, producing an effectively hyperuniform regime.

Conclusion: Stochastic Turing patterns arise from demographic noise without deterministic instability, leading to a previously unidentified hyperuniform, fine-tuning-free regime in non-conserved multispecies stochastic systems.

Abstract: Demographic noise generates stochastic Turing patterns even when reaction-diffusion systems are deterministically stable. We show analytically and verify numerically in the Levin-Segel model that temporal integration of configurations reveals emergent large-scale organization. The intensive number variance in a window of size $R \gg 1$ approaches a finite reaction-kinetic floor as $1/R$, over a spatial range growing by orders of magnitude near the Turing instability. This yields an effectively hyperuniform, fine-tuning-free regime previously unidentified in non-conserved multispecies stochastic systems.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.mtrl-sci'></div>

# cond-mat.mtrl-sci [[Back]](#toc)

### [125] [Boosting lattice polarization Mixing the perspectives of geometry optimization and cell-augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20782)
*Pegah Azizi,Rahul Dev Kundu,Xiaojia Shelly Zhang,Stefano Gonella*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper introduces a topology optimization framework to design topologically polarized mechanical metamaterials, unlocking new lattice geometries beyond canonical Maxwell lattices. The method produces a new family of augmented kagome trusses exhibiting strong polarization signatures with up to three-count edge state mismatch, validated through theory, simulations, laser vibrometry, and 3D-printed prototype tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current understanding of topological polarization in mechanical metamaterials is limited to a few ideal Maxwell lattices, lacking systematic design criteria. This work aims to fill that gap by proposing a general optimization-based framework to design polarized structural systems.

Method: The authors employ topology optimization (TO) using both band and mode morphological properties as drivers. The optimization explores a large design space beyond canonical lattices, and the TO-generated geometries inspire the identification of a new family of Maxwell trusses based on augmented kagome geometry.

Result: Achieved strong polarization signatures with up to a three-count edge state mismatch. The TO-generated lattice reveals a new family of Maxwell trusses (augmented kagome geometry). Theory, simulations, laser vibrometry wave measurements on waterjet-cut specimens, and static tests on 3D-printed prototypes all show agreement.

Conclusion: Topology optimization is an effective systematic tool for designing polarized mechanical metamaterials. The framework elucidates criteria for robustness against edge morphology perturbations and enables discovery of new ideal lattice configurations with extreme polarization.

Abstract: Topologically polarized mechanical metamaterials enjoy a special built-in asymmetry that manifests as a preferred localization of edge states on selected edges. While this property has been shown for a few ideal Maxwell lattices, we currently lack systematic criteria to design families of structural systems exhibiting polarization. Here, we propose a framework to design polarized structural configurations enabled by topology optimization (TO), using both band and mode morphological properties as drivers of the optimization algorithm. Through the lens of TO, we are able to tap into a vast design space, unlocking geometric freedom far beyond what is achievable with canonical lattice architectures. At the same time, we elucidate important criteria that need to be satisfied, beyond the optimization outcome, to ensure robustness of the achieved polarization against perturbations of the edge morphology. These results provide the inspiration to loop back into the realm of ideal lattices in search of new configurations characterized by extreme polarization. The peculiar shape and connectivity of the TO-generated lattice offer a blueprint for identifying a new family of Maxwell trusses based on augmented kagome geometry. We demonstrate the achievement of strong polarization signatures up to a three-count edge state mismatch. For all the cases studied, we show agreement between theory, simulations, and experiments, which include laser vibrometry wave measurements on a waterjet-cut specimen and static tests on a 3D-printed prototype.

</details>


### [126] [Magnetic Anisotropy and Metamagnetic Transitions in Er3Pt2Sb4.55 with A Distorted Square Net Lattice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20862)
*Dylan Correll,Chaoguo Wang,Xin Gui*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper reports the synthesis and characterization of a new rare-earth intermetallic compound Er3Pt2Sb4.55(5) with a distorted square-net framework of Er, which exhibits anisotropic magnetic behavior, long-range antiferromagnetic ordering, and metamagnetic transitions based on a Jeff = 1/2 Er3+ motif.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore magnetic frustration in square-net sublattice materials and discover new rare-earth-based compounds that can help understand the interplay between magnetic ordering, crystal-electric field effects, and crystal structure.

Method: Crystal growth and characterization using structural, magnetic, thermodynamic, and electronic transport property measurements.

Result: A new compound Er3Pt2Sb4.55(5) with a distorted square-net Er framework was synthesized, showing anisotropic magnetic behavior, long-range antiferromagnetic ordering, and metamagnetic transitions.

Conclusion: This new material expands the R3Pt2Sb4+x family and provides new opportunities to study the relationship between magnetic ordering, crystal-electric field effects, and crystal structure in rare-earth-based compounds.

Abstract: Magnetic materials with square-net sublattices are of great interest due to its potential to realize magnetic frustration. Here we report the crystal growth and structural, magnetic, thermodynamic and electronic transport properties of a new rare-earth-based intermetallic compound, Er3Pt2Sb4.55(5). The new material possesses a distorted square-net framework of Er. Magnetic properties measurements suggest anisotropic behavior, long-range antiferromagnetic ordering and metamagnetic transitions based on a Jeff = 1/2 Er3+ motif. A magnetic structure is proposed based on the observed magnetic and thermodynamic properties. This new material expands the R3Pt2Sb4+x family with distorted square-net lattice of rare-earth elements and offers a new opportunity to study the relationship between magnetic ordering, crystal-electric field effect and crystal structure in rare-earth-based compounds.

</details>


### [127] [Silicon Nanostructures for Biosensing: From Field-Effect Transistors to Photonic Resonators, and the Long Road to the Clinic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20902)
*Ang Liu,Jun Cao,Zhihao Sun,Jingsong Shang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A review of silicon biosensors covering nanowire BioFETs, porous silicon, and silicon photonics, identifying bottlenecks like Debye screening, fouling, and thermal drift, and arguing that future progress depends on integrated readout, interface engineering, and systematic benchmarking rather than new nanostructures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite silicon's excellent properties (low cost, CMOS compatibility, smooth native oxide, multiple transduction mechanisms) and decades of research demonstrating single-molecule/virus or sub-femtomolar sensitivity in lab conditions, silicon biosensors have rarely been deployed in clinical assays. The motivation is to understand and address this gap.

Method: This paper is a review that traces the development of silicon biosensors, organizes literature around three mature platforms (nanowire BioFETs, porous silicon, silicon photonics) and emerging directions, and compares their performance and bottlenecks.

Result: Identified key bottlenecks: nanowire BioFETs suffer from device variability and Debye screening; porous silicon faces fouling, pore wetting, and surface stability; silicon photonics encounters thermal drift, spectral readout, and packaging; all platforms lack calibration, reproducibility, and validation in real biofluids.

Conclusion: Progress over the next decade will come primarily from integrated readout, interface engineering, and systematic benchmarking rather than from the discovery of new silicon nanostructures.

Abstract: Silicon has a unique combination of properties that makes it one of the best material choices for biosensor platforms: it is inexpensive, its native oxide is atomically smooth, its fabrication processes are CMOS-compatible and have been refined for more than three decades, and it can support many transduction mechanisms in biosensor design. Over the past thirty years, researchers and engineers have used silicon nanostructures to produce ion-sensitive transistors, ultrasensitive nanowire field-effect biosensors, refractive-index-based porous silicon films, microring photonic resonators, suspended cantilevers, luminescent quantum dots, and solid-state nanopores. These device families have demonstrated successful sensing capabilities at the single-molecule, single-virus, or sub-femtomolar level under laboratory conditions; however, they have rarely been widely deployed in clinical assays. This gap is mainly caused by several well-characterized bottlenecks: for nanowire BioFETs, device variability and Debye screening; for porous silicon, fouling, pore wetting, and surface stability; for silicon photonics, thermal drift, spectral readout, and packaging; and across all platforms, calibration, reproducibility, and validation in real biofluids. In this review, we trace the development of silicon biosensors from their early stages to their current state, search and organize the literature focusing on the three most mature platforms and a set of emerging directions, summarize and compare the performance and bottlenecks of different platforms, and argue that progress over the next decade will come primarily from integrated readout, interface engineering, and systematic benchmarking rather than from the discovery of new silicon nanostructures.

</details>


### [128] [Persistent structural distortions and absent superconductivity in trilayer nickelate thin films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20941)
*Abigail Y. Jiang,Maria Bambrick-Santoyo,Lopa Bhatt,Kyeong-Yoon Baek,Yi-Feng Zhao,Dan Ferenc Segedin,Ari B. Turkiewicz,Jenna Hatmin,Grace A. Pan,Suchismita Sarker,Donald A. Walko,Charles M. Brooks,David A. Muller,Berit H. Goodge,Hua Zhou,Antia S. Botana,Julia A. Mundy*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper investigates why compressive strain stabilizes ambient-pressure superconductivity in n=2 Ruddlesden-Popper nickelates but not in n=3 La4Ni3O10 thin films. Despite suppressing density wave order, strain fails to induce superconductivity in the n=3 phase due to persistent layer-inequivalent octahedral rotations around the c-axis, highlighting key differences between the two systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why strain-stabilized ambient-pressure superconductivity, successful in the n=2 Ruddlesden-Popper nickelate phase, cannot be replicated in the n=3 phase (La4Ni3O10), and to identify the structural origins of this discrepancy.

Method: Atomically-precise synthesis of n=3 La4Ni3O10 thin films, combined with transport measurements, picoscale electron microscopy, and synchrotron X-ray diffraction to probe structural and electronic properties under compressive strain.

Result: Compressive strain suppresses density wave order in n=3 films, but no superconductivity is observed even at the largest strain state. A unique structural distortion—persistent, layer-inequivalent octahedral rotations around the c-axis—is identified in strained n=3 films, which may inhibit superconductivity.

Conclusion: Ambient-pressure superconductivity in the n=3 system may require alternative approaches beyond epitaxial strain engineering, as the structural response to strain differs fundamentally from the n=2 system.

Abstract: A new family of high-temperature superconductors was recently discovered in the $n=2,3$ Ruddlesden-Popper nickelates, where superconductivity emerges concomitant with suppression of parent density waves and structural octahedral rotations under hydrostatic pressure. Intriguingly, compressive strain mimics the structural effects of pressure in the $n=2$ phase, yielding ambient-pressure superconductivity. However, analogous strain-stabilized superconductivity has not been realized in the $n=3$. Here, we use atomically-precise synthesis, transport, picoscale electron microscopy, and synchrotron X-ray diffraction to probe $n=3$ La$_4$Ni$_3$O$_{10}$ thin films. Although compressive strain suppresses density wave order, we do not observe superconductivity even under the largest strain state. Importantly, we identify a structural distortion unique to strained $n=3$ thin films that may inhibit superconductivity: persistent, layer-inequivalent octahedral rotations around the $c$-axis. Our results highlight key differences between the $n=3$ and $n=2$ systems, suggesting that ambient-pressure superconductivity in the $n=3$ may require new methods beyond epitaxial strain engineering.

</details>


### [129] [Deciphering Noise in tip--sample Interactions: Insights into Nanoscale Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20974)
*Jaime Colchero,Juan F. González-Martínez*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper reveals that tip-sample interactions in ambient DAFM introduce a non-thermal noise component from stochastic liquid neck formation/rupture, which exceeds thermal fluctuations and provides complementary chemical contrast to KPFM, proposing FM-DAFM to decouple these dissipative effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demonstrate that noise in DAFM is not solely thermal but includes a significant non-thermal component arising from tip-sample interactions, and to uncover its physical origin as a useful spectroscopic signal rather than a mere artifact.

Method: Using a model system of SDS on graphite, the authors characterize noise through force spectroscopy, 3D imaging modes, and KPFM, analyzing fluctuations from nanoscopic liquid neck formation and rupture.

Result: Interaction-induced noise from stochastic liquid neck dynamics significantly exceeds the thermal background and provides chemical contrast distinct from and complementary to KPFM potential mapping.

Conclusion: Noise is a rich spectroscopic signal of local wettability and dissipative dynamics; FM-DAFM is proposed as a superior technique to decouple these dissipative effects for high-resolution imaging.

Abstract: Noise sets the fundamental limits of resolution and sensitivity in Dynamic Atomic Force Microscopy (DAFM). While thermal fluctuations are conventionally assumed to be the dominant noise source, this work demonstrates that tip--sample interactions in ambient conditions introduce a non--thermal noise component that significantly exceeds the thermal background. Using a model system of sodium dodecyl sulfate (SDS) on graphite, we characterize this noise through force spectroscopy, 3D imaging modes, and Kelvin Probe Force Microscopy (KPFM). This interaction--induced noise arises from the stochastic formation and rupture of nanoscopic liquid necks, serving as a direct fingerprint of local wettability and dissipative dynamics. Crucially, we find that this ``noise channel'' provides chemical contrast that is distinct from and complementary to the electrostatic potential mapped by KPFM. By deciphering the physical origin of these fluctuations, we establish that noise is not merely an instrumental artifact but a rich spectroscopic signal, and we propose that Frequency Modulation (FM--DAFM) offers a superior approach to decouple these dissipative effects for high--resolution imaging.

</details>


### [130] [Quasi-1D Spin Textures: From Chiral Soliton Lattice to Fan State](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21034)
*M. Winter,A. Pignedoli,A. S. Sukhanov,M. Azhar,A. Tahn,B. Achinuq,J. R. Bollard,V. Ukleev,C. Luo,F. Radu,S. Wintz,M. Weigand,A. Mistonov,P. Vir,J. Geck,C. Felser,G. van der Laan,T. Hesjedal,K. Everschor-Sitte,B. Rellinghaus,M. C. Rahn*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates the behavior of anisotropic chiral magnet Mn₁.₄PtSn under a magnetic field applied perpendicular to the crystallographic axis, revealing a transformation from a π-chiral soliton lattice to a fan-like state with continuously decreasing modulation length, where magnetostatic interactions in finite samples stabilize the behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study the unwinding process of helical spin textures in a regime where the propagation direction is constrained by crystal symmetry and the magnetic field is applied perpendicular to that direction, avoiding the typical conical state formation seen in most helimagnets.

Method: Resonant elastic X-ray scattering in a vector magnet combined with micromagnetic simulations to track the evolution of quasi-one-dimensional spin textures in Mn₁.₄PtSn under a perpendicular magnetic field.

Result: A transformation from zero-field π-chiral soliton lattice to a fan-like state was observed, where the propagation direction remains transverse to the field while spins oscillate about the field direction; modulation length continuously decreases with field and approaches the field-polarized state; magnetostatic interactions in finite samples stabilize this behavior.

Conclusion: The results provide evidence for a fan-like regime in a chiral magnet and demonstrate that field orientation relative to crystal symmetry can be used to control one-dimensional spin textures.

Abstract: In most helimagnets, an applied magnetic field aligns the propagation direction of a helical spin texture with the field, resulting in a conical state and obscuring the unwinding process. Here, we access a complementary regime in the anisotropic chiral magnet Mn$_{1.4}$PtSn, where crystal symmetry constrains the propagation direction of the spin modulation. Using resonant elastic X-ray scattering in a vector magnet, we track the evolution of quasi-one-dimensional spin textures that propagate along a chiral crystallographic axis while the magnetic field is applied perpendicular to this direction. Together with micromagnetic simulations, our measurements reveal a transformation from the zero-field $π$-chiral soliton lattice into a fan-like state. In this state, the propagation direction remains transverse to the applied field, while the spins oscillate about the field direction. During magnetization, the modulation length decreases continuously with the field and approaches the field-polarized state. Simulations indicate that magnetostatic interactions in finite samples play a key role in stabilizing this behavior. Our results provide evidence for a fan-like regime in a chiral magnet and highlight how field orientation can be used to control one-dimensional spin textures.

</details>


### [131] [SALMON 2.3: Implementation of divide-and-conquer ground-state initialization for large-scale real-time TDDFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21125)
*Shunsuke Yamada,Tomohito Otobe*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: SALMON 2.3 implements a divide-and-conquer DFT (DC-DFT) scheme for efficient ground-state preparation, enabling large-scale real-time TDDFT simulations of electron dynamics in systems with thousands of atoms, with linear scaling demonstrated on Fugaku.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ground-state electronic structure preparation in large-scale real-time TDDFT can be more expensive than time propagation, creating a bottleneck for simulating nonequilibrium electron dynamics in disordered materials, liquids, nanostructures, and heterogeneous systems containing thousands of atoms.

Method: The authors implement a divide-and-conquer DFT (DC-DFT) scheme with a postprocessing method that reconstructs spatially extended Kohn-Sham orbitals of the entire system, connecting efficient linear-scaling ground-state preparation to SALMON's existing real-time, real-space TDDFT module.

Result: Weak-scaling tests on Fugaku confirm linear scaling with system size. Accuracy tests for a 512-atom amorphous Si system and a 4134-atom bulk H2O liquid system demonstrate practical large-scale real-time TDDFT simulations.

Conclusion: The DC-DFT workflow addresses the ground-state bottleneck in large-scale electron-dynamics simulations while retaining the robustness and broad applicability of SALMON's established time-propagation scheme, enabling practical simulations of realistic systems.

Abstract: In large-scale real-time time-dependent density functional theory (TDDFT), preparing the ground-state electronic structure can be more expensive than the subsequent time propagation, limiting simulations of nonequilibrium electron dynamics in realistic systems containing thousands of atoms. This bottleneck is especially important for disordered materials, liquids, nanostructures, and heterogeneous condensed-matter systems, where nonlinear and strong-field phenomena such as high-harmonic generation and light-induced phase transitions require explicit real-time treatment.
  SALMON is an open-source first-principles code for light-matter interaction simulations based on real-time TDDFT on real-space grids, supporting massively parallel calculations with MPI combined with OpenMP or GPU acceleration. In SALMON 2.3, we implement a divide-and-conquer density functional theory (DC-DFT) scheme and combine it with a postprocessing method that reconstructs spatially extended Kohn-Sham orbitals of the entire system. These reconstructed global orbitals are used directly as initial states for the standard real-time, real-space TDDFT module of SALMON.
  The resulting workflow connects efficient DC-DFT ground-state preparation to conventional real-time TDDFT. The DC-DFT self-consistent-field procedure exhibits linear scaling with system size, addressing a major bottleneck in large-scale electron-dynamics simulations while retaining the robustness and broad applicability of SALMON's established time-propagation scheme. We describe the computational procedure, parallelization strategy, and input/output design. Weak-scaling tests using Si supercells on Fugaku confirm the linear-scaling behavior. Accuracy tests for a 512-atom amorphous Si system and a bulk H2O liquid system containing 4134 atoms demonstrate that the workflow enables practical large-scale real-time TDDFT simulations.

</details>


### [132] [Room-Temperature Noncolinear Ferroelectricity in van der Waals WO$_2$Cl$_2$ with a Wide Bandgap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21149)
*Yu Xing,Ning Ding,Zhipeng Wang,Zhiwen Pan,Lei Guo,Guowei Du,Yangrui Liu,Xiaoxing Cao,Ran Su,Mengting Jiang,Xuezhi Ma,Xiyu Chen,Junchao Zhang,Xinyu Yang,Haoran Ye,Honghong Yao,Rui Feng,Dexiang Chen,Le-Ping Miao,Yumeng You,Zejun Li,Dongsheng Song,Linglong Li,Shuai Dong*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper reports the first experimental demonstration of room-temperature ferroelectricity in van der Waals WO₂Cl₂, which exhibits a large dipole (~3 eÅ), wide bandgap (2.80 eV), and noncollinear dipole order at the atomic level, overcoming key weaknesses of typical low-dimensional ferroelectrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-dimensional ferroelectrics are promising for nanoelectronics but suffer from small bandgaps (mostly <2 eV) or weak polarizations (<1 μC/cm² for sliding ferroelectrics). The authors aim to find a material that simultaneously achieves large polarization and wide bandgap by applying the d0 rule from ferroelectric perovskites to van der Waals systems.

Method: The authors experimentally demonstrate ferroelectricity in van der Waals WO₂Cl₂ using multiple characterization techniques: second harmonic generation (SHG), piezoresponse force microscopy (PFM), and ferroelectric hysteresis loops. Noncollinear dipole order is directly observed at the atomic level via integrated differential phase contrast scanning transmission electron microscopy (iDPC-STEM).

Result: WO₂Cl₂ exhibits room-temperature ferroelectricity with a large dipole (~3 eÅ) from off-center W⁶⁺ displacement, a wide bandgap of 2.80 eV, and an exotic noncollinear dipole order confirmed at the atomic scale.

Conclusion: This work provides an alternative route for low-dimensional ferroelectrics to achieve both excellent ferroelectric performance and rich polar physics, overcoming inherent weaknesses of conventional low-dimensional ferroelectric materials.

Abstract: Low-dimensional ferroelectrics are attractive for their promising prospects in nanoelectronics. Compared with widely-used ferroelectric perovskites, most low-dimensional ferroelectrics exhibit several inborn weaknesses such as small bandgaps (mostly <2 eV, i.e. semiconductors-like) or faint polarizations (e.g. $<1$ $μ$C/cm$^2$ for sliding ferroelectrics even if their bandgaps can be large). Here we experimentally demonstrate the room-temperature ferroelectricity of van der Waals WO$_2$Cl$_2$ . The well-tested d0 rule inherited from ferroelectric perovskites leads to a large dipole (~3 eÅ) from the off-center displacement of W$^6+$ ion and a wide bandgap of 2.80 eV. Its ferroelectricity is proved by multiple characterizations including second harmonic generation, piezoresponse force microscopy, and ferroelectric hysteresis loops. More interestingly, the exotic noncollinear dipole order is directly observed at the atomic level by integrated differential phase contrast scanning transmission electron microscopy. Our work paves an alternative route for low-dimensional ferroelectrics to pursue excellent ferroelectric performance and distinct physics of polarity.

</details>


### [133] [Atomistic Mechanisms of Hard Carbon Formation from Polyvinylidene Chloride](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21196)
*Litong Wu,Zitong Wu,Zakariya El-Machachi,Volker L. Deringer*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using machine-learned interatomic potential simulations, the authors reveal that hard carbon formation from PVDC involves a two-stage process: radical-mediated dehydrochlorination followed by cross-linking and rearrangement into an sp²-bonded network with non-hexagonal rings that frustrate graphitization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The atomistic formation mechanisms of hard carbons have remained elusive due to challenges in both in situ experimental characterization and first-principles simulations, despite decades of research and their widespread application in energy storage.

Method: First-principles-quality simulations using a bespoke machine-learned interatomic potential model to study the thermal decomposition of polyvinylidene chloride (PVDC).

Result: A two-stage formation process: (i) radical-mediated dehydrochlorination generating reactive unsaturated carbon sites, followed by (ii) progressive C-C cross-linking and thermally activated rearrangement into an extended sp²-bonded network. Non-hexagonal ring motifs emerge during pyrolysis.

Conclusion: Non-hexagonal ring motifs induce intrinsic curvature that frustrates graphitic ordering in hard carbons, supporting the empirically-derived theory of their structure.

Abstract: Hard carbons are a class of disordered materials with widespread application in energy storage. Despite decades of research, their atomistic formation mechanisms have remained elusive, due to the difficulty of both in situ experimental characterization and first-principles simulations. Here, we describe the formation mechanism of hard carbon from the thermal decomposition of polyvinylidene chloride (PVDC), using first-principles-quality simulations with a bespoke machine-learned interatomic potential model. Our results indicate a two-stage process, consisting of (i) radical-mediated dehydrochlorination, which generates reactive unsaturated carbon sites, and (ii) progressive carbon-carbon cross-linking followed by thermally activated rearrangement into an extended sp$^{2}$-bonded network. We provide an atomistic account of non-hexagonal ring motifs emerging during pyrolysis, supporting the empirically-derived theory that these motifs induce the intrinsic curvature that frustrates graphitic ordering in hard carbons.

</details>


### [134] [Strain induced magnetic phase transitions in Fe3GeTe2 monolayer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21266)
*Anjali Jyothi Bhasu,Satish Kumar,Mátyás Török,Dániel Tibor Pozsár,Bendegúz Nyári,László Udvardi,Gabriel Martínez-Carracedo,Balázs Nagyfalusi,Amador García-Fuente,Jaime Ferrer,Zoltán Tajkov,László Oroszlány,Levente Rózsa,László Szunyogh*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates monolayer Fe3GeTe2's magnetic properties under varying lattice constants using first-principles calculations and spin dynamics simulations, finding that compressive strain strengthens antiferromagnetic couplings, lowers Curie temperature, and induces a transition from ferromagnetic to conical spin-spiral state, with multiple magnetic phases (ferromagnetic, conical spiral, planar Néel) depending on lattice constant and temperature, and Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions affecting Néel temperature but not Curie temperature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how lattice distortions (strain) control complex magnetic phases in monolayer Fe3GeTe2, including the competition between ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic couplings and their temperature evolution.

Method: First-principles calculations combined with atomistic spin dynamics simulations based on the stochastic Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation; magnetic exchange interactions were calculated to reveal coupling competition; a simple spin-model was introduced to explain spiral phase stabilization.

Result: Compressive strain strengthens antiferromagnetic couplings; Curie temperature decreases with decreasing lattice constant; ground state transitions from ferromagnetic to conical spin-spiral; multiple magnetic phases (ferromagnetic, conical spiral, planar Néel) observed; Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions significantly affect Néel temperature but not Curie temperature.

Conclusion: Lattice distortions are crucial for controlling complex magnetic phases and their temperature evolution in monolayer Fe3GeTe2.

Abstract: We investigate the magnetic properties of a monolayer of Fe3GeTe2 as a function of the lattice constant by combining first-principles calculations with atomistic spin dynamics simulations. The calculated magnetic exchange interactions reveal a competition between ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic couplings, with the latter being significantly strengthened under compressive strain. Stochastic Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert simulations reveal a substantial decrease in the Curie temperature with decreasing lattice constant, and predict a transition of the magnetic ground state from a ferromagnetic configuration to a conical spin-spiral state. We introduce a simple spin-model which explains the stabilization of the spiral phase due to competing exchange interactions. We found multiple magnetic phase transitions involving ferromagnetic, conical spin-spiral, and planar Neel states, depending on both the lattice constant and the temperature. The absence of Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions is found to significantly reduce the Neel temperature, while leaving the Curie temperature largely unaffected. Our findings reveal the importance of lattice distortions in controlling complex magnetic phases and their evolution with temperature.

</details>


### [135] [Bulk Photovoltaic Effect in Two-Dimensional Perovskite Oxides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21441)
*Chunmei Zhang,Jian Zhou,Liang Si*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Ultrathin 2D perovskite oxide films (e.g., SrTiO₃) naturally break inversion symmetry, producing a spontaneous out-of-plane bulk photovoltaic effect that is highly tunable and broadly applicable to 2D oxides.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate emergent quantum phenomena in low-dimensional ABO₃ perovskites, specifically focusing on the bulk photovoltaic effect in ultrathin 2D films where reduced symmetry can induce exotic quantum effects.

Method: First-principles density functional theory, tight-binding modeling, and symmetry analysis to study ultrathin 2D ABO₃ films (exemplified by SrTiO₃).

Result: Ultrathin 2D ABO₃ films naturally break inversion symmetry, producing a spontaneous out-of-plane bulk photovoltaic effect, which is more applicable and experimentally detectable than previous in-plane BPV signals. The effect is highly tunable via thickness, strain, surface termination, crystallographic orientation, and Moiré twisting.

Conclusion: These findings are broadly applicable to a wide range of 2D perovskite and other layer-resolved oxides, offering a new route to engineer out-of-plane BPV effects in low-dimensional oxide systems.

Abstract: Perovskite oxides ABO$_3$ host a rich interplay of charge, spin, lattice, and orbital degrees of freedom, giving rise to diverse quantum phenomena. In low-dimensional ABO$_3$, reduced symmetry can induce exotic quantum effects such as the two-dimensional electron gas and unconventional superconductivity. Using first-principles density functional theory, tight-binding modeling, and symmetry analysis, we show that ultrathin two-dimensional (2D) ABO$_3$ films -- exemplified by SrTiO$_3$ -- naturally break inversion symmetry, producing a spontaneous out-of-plane bulk photovoltaic (BPV) effect. This differs from previous studies on in-plane BPV current signals and is more applicable and experimentally detectable. Such an effect is highly tunable via thickness, strain, surface termination, crystallographic orientation, and Moiré twisting. These findings are broadly applicable to a wide range of 2D perovskite and other layer-resolved oxides.

</details>


### [136] [Floquet-induced anisotropic magnetoresistance and anomalous Hall effect in 2D $d$-wave altermagnets with Rashba spin-orbit coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21281)
*Mohsen Yarmohammadi,Pieter M. Gunnink,Jairo Sinova,James K. Freericks*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Floquet engineering using monochromatic and bichromatic light can induce equilibrium-forbidden magnetizations and transport responses (anisotropic magnetoresistance, anomalous Hall effect) in 2D d-wave altermagnets, offering tunable, static-field-competitive control without requiring hundreds of tesla.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Altermagnets combine momentum-dependent spin splitting with zero net magnetization, offering spintronic potential, but static control is limited. The authors aim to unlock symmetry-forbidden transport responses via periodic driving, avoiding the impractical large static magnetic fields otherwise needed.

Method: The authors apply Floquet engineering to 2D d-wave altermagnets with out-of-plane Néel order and Rashba spin-orbit coupling, using monochromatic and bichromatic light, and analyze the resulting magnetizations and transport properties (AMR, AHE) across linear, circular, and mixed polarizations.

Result: Monochromatic driving induces purely out-of-plane magnetization, yielding longitudinal AMR and anomalous Hall effect. Bichromatic driving generates both in-plane and out-of-plane magnetizations and activates transverse AMR via the second harmonic. Effects are tunable via light parameters and persist across polarization types.

Conclusion: Multi-color Floquet engineering provides a versatile, tunable approach to control magnetization and symmetry-protected transport in altermagnets, achieving effects that would otherwise require static magnetic fields of hundreds of tesla.

Abstract: Altermagnets (AMs) combine momentum-dependent spin splitting with zero net magnetization, making them promising for spintronics. Periodic driving enables dynamic symmetry engineering beyond static, material-specific control. We show that Floquet engineering in 2D $d$-wave AMs with out-of-plane Néel order and Rashba spin-orbit coupling unlocks equilibrium-forbidden transport responses. Monochromatic driving produces purely out-of-plane magnetization, yielding longitudinal anisotropic magnetoresistance (AMR) and an anomalous Hall effect, whereas bichromatic driving generates both in-plane and out-of-plane magnetizations and additionally activates transverse AMR via the second harmonic of the secondary beam. Comparable static magnetic fields would require hundreds of tesla, avoided in Floquet driving. These effects persist across linear, circular, and mixed light polarizations and are tunable via light parameters. Our results establish multi-color Floquet engineering for controlling magnetization and symmetry-protected transport in AMs.

</details>


### [137] [Pyroelectric, electrocaloric and thermoelectric properties of core-shell HfxZr1-xO2 nanoparticles: theory and experiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22654)
*Anna N. Morozovska,Oleksandr S. Pylypchuk,Nicholas V. Morozovsky,Eugene A. Eliseev,Dean R. Evans*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper analyzes pyroelectric and electrocaloric properties of HfxZr1-xO2 nanoparticles using theoretical modeling and experimental measurements, showing qualitative agreement between theory and experiment, paving the way for CMOS-compatible applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore the pyroelectric and electrocaloric properties of nanosized HfxZr1-xO2 (hafnia-zirconia) nanoparticles, which are promising CMOS-compatible ferroelectric materials for advanced electronic memories and logic devices.

Method: Theoretical analysis using Landau-Ginzburg-Devonshire free energy functional with trilinear and biquadratic couplings of polar, nonpolar, and antipolar order parameters; experimental measurement of temperature-dependent electric charge in pressed powders of oxygen-deficient Hf0.5Zr0.5O2 nanoparticles (avg. 7 nm).

Result: Qualitative agreement between theoretical calculations (polarization and pyroelectric coefficient) and experimental data (accumulated charge and its temperature derivative) for densely packed spherical core-shell HfxZr1-xO2 nanoparticles.

Conclusion: These findings can enable the development of CMOS-compatible HfxZr1-xO2 nanoparticles for pyroelectric, electrocaloric, and thermoelectric applications.

Abstract: Nanosized hafnia-zirconia (HfxZr1-xO2) in the form of thin films, multilayers, and nanoparticles are indispensable CMOS-compatible ferroelectric materials for advanced electronic memories and logic devices. Using the Landau-Ginzburg-Devonshire free energy functional with trilinear and biquadratic couplings of polar, nonpolar, and antipolar order parameters, we analyze the pyroelectric and electrocaloric properties of an ensemble of spherical core-shell HfxZr1-xO2 nanoparticles. Complementary to theoretical calculations, we experimentally measure the temperature dependence of the electric charge accumulated in pressed powders consisting of oxygen-deficient Hf0.5Zr0.5O2 nanoparticles with an average size of 7 nm. The observed temperature-dependent behavior of the accumulated charge and its derivative with respect to temperature are in qualitative agreement with the dependences of polarization and pyroelectric coefficient calculated for the ensemble of densely packed spherical core-shell HfxZr1-xO2 nanoparticles. Thus, these results can open the way for creation of CMOS-compatible HfxZr1-xO2 nanoparticles for pyroelectric, electrocaloric, and thermoelectric applications.

</details>


### [138] [Machine learning metallic glass critical cooling rates through elemental and molecular simulation based featurization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21467)
*Lane E. Schultz,Benjamin Afflerbach,Paul M. Voyles,Dane Morgan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A machine learning model predicts critical cooling rates for metallic glasses using features from elemental properties and simulations (energies above convex hull, heat capacity changes, icosahedra-like Voronoi fraction), achieving R²=0.78 and MAE=0.76 log₁₀(K/s) across 34 alloys from 20 chemical systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a predictive model for critical cooling rates of metallic glasses using computationally derived features, enabling high-throughput screening across diverse alloy compositions without costly experiments.

Method: Features were derived from easy-to-compute elemental properties and complex simulations (ab initio, machine-learning potentials, empirical potential MD). The best model used one elemental feature (ideal entropy from stoichiometry) and three simulated features (energies above convex hull, heat capacity change, icosahedra-like Voronoi fraction). Models were assessed via leave-out chemical system cross-validation.

Result: The model achieved R²=0.78 and MAE=0.76 log₁₀(K/s) across 34 alloys from 20 chemical systems. SHAP analysis confirmed physically reasonable feature influence on predictions.

Conclusion: The methodology enables high-throughput prediction of critical cooling rates for diverse alloys and can be extended to other material property studies.

Abstract: We have developed a machine learning model for critical cooling rates for metallic glasses based on computational properties. We compare results for features derived from easy-to-compute functions of elemental properties to more complex physically motivated properties using ab initio, machine-learning potentials, and empirical potential molecular dynamics methods. The established approach enables property acquisition across a diverse range of alloys. Analysis of various features for 34 alloys from 20 chemical systems shows that the best model for critical cooling rates was learned from one elemental property-based feature and three simulated features. The elemental property-based feature is an ideal entropy value based on alloy stoichiometry. The simulated features were acquired from estimates of energies above the convex hull, changes in heat capacity, and the fraction of icosahedra-like Voronoi polyhedra. Models were assessed through a demanding cross validation test based on repeatedly leaving out full chemical systems as test sets and had an $R^2$ of 0.78 and a mean average error of 0.76 in units of $[log_{10}(K/s)]$. We demonstrate with Shapley additive explanation analysis that the most impactful features have physically reasonable influence on model predictions. The established methodology can be applied to other high-throughput studies of material properties of diverse compositions.

</details>


### [139] [High Throughput Analysis of Nanobeam Electron Diffraction Datasets using Unsupervised Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23201)
*Ian MacLaren,Ala Al-Afeef,Trevor Almeida,Rantej Bali,Shriyar Tariq,Emily Wackan,Luke Daly,Joshua F. Einsle*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Clustering algorithms applied to nanobeam electron diffraction datasets (processed via disk detection) can automatically decompose polycrystalline, single crystal, and amorphous/nanocrystalline components, enabling automated imaging and analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To automate the decomposition of nanobeam electron diffraction datasets and enable high-throughput imaging and analysis of polycrystalline, single crystal, and partially crystallized samples.

Method: Applying clustering algorithms to vector lists of diffraction peak positions (real and reciprocal space) obtained from disk detection on nanobeam electron diffraction datasets.

Result: Clustering effectively decomposes datasets for polycrystalline and single crystal (with precipitates) systems, and automatically separates amorphous or deeply nanocrystalline components, enabling composite imaging of both amorphous and crystalline phases.

Conclusion: These advances increase throughput in atomic structure analysis, simplify finding minor components, and serve as a preliminary step for detailed crystallographic or size/shape distribution analysis.

Abstract: If disk detection is applied to nanobeam electron diffraction datasets, then the results are effectively a list of vectors describing the position of every diffraction peak in real and reciprocal space. This is the natural territory for the application of clustering algorithms, and they are shown to be highly effective at decomposing such datasets and automating imaging and analysis. Examples are shown in both polycrystalline and single crystal (with precipitates) systems. Additionally, automated separation of amorphous or deeply nanocrystalline components is also found to be possible allowing composite images of both amorphous and crystalline components in partially crystallised samples to be easily and automatically generated. These advances promise to increase throughput in atomic structure analysis with nanobeam diffraction, and also make finding minor components much easier. They can also serve as a preliminary step towards more detailed crystallographic or crystal size/shape distribution analysis.

</details>


### [140] [Two-dimensional stealthy hyperuniform polycrystalline disk packings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23628)
*Carlo Vanoni,Paul J. Steinhardt,Salvatore Torquato*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper compares two types of two-dimensional polycrystalline disk packings: ultradense, stealthy, hyperuniform (SHU) packings generated via collective-coordinate optimization, and standard nonhyperuniform packings (LS) from rapid compression molecular dynamics. SHU packings show distinctive grain-size distributions, perfect transparency to electromagnetic waves at small wave vectors, and enhanced diffusion spreadability, making them promising for metamaterial applications via nanolithography or 3D printing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether hyperuniform polycrystalline structures can be designed and to characterize their structural, transport, and optical properties compared to realistic nonhyperuniform polycrystalline microstructures.

Method: Collective-coordinate optimization for generating SHU polycrystalline packings; modified Lubachevsky-Stillinger rapid compression algorithm (LS) for standard reference packings; comparison using two-point statistics, grain size, specific surface area, diffusion spreadability, and optical response via imaginary part of effective dynamic dielectric constant.

Result: SHU packings exhibit a distinctive grain-size distribution due to long-range inter-grain correlations absent in LS packings. They are perfectly transparent to electromagnetic waves at small wave vectors and offer enhanced diffusion spreadability.

Conclusion: Although SHU polycrystalline packings do not form spontaneously, they can be fabricated as metamaterials via nanolithography or 3D printing for applications leveraging their unique optical and transport properties.

Abstract: Polycrystals consist of grains of local crystalline order separated by grain boundaries. Their structure is not hyperuniform, even though perfect crystals are, because polycrystals consist of randomly sized and oriented grains that generate appreciable long-wavelength density fluctuations. In this paper, we use a collective-coordinate optimization procedure to generate two-dimensional polycrystalline packings composed of identical disks arranged in a pattern that is ultradense, stealthy, and hyperuniform (hereafter named SHU). We compare them with polycrystalline disk packings obtained via a modified Lubachevsky--Stillinger rapid compression algorithm (hereafter named LS), a molecular dynamics protocol that serves as a standard reference model describing realistic, nonhyperuniform polycrystalline microstructures. We carry out an extensive comparison of polycrystalline SHU and LS packings that includes differences in two-point statistics, grain size, specific surface area, diffusion spreadability, and optical response as quantified by the imaginary part of the effective dynamic dielectric constant. We find that the polycrystalline SHU packings exhibit a distinctive grain-size distribution, a consequence of long-range correlations between different grains that is absent in the nonhyperuniform case. Within the nonlocal strong-contrast expansion, we confirm that polycrystalline SHU packings made of dielectric material are perfectly transparent to electromagnetic waves at small wave vectors, in contrast to LS packings. Moreover, polycrystalline SHU packings offer enhanced diffusion spreadability. Although polycrystalline SHU packings are not expected to form spontaneously in nature, they may be created for applications as metamaterials via nanolithography or 3D printing that take advantage of their distinctive optical and transport properties.

</details>


### [141] [Peripheral Nitrogen Topology as a Defect-Chemical Switch for Electronic and Magnetic States in Graphene: A First-Principles Study of Pyridinic, Pyridazinic, Pyrrolic, and Pyrazolic Configurations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21480)
*Md. Moktadir Billah Tahmid,Indranil Rudra,Jahid Emon,Mohammad Jane Alam Khan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: First-principles calculations reveal that peripheral nitrogen topologies (pyridinic, pyridazinic, pyrrolic, pyrazolic) around graphene voids differentially modulate structural stability, electronic structure, and magnetic response, with pyrazolic configuration opening a narrow band gap and being spin-compensated, while others retain metallic character and finite magnetization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how specific peripheral nitrogen configurations around graphene voids influence electronic and magnetic properties, as the role of these topologies was previously insufficiently understood.

Method: Spin-polarized first-principles calculations (DFT) examining four heterocyclic-like peripheral N configurations (pyridinic, pyridazinic, pyrrolic, pyrazolic) around a central vacancy in graphene, using bond-length analysis, charge-density/difference/Bader analysis, spin-resolved band structures, and spin-density/SPDOS analysis.

Result: Pyridinic N offers the best structural-energetic balance. N atoms act as electron-accumulating centers. Pyridinic, pyridazinic, pyrrolic configurations retain metallic/near-metallic character; pyrazolic graphene opens a narrow band gap. Pyrazolic is spin-compensated (zero magnetization), while others have finite spin-polarized moments. Magnetism originates from N-modulated vacancy-edge states involving N 2p and neighboring C 2p orbitals.

Conclusion: Peripheral nitrogen topology acts as a deterministic defect-chemical switch, offering a metal-free route to pattern active spintronic and semiconducting domains into graphene lattice through controlled vacancy-edge nitrogen coordination.

Abstract: Defect and heteroatom engineering offer powerful routes for tuning the electronic and magnetic properties of graphene, yet the role of specific peripheral nitrogen topologies around graphene voids remains insufficiently understood. Here, spin-polarized first-principles calculations were performed to investigate how four heterocyclic -- like peripheral nitrogen configurations -- pyridinic, pyridazinic, pyrrolic, and pyrazolic modify the structural stability, charge redistribution, electronic structure, and magnetic response of graphene containing a central void. Among the four peripheral N configurations, the pyridinic N provides the most favorable structural-energetic balance among the investigated motifs. Bond-length analysis reveals that nitrogen topology strongly controls local lattice reconstruction. Charge-density, charge-density-difference, and Bader analyses demonstrate that the peripheral N atoms act as electron-accumulating centers and reshape the local electronic environment around the vacancy rim. Spin-resolved band structures show that pyridinic, pyridazinic, and pyrrolic configurations retain metallic or near-metallic defect-state character, whereas pyrazolic graphene opens a narrow band gap. Magnetic analysis further reveals that pyrazolic graphene is spin-compensated, with zero net magnetization, unlike the other systems, which possess finite spin-polarized moments. Spin-density and SPDOS analyses indicate that the magnetism originates from N-modulated vacancy-edge states involving both N 2p and neighboring C 2p orbitals. These findings establish peripheral nitrogen topology not merely as a structural defect descriptor, but as a deterministic defect-chemical switch, offering a metal-free route to pattern active spintronic and semiconducting domains directly into the graphene lattice through controlled vacancy-edge nitrogen coordination.

</details>


### [142] [Data analysis methods for powder x-ray diffraction intensity under laser-driven dynamic compression at Omega and NIF laser facilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23602)
*Marius Millot,Federica Coppari,Amy Lazicki,Jon H. Eggert*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper presents improved data analysis methods for powder X-ray diffraction (PXRD) under laser-driven dynamic compression, focusing on enhancing the fidelity of XRD intensity determination. It describes using in-situ references (collimating pinhole or un-compressed material layers), methods for comparing data across different x-ray sources, and accounting for thermal damping effects, illustrated with shock compression data on diamond near 1 TPa.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve the fidelity of XRD intensity determination in PXRD platforms at large laser facilities (Omega, NIF) by developing more robust data analysis methods that account for variability in x-ray sources and thermal effects.

Method: Development of data analysis methods using in-situ reference signals (collimating pinhole or un-compressed material layers), cross-source data comparison techniques, and thermal damping correction for hot, shock-compressed materials. Implementation in a data analysis package applied to diamond at ~1 TPa.

Result: Demonstrated improved XRD intensity determination fidelity through in-situ referencing and thermal damping correction, enabling more reliable comparison between shock-compressed and ambient reference materials across different x-ray sources.

Conclusion: The developed methods enhance the accuracy and comparability of PXRD data from dynamic compression experiments, facilitating more reliable material response studies under extreme conditions.

Abstract: Powder x-ray diffraction (PXRD) under laser-driven dynamic compression is a powerful tool to investigate material response to extreme pressure, temperature and strain rates. Robust PXRD platforms have been developed at kJ and MJ laser facilities worldwide including the Powder X-Ray Diffraction Image Plate (PXRDIP) at the Omega Laser Facility at the Laboratory for Laser Energetics (LLE) and the TARget Diffraction In Situ (TARDIS) at the National Ignition Facility (NIF). Here we present further developments of data analysis methods focused towards improving the fidelity of the PXRD intensity determination for these platforms. We illustrate these methods by discussing how they can be implemented in a data analysis package and applied to shock compression data on diamond near 1 TPa. We discuss using the XRD signal from the collimating pinhole or a layer of un-compressed material in the sample package as \textit{ in-situ} references for XRD intensity. We detail how to compare data collected with different x-ray sources and how to account for thermal damping of XRD signal when comparing XRD from a shock-compressed, hot material with the reference material at ambient.

</details>


### [143] [Fine-Tuning a Universal Machine-Learned Interatomic Potential for Oxygen Plasma Interactions with WS$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21632)
*Jaehong Kwon,David B. Graves*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper explores using Meta FAIR's UMA foundation model (pretrained on OC20) for MLIP in plasma-surface interactions on WS₂. Despite no fine-tuning needed for production-scale observables under O⁺/O₂⁺ bombardment, fine-tuning reduces energy MAE to 4.5×10⁻³ eV/atom and force MAE to 0.076 eV/Å.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Molecular dynamics of plasma-surface interactions requires an interatomic potential that is accurate, computationally efficient, and able to handle many elements and bonding types in reactive systems. A foundation model for MLIP may meet these demands.

Method: Starting from the pretrained uma-s-1p1 model (OC20 task), the authors apply an iterative fine-tuning loop with maximally diverse configuration sampling using SOAP and Farthest Point Sampling, DFT labeling at PBE+D3+U+spin level, and fine-tuning on energy, force, and stress labels.

Result: Without fine-tuning, the pretrained model reproduces production-scale observables (chemisorbed S and O coverage under 15eV O⁺ and O₂⁺ bombardment). Fine-tuning reduces energy MAE to 4.5×10⁻³ eV/atom and force MAE to 0.076 eV/Å.

Conclusion: The foundation model shows strong transferability to reactive plasma-surface interactions on WS₂, with fine-tuning further improving accuracy. Results were obtained without spin polarization and Hubbard U correction.

Abstract: Molecular dynamics simulation of plasma-surface interactions requires an interatomic potential that is simultaneously accurate, computationally efficient, and able to describe many elements and bonding types in reactive systems. In principle, a foundation model for machine-learned interatomic potential (MLIP) can meet these demands. We explore the use of the Universal Models for Atoms (UMA) model, developed by Meta FAIR, for the interactions of oxygen plasma species on a multilayer of WS$_2$, a promising 2D material. Starting from the pretrained uma-s-1p1 model under the Open Catalyst 2020 (OC20) task, we apply an iterative fine-tuning loop with maximally diverse configuration sampling using Smooth Overlap of Atomic Positions (SOAP) and Farthest Point Sampling (FPS); DFT labeling at the PBE+D3+$U$+spin level; and fine-tuning on energy, force, and stress labels. Even in the absence of fine-tuning, the pretrained model reproduces the production-scale observables of interest, namely, chemisorbed S and O coverage under 15eV O$^+$ and O$_2^+$ bombardment. These results were obtained without spin polarization and Hubbard $U$ correction. Nonetheless, fine-tuning reduces the energy and force mean absolute error (MAE) to $4.5\times10^{-3}$eV/atom and $0.076$eV/angstrom, respectively.

</details>


### [144] [Interaction between point defects and vertical inversion domain walls in wurtzite AlN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21733)
*Loris Naudin,Charles Paillard,Laurent Bellaiche,Lionel Calmels,Rémi Arras*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: First-principles calculations show that point defects in ferroelectric AlN compounds preferentially segregate at inversion domain walls, with distinct effects on domain wall motion and potential roles in leakage currents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the stability and role of point defects near inversion domain walls in alloyed AlN ferroelectrics, given their large coercive fields and unclear polarization-switching mechanisms.

Method: First-principles density functional theory calculations investigating isolated point defect stability near a vertical inversion domain wall.
